\title{\LARGE\bfseries Auditing Agent-Memory Benchmarks:\\[0.25em] Question-Blind Cues, Fair Nulls,\\and Replica Controls}
\author{Özgür Uğurlu}
\date{}
\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
Agent-memory benchmarks score systems that remember long conversation histories. We audit whether such scores reflect how a benchmark was built, scored, or run rather than memory. First, evidence can be located without the question on STALE and LoCoMo-Plus: on STALE, the curly apostrophe (U+2019) marks all 800 evidence sessions and none of 19,200 distractors, so a one-character rule finds both answer-bearing (``gold'') sessions in all 400 scenarios. On BEAM, by contrast, retrieval with another item's question is equivalent to chance once position, length, and turn count are accounted for (for our retriever and unit definitions). Second, the STALE cue is exploitable: a pointer built from it names the gold pair without the question and raises accuracy, as judged by a large language model (LLM), by 13--21 points over two-session retrieval pointers for four readers from four vendors (preregistered, on a test split whose outcomes had already been seen). This measures the value of exact retrieval, not a cue-specific effect, and those retrieval pointers trail an instruction that reveals what the rubric rewards. Third, native (unprompted) use of the cues is not shown. Removing typography and clock times together leaves one reader's accuracy within ±5 points in a user-only layout and, in the official layout, a retrieval-augmented generation pipeline's accuracy equivalent within ±3 points (+0.15 {[}−1.29, +1.60{]}, Holm-adjusted); a mem0 test is inconclusive (+4.30 {[}−0.21, +8.89{]}). Replacing STALE's distractors lowers retrieval recall by 12.6 points but adds harder negatives, and both end-to-end tests of replaced distractors are invalid because judge failures left one primary cell of each below the 98\% completeness bar. Fourth, item labels are unstable: two rebuilds of one pipeline disagree on 16\% of LLM-judged labels on average, while run-level accuracy varies by about 1 point, as averaging over 671 questions predicts; the author, as the sole and design-aware human grader, judged 10 of 15 sampled disagreements real. Finally, ForgetEval's public score can reward wiping memory. None of this shows that published scores are inflated. We recommend question-blind scans, fair-null baselines, and replica reruns, and release the memaudit toolkit.
\end{abstract}

\section{1. Introduction}\label{introduction}

Agent-memory benchmarks evaluate how well systems remember long conversation histories. Each score summarizes decisions about storage, retrieval, use, and forgetting. A single score can conceal evidence with a recognizable signature, instructions that reveal what the rubric rewards, or label differences that appear when an unchanged pipeline is rebuilt. We ask whether such scores can reflect how a benchmark was built or scored, or how one run of a pipeline happened to go, rather than memory, that is, whether they are valid measures of memory \citep{JacobsWallach2021Measurement, Raji2021Everything}.

These scores rank memory systems and guide their design, so a construction artifact can propagate into both. The closest audits vary what a reader sees for a given question \citep{Jiang2026Presentation, Si2026Render}, study shortcuts in a small streaming environment \citep{Huang2026RetentionSelection}, or show that memory size can leak evidence-sufficiency labels \citep{Mondal2026BeforeAnswering}. We apply established partial-input and contrast-set controls (§2) to the annotated evidence of published long-history benchmarks, namely STALE \citep{Chao2026STALE}, LoCoMo-Plus \citep{Li2026LoCoMoPlus}, LongMemEval \citep{Wu2025LongMemEval}, BEAM \citep{Tavakoli2025BEAM}, HaluMem \citep{Chen2025HaluMem}, PersonaMem-v2 \citep{Jiang2025PersonaMemV2}, LoCoMo \citep{Maharana2024LoCoMo}, and MemOps \citep{Hao2026MemOps}, against nulls that account for position, length, and turn count. We then ask whether readers, retrievers, and rebuilt memory pipelines are affected, and what the public score of ForgetEval, introduced by \citet{Yang2026ForgetEval}, rewards. The application of these controls, not the auditing principle, is new.

Four questions, each with its own estimand and intervention, organize the audit (Table 1). The first three form a chain: a construction cue can raise a score only if it can be detected without the question (Q1), if a system that used it would gain (Q2), and if ordinary systems do use it (Q3). Inflation, the last row of Table 1, ends that chain. It is not estimated here: the end-to-end tests designed to measure it are invalid (§6.3). Q4 asks whether the measurement itself, item labels and scoring rules, is stable enough to detect such effects.

Q1. Where do question-blind evidence cues exist, and what do fair nulls show? (§4) & Detectability: can an auditor identify evidence without the question? & Detectability AUROC (area under the ROC curve; macro within-pool) of a question-blind classifier (§3.2); chance-corrected excess (CE) of wrong-question retrieval over fair nulls (§3.3) & Strong on STALE and LoCoMo-Plus, moderate on LongMemEval-S, weak elsewhere; permutation evidence for the selected settings is post hoc. Fair-null results differ across benchmarks: BEAM is equivalent to chance for our retriever and unit definitions, and our HaluMem adaptation creates a position shortcut. \\
Q2. How far is a cue exploitable? (§5) & Exploitability: does deliberately supplying the evidence--distractor distinction help a reader or retriever? & Accuracy gain of the artifact pointer over retrieval pointers (§3.4); recall gain of a constructed style ranker (§5.2) & On a reused test split, the artifact pointer, which supplies exact two-session retrieval without the question, beats two-session retrieval pointers by 13--21 points for four readers; this is the value of exact retrieval, not a cue-specific effect. A constructed ranker adds 34.00 recall points on STALE but loses 4.68 points on LongMemEval-S. \\
Q3. Do readers and memory systems rely on the cues natively? (§6) & Native reliance: do readers or memory systems benefit from the cues in ordinary use, with no pointer or constructed ranker? & Recall change under replaced distractors (§3.3, §6.1); accuracy change when cues are removed, for one reader (§6.2) and for two pipelines in the official layout (§6.4); end-to-end accuracy change under replaced distractors (§6.3) & Replacing STALE's distractors lowers retrieval recall but also adds harder negatives. Removing clock and typography cues together leaves one plain reader's accuracy practically equal within the registered ±5 points (reused split, user-only layout; clock times are also legitimate content); a benefit of up to about 4 points is not excluded. In the official layout, it leaves a hybrid retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline's accuracy equivalent within ±3 points (+0.15 {[}−1.29, +1.60{]}), while the mem0 test is inconclusive (+4.30 {[}−0.21, +8.89{]}); both are conditional robustness evidence on a reused population. The end-to-end successor study's two primary tests are final INVALID. \\
Q4. How stable are scores, and what do scoring rules reward? (§7) & Stability: how much do replica labels and accuracy vary, and how sensitive are scores to scoring rules? & Replica label disagreement and oracle gain (§3.5); null-memory-policy scores (§7.4) & Pairs of replicas disagree on 16.25\% of labels from a large language model (LLM) judge on average, while run-level accuracy has an SD of 1.04 points, as averaging over 671 questions predicts; failed gates and the one-grader human check leave true correctness unresolved. Trivial memory policies (storing nothing, wiping memory) show that ForgetEval's public score can reward wiping memory. \\
--- & Inflation: is a published score higher than under an evaluation that preserves the intended task? & End-to-end accuracy with original against source-swap distractors (§6.3) & Not estimated. The end-to-end tests failed the completeness rule and yield no effect estimate (§6.3). Even if complete, source-swap distractors also add harder negatives, so the contrast could not separate inflation from harder interference. The pipeline de-cue study (§6.4) estimates a different contrast, RAW against DECUE, and does not measure inflation. \\
\end{longtable}

\endgroup

We make five contributions, each building on established controls (§2):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{(i) Q1, §4: a question-blind detectability audit and a fair-null atlas.} The audit covers nine settings from seven benchmark families (six if LoCoMo-Plus is merged with LoCoMo), and the wrong-question atlas covers seven benchmarks (adding MemOps; PersonaMem-v2 is not in the atlas). Cues are strong on STALE and LoCoMo-Plus, whereas on BEAM, under fair nulls, retrieval with another item's question is equivalent to chance for one retriever stack and the registered unit definitions (a registered result).
\item
  \textbf{(ii) Q2, §5: registered exploitability tests with four readers from four vendors.} On a reused test split, a pointer that names STALE's gold pair without using the question beats two-session retrieval pointers in LLM-judged accuracy for each of the four readers. The cue therefore yields question-blind oracle retrieval on STALE, and the gain measures the value of exact retrieval, not a cue-specific effect. A three-sentence instruction that reveals what STALE's rubric rewards closes about 53\% of the plain-to-oracle gap for the original reader, and 51 of its 58 net extra passes are false-premise rejections (§5.1), so reader comparisons need instruction controls (§8).
\item
  \textbf{(iii) Q3, §6: counterfactual-haystack, de-cue, and end-to-end tests of native reliance, none of which shows reliance.} Replacing STALE's distractors lowers retrieval recall but also adds harder negatives; removing typography and clock times together leaves one reader's accuracy practically equal in a user-only, non-official layout; and both end-to-end tests of replaced distractors, with RAG and a mem0 2.2.1 pipeline \citep{Mem0Library}, are final INVALID, with no inflation, null, or equivalence conclusion. In STALE's official layout, a registered pipeline de-cue study finds a hybrid RAG pipeline's accuracy equivalent within ±3 points with and without the two cues, while its mem0 test is inconclusive (§6.4).
\item
  \textbf{(iv) Q4, §7: replica controls with a one-grader human check, and null-memory policies for a public scoring rule.} On four LoCoMo conversations, one bespoke pipeline, modeled on Mem0 \citep{Chhikara2025Mem0} but not its code, was rebuilt ten times; pairs of these replicas disagree on 16\% of LLM-judged labels on average, while run-level accuracy has an SD of about 1 point, as averaging over 671 questions predicts. One design-aware grader, the author, estimates that about two-thirds of the sampled label disagreements are real correctness differences (10 of 15 sampled pairs; bootstrap interval {[}42.6, 91.2{]} for the weighted share, which does not exclude a minority); this check uses one grader, not a panel, and does not supply ground truth. On ForgetEval, a wipe-plus-replacement policy passes 750 of 1,000 template cases against 350 for storing nothing, so the public score can reward wiping memory.
\item
  \textbf{(v) The release statement: the open-source memaudit toolkit.} It implements the question-blind unit audit, wrong-question retrieval with fair-null baselines, null-memory policies for ForgetEval, and numeric replica replay. It runs no language model and is not a reproduction package: its default BM25 ranker does not reproduce the hybrid atlas, and its documented checks reproduce only selected numbers, such as the STALE and LoCoMo-Plus detectability scores.
\end{itemize}

\section{2. Related work}\label{related-work}

\textbf{Agent-memory benchmarks and systems.} Long-term conversational memory is evaluated on multi-session dialogues, such as MSC \citep{Xu2022Goldfish} and LoCoMo \citep{Maharana2024LoCoMo}, and on long histories that combine evidence sessions with separately sourced filler, such as LongMemEval \citep{Wu2025LongMemEval} and STALE \citep{Chao2026STALE}. These benchmarks rank memory systems such as MemGPT \citep{Packer2023MemGPT}, MemoryBank \citep{Zhong2024MemoryBank}, Mem0 \citep{Chhikara2025Mem0}, Zep \citep{Rasmussen2025Zep}, and A-MEM \citep{Xu2025AMem}; \citet{Zhang2024MemorySurvey} survey the area. We audit benchmark families for which a question-blind adapter was tractable, not a representative sample (§9).

\textbf{Controls we build on.} Question-blind auditing is a partial-input baseline that follows hypothesis-only natural-language inference \citep{Gururangan2018AnnotationArtifacts, Poliak2018HypothesisOnly}, question-only and passage-only reading comprehension \citep{KaushikLipton2018ReadingComprehension}, and work on shortcut learning \citep{Geirhos2020ShortcutLearning}. A failing partial-input baseline does not show that a dataset is free of artifacts \citep{Feng2019Misleading}, single features whose label distribution departs from chance can be tested directly \citep{Gardner2021Competency}, and single cues have inflated comprehension and inference scores before \citep{NivenKao2019Probing, McCoy2019Right}. Our counterfactual haystacks have precedents in adversarial filtering \citep{Zellers2018SWAG, LeBras2020AFLite}, distractors \citep{JiangBansal2019Shortcuts}, contrast sets that change the gold label \citep{Gardner2020ContrastSets}, and paired evidence and hard-negative interventions \citep{Du2026PairID, Zhang2026CausalNeg}; our evaluation-time replacements also change how hard the negatives are. Work on judge reliability and agreement with humans \citep{Zheng2023MTBench, Thakur2024JudgingJudges, Bavaresco2025HumanJudges}, order effects \citep{Wang2024NotFair}, judge panels \citep{Verga2024PoLL}, self-preference \citep{Panickssery2024SelfPreference, Pombal2026SelfPreference}, family preference \citep{Awuni2026WhoJudges}, and long-context calibration \citep{Zhao2026LRBench} informs our judge gates. Studies of benchmark and training-run variance \citep{Bouthillier2021Variance, Madaan2024Variance}, error bars for clustered answers \citep{Miller2024ErrorBars}, and routing oracles under stochastic decoding \citep{Chen2026RoutingOracle} inform our replica analysis.

\textbf{Long-context readers.} Readers use evidence in long contexts unevenly by position \citep{Liu2024LostMiddle}, are distracted by irrelevant context \citep{Shi2023Distracted}, and are sensitive to prompt format \citep{Sclar2024Spurious}; needle-in-a-haystack tests overstate effective context use \citep{Hsieh2024RULER}, especially when the needle repeats the question's wording \citep{Modarressi2025NoLiMa}. These effects motivate our pointer controls, position priors, and layout caveats.

\textbf{Construction shortcuts and source preference.} Concurrent with our studies, Before Answering \citep{Mondal2026BeforeAnswering} shows that memory size can leak evidence-sufficiency labels: a classifier that only counts paragraphs, without the question, detects unsafe memory with an AUROC of .979 on MuSiQue, and the proposed estimator, MemSafe, is query-conditioned. We apply the same question-blind logic to a different object, the annotated evidence units of published long-history agent-memory benchmarks, and add benchmark-specific fair nulls and downstream reader, retrieval, and replica controls. \citet{Huang2026RetentionSelection} studies distractor and type shortcuts under uninformative queries and unequal history access in a small streaming environment; we instead test the annotated evidence of existing benchmarks. Preferences for generated documents \citep{Dai2024Cocktail, Dai2024SourceBias}, which have been linked to training data \citep{Huang2026DataNotModel, XionNejdl2026TrainingBias}, motivate our construction controls; we establish no mechanism for them here.

\textbf{Presentation, reconstruction, and visibility.} Several concurrent studies use query-conditioned controls. Presentation, Not Mechanism \citep{Jiang2026Presentation} separates layout from deprecation with render-matched controls, and RENDER \citep{Si2026Render} varies the reader-facing rendering of a fixed conversation. Neither evaluates STALE: the first uses issue histories, Wikipedia, and temporal streams, and the second LongMemEval and HotpotQA. Both find that presentation alone can move scores, which fits our caveat that the de-cue result holds for one layout only (§6.2). Madeleine \citep{Shi2026Madeleine} separates query-encoder instructions from learned association and checks human-written triggers. RealCompanion \citep{Behnam2026RealCompanion} uses no-context, random, recency, and broken-oracle controls with a probe-aware detector. RoutePrism \citep{Xu2026RoutePrism} compares ingestion order with repeated-answer variation within a route, and Causal Memory Policy \citep{BehnamWang2026CausalMemory} intervenes on retrieval exposure. Our reader ladders differ in that the artifact pointer is derived from a construction cue without using the question and is matched to retrieval pointers in session count and note length, and our de-cue test edits STALE's own rendering against a registered ±5-point margin. An engineering blog post distinguishes rebuilding from rescoring \citep{Jones2026Reproducibility}, and a company blog post reports about 95.6\% accuracy for its own memory pipeline on random 15\% samples of STALE's direct- and indirect-update questions \citep{Liu2026StashMemory}; our plain readers score 27.7--71.7\% in the user-only full-history layout, a different setting. Item-level disagreement under stable aggregate accuracy is known as prediction churn or underspecification \citep{MilaniFard2016Churn, DAmour2022Underspecification} and, in LLM evaluation, as run-to-run nondeterminism \citep{Song2025Greedy}; our oracle union over \(k\) replicas is the pass@\(k\) coverage estimator \citep{Chen2021PassAtK, Brown2024Monkeys} applied to judged labels. What the replica study adds is a same-input rebuild design for one memory pipeline, whose label disagreement is expressed as an oracle gain and checked by a human grader (Appendix C.1).

\textbf{Budgets and complete support.} Context budgets affect reranking \citep{SharmaLall2026Selection} and complete-support evaluation \citep{Wang2026ExecutionProvenance}. The released per-question labels of \citet{SharmaLall2026Selection} also supply the reference gain of §3.5. Our reader ladders (§3.4) name sessions within the STALE history that the reader already sees. Context Language Models let agents edit budgeted context files \citep{Shao2026ContextLanguageModels}, a neighboring setting we do not evaluate.

\textbf{Registration and test reuse.} Our studies follow preregistration practice \citep{Nosek2018Prereg, vanMiltenburg2021Prereg}. Retesting on a split whose outcomes are known is adaptive reuse of a holdout, which weakens confirmatory inference \citep{Dwork2015Holdout, Recht2019ImageNet}; we call such follow-ups conditional robustness checks. Low statistical power is a known problem in NLP evaluation \citep{Card2020Power}, so we report registered design power where it exists (§9). Benchmark-quality assessments \citep{BowmanDahl2021Fix, Reuel2024BetterBench} and work on leakage \citep{KapoorNarayanan2023Leakage} motivate the recommendations of §8.

\section{3. Audit design}\label{audit-design}

We answer Q1--Q4 with the following instruments. A question-blind classifier (§3.2) and wrong-question retrieval against fair nulls (§3.3) test Q1: if gold is found without its own question more often than position, length, and turn count explain, gold has a question-independent advantage. Reader ladders (§3.4) test Q2 by giving a reader the sessions that this advantage identifies. Counterfactual haystacks (§3.3), the de-cue and end-to-end tests (§6.2--§6.3), and the pipeline de-cue study (§6.4) test Q3 by removing or replacing the cue. Replica rebuilds and judge gates (§3.5), together with null-memory policies (§7.4), test Q4.

\subsection{3.1. Studies, protocol, and conventions}\label{studies-protocol-and-conventions}

Before testing, each registered study had a reviewed preregistration, a hash-locked protocol and code, and development gates. The post hoc statistics rounds had none of these, and the one-grader human check had a hash-pinned plan but no development gate; its reinstated human route was not registered (Appendix J.6). With each result we disclose post-lock changes and earlier exposure to outcomes; a hash lock fixes a protocol's content, not which outcomes had already been seen (Appendix I). Table 2 maps the studies, whose populations differ; each judge validation covers only the packet it examined. We call every row of Table 2 a study; its name (audit, atlas, ladder, run, test, follow-up, regrade, or check) describes its design, not a different kind of object.

\textbf{Conventions.} \emph{Registered} means fixed in a study's preregistration before its test. \emph{Post hoc} marks an analysis chosen after outcomes were seen; none changes a registered verdict. \emph{Descriptive} and \emph{exploratory} results support no registered verdict. Capitals mark registered verdict and status labels (POSITIVE, SMALL, EQUIVALENT, REVERSED, BELOW-SESOI, INCONCLUSIVE, PARTIAL, INVALID, FAILED PIPELINE, UNRESOLVED, COMPLETE, INCOMPLETE, UNPERFORMED, EXPLORATORY, PASS, ABOVE, and NO-COMMON-SUPPORT) and the arm names RAW, DECUE, NEW, and OLD; reader tiers are lowercase and quoted (``supported'', ``practically equal'', ``reversed''). \emph{Final} marks a verdict or status that no remaining registered repair or retry can change. A study's \emph{binding judge} is the judge whose labels decide its registered verdicts. A \emph{reused test split} is a test split whose outcomes had already been seen (registered texts quoted in the appendix call it ``consumed''); a follow-up on it tests conditional robustness on that split, not fresh confirmation.

\textbf{Terms.} A \emph{scenario} is one STALE test case: a 50-session history with three probe questions, or \emph{probes} (§3.2). A \emph{unit} is what a classifier or retriever scores: a session, round, or turn, depending on the benchmark. A \emph{pool} is the candidate set of units within which a detectability AUROC is computed (for STALE, the sessions of one scenario), and a \emph{haystack} is the set of units searched for one item. A \emph{read} is one reader answer to one probe in one arm, and a \emph{judge call} is one judge request that scores the three probes of one scenario and arm. A \emph{gate} is a registered pass-or-fail check, such as judge validation, that must pass before a verdict is drawn; a \emph{packet} is the sample of answers labeled for a judge-validation gate. A \emph{reader ladder} compares one reader's accuracy across arms (§3.4). A \emph{test family} is a set of contrasts adjusted together for multiple testing; \emph{model family} and \emph{vendor} refer to models.

Answer-correctness labels come from LLM judges, except in the one-grader human check. \emph{LLM adjudication} means that a further language model grades a sample of answers; its \emph{adjudicator labels} serve as LLM reference labels, and agreement with them is not human validation. Square brackets give 95\% intervals unless the text names another kind, such as a Holm-adjusted interval \citep{Holm1979} (atlas and counterfactual-haystack tests) or a Bonferroni per-contrast interval (ladders), whose level is given in percent.

\textbf{Verdict tiers.} Tiers differ by study. The three-reader ladder, the four-vendor ladder, the Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run, and the de-cue test classify an accuracy difference \(D\) with the reader tiers of Table 2B. The ladders share one rule set with Holm adjustment and an open equivalence band, while the de-cue test, a single contrast, uses its 95\% interval and a closed band. The atlas and counterfactual-haystack tiers are given in §3.3, and those of the pipeline de-cue study in §6.4 and Appendix L.1.

\textbf{Recomputations.} For seven studies, two separate recomputations from the saved outputs checked the registered numbers (Appendix I.4): the three-reader ladder, the four-vendor ladder, the Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run, the de-cue test, the wrong-question atlas, the second-judge study, and the extractor follow-up. The counterfactual-haystack study and the end-to-end successor study each received two further recomputations, and we adopted their documentary corrections. These are computational checks, not replication by another laboratory or label validation, and failed gates and missing data keep their status.

\subsection{3.2. Audited benchmarks and question-blind detectability}\label{audited-benchmarks-and-question-blind-detectability}

The evidence audit asks whether a classifier that never sees the question can tell evidence units from distractors. It covers nine selected settings: STALE \citep{Chao2026STALE}, LoCoMo-Plus \citep{Li2026LoCoMoPlus}, LongMemEval-S \citep{Wu2025LongMemEval}, three BEAM scales \citep{Tavakoli2025BEAM}, HaluMem-Medium \citep{Chen2025HaluMem}, a cached PersonaMem-v2 subset \citep{Jiang2025PersonaMemV2}, and LoCoMo \citep{Maharana2024LoCoMo}. These form seven benchmark families: the three BEAM scales count once, and the separately published LoCoMo-Plus counts apart from LoCoMo (six families if merged). The BEAM rows label evidence only for contradiction-resolution and knowledge-update (CR/\allowbreak{}KU) tasks. The wrong-question atlas (§3.3) covers seven benchmarks: these minus PersonaMem-v2, plus MemOps \citep{Hao2026MemOps}.

Table 3 describes how each benchmark family produced its evidence and its distractors or filler, and which cue the audit found. Three settings combine a benchmark-authored evidence pipeline with distractors or filler from another source: STALE (sessions sampled from LongMemEval), LoCoMo-Plus (cue dialogues inserted into LoCoMo conversations), and LongMemEval-S (public chat as part of its filler). These are the documented different pipelines marked in Figure 1. The pattern is correlational, not a necessary condition: BEAM's instruction-following evidence, whose labels this audit does not use, comes from the same generator as its filler and is still strongly detectable (detectability AUROC .936--.971).

\endgroup

For each setting, an adapter defines units, pools, evidence labels, and groups. The gradient-boosted classifier used 31 style features of each unit's text and role, and it excluded questions, evidence ids, and timestamps. It was evaluated with five-fold grouped cross-validation and scored by detectability AUROC (macro within-pool; Figure 1 and Appendix A.1). Appendix K.2 specifies the learner, its settings, the cross-validation groups and seed, and the 31 features. The released toolkit implements this audit; its documented checks reproduce the STALE and LoCoMo-Plus scores of Figure 1 from the public benchmark files but do not cover the other seven settings (release statement). The registration treats a detectability AUROC below .55 as negligible; this is an interpretation threshold, not a calibrated significance level, and the only permutation null is post hoc (§4.1).

A test-duplicate exclusion removed held-out units whose hash of raw role and text also occurs in training. On STALE it excluded 10,540 of 20,000 rows, so filtered and full-pool scores describe different populations.

\textbf{The STALE task.} STALE has 400 scenarios, 200 of each of two types, and each scenario has a 50-session history between a user and an assistant \citep{Chao2026STALE}. Two generated sessions form the \emph{gold pair}. The older session (OLD), always among the first 15, supports a fact about the user; the newer session (NEW), always among sessions 31--40, implies a change that invalidates the fact without saying so. In \emph{co-referential} scenarios both sessions concern the same attribute; in \emph{propagated} scenarios NEW changes a related attribute, and the old fact fails as a consequence. The other 48 sessions are distractors sampled from LongMemEval and filtered so that none can update the fact. Each scenario has three probes. \emph{State Resolution} (SR) asks directly about the old fact. \emph{Premise Resistance} (PR) asks a question that presupposes the outdated fact, a false premise: the official rubric passes an answer only if it detects the premise or acknowledges the new state, so a pass on PR is a false-premise rejection. \emph{Implicit Policy Adaptation} (IPA) asks for help whose appropriate answer depends on the updated fact, without mentioning either session. An LLM judge grades each answer against the scenario's outdated and updated states. Our reader ladders and the de-cue test use a fixed test split of 100 scenarios (50 of each type), the next 100 after 40 development scenarios in a hash order of scenario ids (Appendix K.1).

\subsection{3.3. Counterfactual haystacks and wrong-question retrieval}\label{counterfactual-haystacks-and-wrong-question-retrieval}

The counterfactual-haystack study changed the distractors while holding questions, evidence, positions, and session counts fixed. On STALE, \emph{source-swap distractors} are evidence-type sessions from other scenarios; on LongMemEval-S, they are simulated sessions that are never evidence. Each test compares the original haystack with its counterfactual. Differences are original minus counterfactual, in points, so positive values mean lower recall or accuracy under the counterfactual; either swap can also change difficulty. The primary test family has three tests of hybrid all-gold@5 (every gold unit in the top five): the STALE source-swap retrieval test, the STALE style-rewrite test, and the LongMemEval-S source-swap test. The style-rewrite test keeps STALE's distractors but applies \emph{style rewrites} to them. A test is POSITIVE if its Holm-adjusted lower bound is above 0 and its estimate is at least +5 points. A counterfactual is PARTIAL, which makes its tests exploratory, if its manipulation check fails: the evidence-audit classifier still separates evidence from the new distractors (detectability AUROC above .60, or a 95\% upper bound above .65). For style rewrites, failed content checks also make it PARTIAL (Appendix D.1). A secondary family measures \emph{same-type wrong-question retrieval}: retrieval when the retriever receives another item's question (a \emph{wrong question}), here ten hash-chosen questions of the same probe dimension (on LongMemEval-S, the same question type).

The wrong-question atlas extends wrong-question retrieval to other benchmarks and compares it with fair nulls: baselines that account for advantages a unit has under any query, such as its turn count, length, or position. Query-independent advantages of this kind, such as length effects and document priors, are long recognized in retrieval \citep{Singhal1996Pivoted, Kraaij2002Priors, Craswell2005QueryIndependent}; our nulls adapt them to wrong-question hits on memory units. For each item, a fixed hybrid retriever ranks the item's units on user-side text once for each of ten wrong questions drawn from a different group (\emph{atlas wrong-question retrieval}). A unit's score is the maximum over its turns, and the outcome is whether any gold unit reaches the top five (any-gold@5). Because max-pooling favors units with more turns under any query, the primary null is turn-weighted chance: Plackett--Luce sampling \citep{Luce1959, Plackett1975} with each unit weighted by its nonempty turns. The effect is the \emph{chance-corrected excess} (CE), the item mean of \((h_i - c_i)/(1 - c_i)\), where \(h_i\) is item \(i\)'s wrong-question hit rate and \(c_i\) its null rate. A CE above 0 means that wrong questions retrieve gold more often than turn count, length, and position explain. Because the two procedures draw wrong questions differently, STALE's same-type rate (42.08\%; §6.1) differs from its atlas rate (41.40\%; Table 4).

A secondary estimand replaces the null with matched placebos: 20 placebo gold sets drawn from non-gold units in the same length, turn-count, and position cells. It needs common support on at least 80\% of items. Rows of the confirmatory and placebo families receive tiers at their Holm-adjusted intervals: POSITIVE if the lower bound is above 0 and CE is at least .05; REVERSED if the upper bound is below 0; and EQUIVALENT if the interval lies strictly inside ±.03 CE, the interval-inclusion form of the two one-sided tests (TOST) procedure \citep{Schuirmann1987TOST, Lakens2017Equivalence}. Appendix E.1 gives the remaining tiers. A benchmark shows a construction cue in the atlas's sense only if its placebo estimand is POSITIVE. The other rows carry descriptive 95\% intervals and no tier; among them, the STALE and LongMemEval-S reference rows are displayed but not tested, beside a legacy run that reproduces the counterfactual-haystack study's rates. A \emph{cue-dominance check} labels a row cue-dominated if any registered question-blind feature separates gold with a single-feature AUROC (within-haystack) of at least .90. These features are length, turns, position, and non-ASCII or typographic marks; in a cue-dominated row only the placebo estimand may be interpreted.

The confirmatory family is HaluMem-Medium and BEAM. MemOps-round \citep{Hao2026MemOps} joins it only if it passes an \emph{admission rule}: each of three features (user characters, nonempty turn count, and position) must have a single-feature AUROC (within-haystack) below .90. The run was frozen before any retrieval.

\subsection{3.4. Reader ladders}\label{reader-ladders}

The reader ladders compare a reader's accuracy across arms that differ in the note placed before the question. Every arm shares the official STALE system prompt and the same user-only rendering of the 50-session history, in the studies' non-official user-message layout. The \emph{plain} arm adds no note, and the \emph{generic instruction} adds a three-sentence instruction aligned with the rubric: facts can change, rely on the latest, and check the question's premise (verbatim in Appendix K.5).

Retrieval pointers name the sessions that a retriever returns for the question: five in \emph{hybrid-5} and \emph{dense-5} and, in the four-vendor ladder only, two in \emph{hybrid-2} and \emph{dense-2}. The hybrid retriever fuses BM25 \citep{RobertsonZaragoza2009BM25} with a configured bge-small-en-v1.5 dense encoder \citep{Xiao2023CPack} by reciprocal rank fusion (RRF) \citep{Cormack2009RRF}; Appendix K.3 gives the retrieval settings. The \emph{artifact pointer} (the gold-pair pointer of Tables 1 and 2) names the sessions that contain the character U+2019, which on STALE are exactly the two gold sessions in 100 of 100 test scenarios; it therefore supplies exact two-session retrieval without using the question. The shared rendering normalizes STALE's typography and keeps dates but not clock times in session headers (Appendix K.5 gives the normalizer's mapping). The history the reader sees therefore lacks the U+2019 marks from which the pointer is built; §6.2 restores both cues. The \emph{oracle note} is privileged: it labels the earlier statement outdated and carries the gold text, not only its location.

All pointer arms share one session-list and staleness template, distinct from the generic instruction; only the listed sessions change. Three control arms of the three-reader ladder use the same template. \emph{NEW only} names only the newer gold session. \emph{OLD + wrong later} names the older gold session and the non-gold session after it that lies closest to the newer gold session. \emph{All wrong} names that non-gold session and the non-gold session closest to the older gold session, so it names no gold session. The four-vendor ladder adds a secondary \emph{gold-pair-plus-three} arm, which names the gold pair and three hash-chosen non-gold sessions; the end-to-end successor study gives its readers such a five-session context and calls it ``Oracle-5''. The appendix keeps the registered ids, for example \nolinkurl{ptr_regex} for the artifact pointer, \nolinkurl{ptr_hyb5} for hybrid-5, and \nolinkurl{ptr_art5} for gold-pair-plus-three.

The pointer notes are 35, 46, and 79 tokens long for one, two, and five sessions. The original and three-reader ladders compare the two-session artifact pointer with five-session retrieval pointers, so in those ladders four factors vary together: the number of named sessions, the note's token budget, which sessions are named, and how well the template's staleness instruction fits them. The four-vendor ladder's two-session pointers share the artifact pointer's 46-token note, matching its count and note length but not the number of returned evidence tokens or complete support.

The original ladder used GLM-5.3-Flash as reader and binding judge. The judge was selected after development validation, and intervals were clustered by scenario. The three-reader ladder estimated scenario-mean paired effects with registered permutation tests; its analysis code changed after the lock (Appendices B.2 and I.2). The four-vendor ladder kept that estimator and those tiers, with Holm adjustment over 16 primary contrasts (Table 2B).

The four-vendor ladder used four readers from four vendors: Z.AI's GLM-5.3-Flash \citep{ZaiGLM53FlashDocs}, Google DeepMind's Gemini 3.8 Flash \citep{Google2026Gemini38Flash}, OpenAI's GPT-6.1 Sol \citep{OpenAIGPT61SolDocs}, and Muse Spark 1.3, a Meta Superintelligence Labs model served through the Meta Model API \citep{Meta2026MuseSpark13}. Muse Spark ran in Meta's standard tier, whose prompts and completions are excluded from model training; the registration refused the discounted ``contributor'' variants. Each reader ran at temperature 1.0 and at its vendor's ``high'' reasoning-effort setting; these vendor labels do not denote comparable reasoning levels. The binding judge was GPT-6.1 Sol at medium reasoning effort, and it also judged its own vendor's reader. Its validation gate used a fresh 72-item packet, labeled after all reads by a fresh Claude Opus 5.5 instance \citep{AnthropicOpus55Announcement} that saw no arm, reader, or outcome. The Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run, a separate registration, reran Gemini 3.8 Flash alone (§5.1). The API ids are \nolinkurl{glm-53-flash} (GLM-5.3-Flash), \nolinkurl{gemini-3.8-flash} (Gemini 3.8 Flash), \nolinkurl{gemini-3.5-flash-lite} (Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite), \nolinkurl{gpt-6.1-sol} (GPT-6.1 Sol), \nolinkurl{muse-spark-1.3} (Muse Spark 1.3), \nolinkurl{claude-sonnet-5-5} (Claude Sonnet 5.5), and \nolinkurl{claude-opus-5-5} (Claude Opus 5.5); Appendix K.7 gives every model's settings.

\subsection{3.5. Replica oracle and judge validation}\label{replica-oracle-and-judge-validation}

A \emph{replica} is one rebuild of the memory store from the same inputs; the replica study built ten per conversation, and the variance component due to rebuilding is the store (extraction) variance. We express the disagreement between replicas as the gain of a per-question oracle, so that rebuild noise can be set beside reported improvements. For aligned binary labels from two replicas with accuracies \(a\) and \(b\), let \(d\) be their \emph{label disagreement}, the percentage of questions on which their labels differ; on such a question the two replicas form a \emph{disagreeing pair} (a \emph{flip}). The per-question oracle union then gains \(G_{\mathrm{mean}} = d/2\) points over the pair mean and \(G_{\mathrm{better}} = d/2 - \lvert a - b \rvert/2\) points over the better replica. These identities hold whenever both replicas are scored on the same questions with the same weights; they do not require independent replicas. In the replica study, the uncertainty estimate combined question resampling with a store jackknife; it is conditional on the observed conversations, and the 45 replica pairs share replicas.

For scale, the per-question union of two memory systems of \citet{SharmaLall2026Selection}, one that selects raw turns and one that retrieves extracted facts, beats the better of them by 8.9 points on 778 LoCoMo questions. We recomputed this gain from their released labels; their paper reports a noninferiority comparison of the two systems (77.0\% against 77.5\%), not this gain. The replica study registered half of it, 4.45 points, as a threshold, and the extractor follow-up tested against the same threshold (§7.2). We use the 8.9 points only as a reference scale, not as a same-population comparator or an estimate of their noise.

The original replica results used LLM labels, so the follow-ups carried a binding judge-validation gate. The gate uses a registered packet of at least 60 labeled or determinate items. On it, the judge must reach agreement of at least .80 and Cohen's \(\kappa\) \citep{Cohen1960Kappa} of at least .70 against the adjudicator labels. The replica gates use \emph{design-weighted} agreement and \(\kappa\), which reweight the packet by its sampling weights to its frame, the full answer set it was drawn from; the gates of the three-reader ladder, the four-vendor ladder, and the Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run are unweighted (Appendix I.3). A failed gate makes the dependent cells UNRESOLVED, whatever their statistics say. Near the thresholds, the gate is a noisy classifier of judge quality: in a post hoc simulation that takes the observed agreement tables as true, a judge like the extractor follow-up's passes a gate-sized packet in about 43--49\% of draws (Appendix I.3). A failed gate therefore withholds a verdict; it does not show that the judge is unreliable (§7.3).

\section{4. Evidence can be located without the question in some settings}\label{evidence-can-be-located-without-the-question-in-some-settings}

We first ask where evidence can be located without the question (§4.1) and whether wrong questions retrieve gold above fair-null baselines (§4.2).

\subsection{4.1. Style features separate evidence on STALE and LoCoMo-Plus, less clearly elsewhere}\label{style-features-separate-evidence-on-stale-and-locomo-plus-less-clearly-elsewhere}

On STALE, a single character locates the evidence exactly. The character U+2019 appears in all 800 evidence sessions and in none of the 19,200 distractors. A rule based on this character recovers both annotated sessions in 400 of 400 scenarios, or in 399 when it scans only user turns. On LoCoMo-Plus, a simple marker also separates the evidence: none of the 758 cue turns (LoCoMo-Plus's evidence turns) contains an exclamation mark, against 72.0\% of host turns. These are corpus properties; they do not show that the markers were planted deliberately.

The evidence audit applied the question-blind style classifier of §3.2 to nine selected settings, each with its own adapter (Figure 1). Detectability AUROC (macro within-pool) is high on STALE and LoCoMo-Plus (1.000 and .995), moderate on LongMemEval-S, and weaker elsewhere; LoCoMo's interval includes .5. In the audit's registered classes (below .55 negligible, .55--.65 weak, .65--.90 strong, at least .90 saturated), STALE and LoCoMo-Plus are saturated, LongMemEval-S is strong, and the three BEAM scales, HaluMem-Medium, and PersonaMem-v2 are weak. LoCoMo, at .518, is the only setting below the registered .55 threshold. LongMemEval-S scores .783 on 379 duplicate-filtered pools and .752 on all 470 pools in an earlier fit. Earlier LongMemEval-S fits conditioned on filler source gave .947 against public-chat filler (ShareGPT and UltraChat \citep{Ding2023UltraChat}) and .532 against simulated filler.

Separating evidence from distractors is not the same as recovering it. On LoCoMo-Plus, the classifier ranks an evidence turn first in only 52.4\% of scored pools and places at least one in its top five in 91.9\%. The question-driven retriever of the original ladder, applied to its 161 LoCoMo-Plus test items, places at least one evidence turn in its top five for only six of them.

In post hoc permutation tests, all nine classifiers beat their nulls after Holm correction at .05 (Appendix H.1). Each classifier was refitted on 1,000 within-pool label permutations; eight settings reach the resolution floor of p = 1/\allowbreak{}1,001 = .000999, which is not a confidence bound. LoCoMo, at a detectability AUROC of .518, is barely above chance; it is the only setting in which some permuted refits reach the observed AUROC (10 of 1,000, p = .011). It passes only as the last Holm step and would fail a Bonferroni cut (.0056). Holm does not correct for our outcome-informed choice of settings and classifier, and a small p does not identify the mechanism.

In post hoc checks, simpler features also separate STALE's evidence: the curly-apostrophe count alone gives a single-feature AUROC of 1.000, and length alone gives .947. A refit without typography and punctuation features gives a detectability AUROC of about .999, but its predictions were not saved and no separate refit repeated it. The pattern fits redundant marking, yet the remaining features can still carry punctuation effects, so the refit is not a causal test of typography.

Weak style scores do not mean that evidence cannot be located \citep[cf.][]{Feng2019Misleading}. In a post hoc check, position alone (which the style classifier omits) scores a higher AUROC than the classifier on BEAM and HaluMem; on HaluMem it reaches .838 against the classifier's .611. Detectability by itself shows neither exploitation nor score inflation.

\centering

\caption*{\textbf{Figure 1. Question-blind evidence detectability by setting.} Question-blind style features separate evidence almost perfectly on STALE and LoCoMo-Plus, moderately on LongMemEval-S, and weakly elsewhere. Points give the detectability AUROC (macro within-pool) of supervised 31-feature unit-style classifiers, scored on held-out groups after the test-duplicate exclusion, in nine selected settings from seven benchmark families (six if LoCoMo-Plus is merged with LoCoMo). Bars are saved 95\% group-bootstrap intervals (2,000 draws); they condition on the saved predictions and exclude fitted-model uncertainty. The dashed line marks chance (.5). No permutation band is drawn; §4.1 reports the post hoc permutation nulls. Colors mark the three settings with documented different evidence and filler pipelines (Table 3); they describe source construction and do not establish its causal effect. CR/\allowbreak{}KU: contradiction-resolution and knowledge-update evidence labels. The pool column gives scored and full pools: LongMemEval-S uses 379 of 470 pools, and PersonaMem-v2 a cached eight-pool subset. For BEAM 1M the full count is the preregistered loaded population (31 pools); the audit report's pre-exclusion count is 35 (Appendix Table A1).}

\subsection{4.2. Under fair nulls, wrong-question retrieval is at chance on BEAM; STALE and our HaluMem adaptation are cue-dominated}\label{under-fair-nulls-wrong-question-retrieval-is-at-chance-on-beam-stale-and-our-halumem-adaptation-are-cue-dominated}

Given another item's question, a retriever finds BEAM's gold evidence at a rate equivalent to chance under fair nulls, while STALE and our HaluMem adaptation are cue-dominated (Figure 2, Table 4). These results come from atlas wrong-question retrieval (§3.3), in which ten wrong questions from a different group rank each item's units, and they hold for one retriever stack and the registered unit definitions. The atlas covers seven benchmarks: those of §4.1 except PersonaMem-v2, plus MemOps. BEAM and HaluMem-Medium form the confirmatory test family and carry Holm-adjusted intervals; the other rows carry descriptive 95\% intervals (Appendix E.1). Table 4 also gives the best query-blind prior, a ranking by one feature such as length or position.

\textbf{BEAM (confirmatory): EQUIVALENT on both estimands.} Wrong questions retrieve gold at 4.44\%, against 4.84\% under turn-weighted chance (both item-weighted rates). The chance-corrected excess is CE = −.004, Holm-adjusted interval {[}−.014, .006{]}. Against matched placebos, with common support on 98.8\% of items, CE = −.007, Holm-adjusted interval {[}−.020, .006{]}. Both intervals therefore exclude any query-independent advantage larger than the ±.03 CE margin, about ±2.9 points at BEAM's item-weighted turn-weighted null of 4.84\% (.03 × 95.16 = 2.85; a post hoc, illustrative translation). The EQUIVALENT verdict does not certify that BEAM is free of other cues or that its scores are valid.

\textbf{Fair nulls change interpretations.} On LongMemEval-S, atlas wrong-question retrieval (22.04\%) exceeds uniform chance (18.93\%) by 3.1 points. Turn-weighted chance (21.06\%) accounts for about two-thirds of that gap, leaving CE = .011 {[}−.017, .041{]} (Table 4; Appendix E.4).

\textbf{HaluMem-Medium: not interpreted, because our own adaptation created a shortcut.} Its wrong-question rate is below the turn-weighted null, and the row's tier is REVERSED. Yet position alone separates gold with a single-feature AUROC (within-haystack) of .968 on our prefix haystacks (compare the macro within-pool .838 of position on the evidence-audit pools, §4.1), which makes the row cue-dominated (§3.3). The shortcut comes from our registered prefix haystacks, which end at the later of the question's session and the latest evidence session. Gold is the last unit in 72.3\% of sampled items.

HaluMem-Medium's placebo estimand also lacks common support, with only 20.8\% of items supported. A descriptive sensitivity analysis used the full histories of the same items instead. There the row is not cue-dominated, the best prior falls to uniform chance, and CE is near zero (Table 4).

Among the other rows, LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S, both exploratory, sit near the turn-weighted null with intervals that include 0, and LoCoMo-Plus is slightly above its null. The MemOps round row failed its admission rule, with a single-feature AUROC of .976 for user characters, so it is only descriptive. Its matched-placebo CE is .099 {[}.063, .134{]}, but that interval may be too narrow, because some host conversations are reused across clusters. STALE is cue-dominated, with a single-feature AUROC of 1.000 for typographic marks; on the 63.0\% of its items with placebo support, wrong-question retrieval reaches 41.2\%, against 16.6\% for matched placebos (Appendix E.4).

A strong prior shows that gold differs on a query-blind feature, but not that a query-based retriever's wrong-question hits come from that feature. The best of eight registered single-feature priors ranks units with the fewest turns first on STALE, the last unit first on our HaluMem prefix haystacks, and the longest first on MemOps sessions (the HaluMem and MemOps patterns were known before registration; Appendices E.5 and I.2). Each best prior is a selected maximum and therefore descriptive; a post hoc check compares it with its selection null (Appendix E.4).

The atlas establishes no native reliance on cues, no inflation of published scores, and no cue mechanism. Only the two confirmatory rows can carry placebo tiers, and neither is positive: BEAM's is EQUIVALENT, and HaluMem-Medium's lacks common support. Descriptively, the MemOps round row (CE .099 {[}.063, .134{]}) and STALE's supported subset (CE .272 {[}.231, .314{]}) exceed the .05 practical threshold but receive no tier, because of the failed admission rule and cue dominance, respectively. Design power differs between the two confirmatory rows (Appendix E.4).

Fair nulls thus change the interpretation of wrong-question hits: on BEAM the hits are at chance, and where they exceed chance most (STALE, MemOps), a single query-blind feature already separates gold.

\section{5. On STALE, the cue yields oracle retrieval that is worth 13--21 points to readers}\label{on-stale-the-cue-yields-oracle-retrieval-that-is-worth-1321-points-to-readers}

We next ask whether supplying the evidence that the cue marks helps readers (§5.1) and whether a ranker built from the cue improves retrieval (§5.2). We test both on STALE, where a single rule recovers the gold pair in every scenario. On LoCoMo-Plus, the original ladder's registered hint raised full-context accuracy by +6.2 points over the official prompt (four-host bootstrap interval {[}+1.2, +11.2{]}; t interval {[}−3.2, +15.7{]}), short of its registered +15-point prediction, which the 85.1\% baseline made unreachable (Appendix B.1). LoCoMo-Plus was outside the counterfactual-haystack study by a scope decision that its registration cites (Appendix D.1).

\subsection{5.1. Naming STALE's gold pair beats retrieval pointers, even matched ones}\label{naming-stales-gold-pair-beats-retrieval-pointers-even-matched-ones}

Because the character rule names exactly STALE's gold pair, the artifact pointer supplies exact two-session retrieval without using the question. The readers never see U+2019, because the shared rendering normalizes typography (§3.4), so any pointer that names the gold pair would earn the same advantage. We call this advantage over question-driven retrieval pointers the \emph{gold-pair premium}. It measures the value of the exact retrieval that the cue makes question-blind, for a reader that already holds the full history; it is not a cue-specific effect. Against pointers that name the same number of sessions with the same note, the premium is 13.0--21.0 points of LLM-judged accuracy for four readers from four vendors. The original and three-reader ladders do not match count or note length (§3.4). Every later ladder reuses the original ladder's test split, so it tests robustness, not fresh confirmation.

\textbf{Budget-matched pointers.} Across the four-vendor ladder and the Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run, all eight contrasts against the hybrid-2 and dense-2 pointers, which carry the artifact pointer's note, are ``supported, at least +5'' (Figure 3). The successor run's registration also defined a combined count: the tiers of three readers from the four-vendor ladder plus Gemini 3.8 Flash's tier from the successor run. By this count, the premium is supported for all four readers against each two-session pointer. It is a count of tiers across two runs, not a pooled comparison. Against the five-session pointers, all eight contrasts are ``supported'', but only three reach ``at least +5''. Gemini 3.8 Flash's rows come from the separately registered successor run, which completes the four-vendor ladder's test families without redeciding any of its 12 classifications (Appendix B.5).

The direction of the two-session contrasts was predictable, since the hybrid-2 and dense-2 pointers include the newer gold session in only 130 and 163 of 300 probes. Table 5 shows that these budget-matched comparators are weak: for every reader, both score below the generic instruction, which needs no retrieval, by 3.3--17.0 points. Against the generic instruction, the artifact pointer's margin is 4.0--15.7 points (descriptive), so part of the matched premium reflects weak comparators. Where a two-session set is the gold pair (105 hybrid-2 and 140 dense-2 probes), the two arms send byte-identical requests and so share one answer. Their residual \(D\) of −2.2 to +1.4 points therefore comes from judging alone: judge noise plus a possible halo from the other answers judged in the same call. Elsewhere, the premium can include misdirection by a wrong pointer as well as missing evidence.

Both LLM-adjudicated gates passed (72 determinate items each; agreement .972 and .986, κ .906 and .882; §7.3). Because \(D\) is a difference of judged accuracies between arms, it assumes that judge error does not differ by arm: a leniency common to all arms cancels in \(D\), but an arm-dependent one would not. The four-vendor ladder's packet held no failing artifact-arm answer and the successor's held one, so the judge's false-pass rate on artifact answers is essentially untested where the headline contrast is decided (Appendix B.4.3, B.5.3).
