\textbf{Earlier, unmatched ladders agree.} With GLM-5.3-Flash as reader and binding judge, the original ladder's artifact pointer beats the hybrid-5 pointer by +12.0 points, scenario-cluster interval {[}+8.0, +16.0{]}, and the gap varies across scenario types (Appendix B.1). Accuracy is 63.7\% with the plain template, 83.0\% with the generic instruction, 86.0\% with the hybrid-5 pointer, 98.0\% with the artifact pointer, and 100.0\% with the oracle note. These values come from the original ladder's draw; Figure 4's GLM-5.3-Flash panel is a later descriptive redraw on the same split, so its values differ by up to 2.3 points. Ten test scenarios had earlier pilot outcomes; excluding them retrospectively gives +13.3 {[}+9.3, +17.8{]}, which does not undo the exposure. The hybrid-5 pointer names both gold sessions in 61\% of probes; a dense five-session ranking, which had no reader arm in this ladder, does so in 74\%.

The three-reader ladder added two readers from one vendor, Gemini 3.8 Flash \citep{Google2026Gemini38Flash} and Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite \citep{Google2026Gemini35FlashLite}, to the GLM-5.3-Flash anchor and binding judge (Figure 4); its effect direction was known, and some of its scenarios were pilot-exposed. Against the five-session pointers, all four primary contrasts are ``supported'': +14.0 points {[}+9.0, +19.7{]} against hybrid-5 and +7.0 {[}+3.0, +11.5{]} against dense-5 for Gemini 3.8 Flash, and +19.0 {[}+12.3, +25.7{]} and +17.3 {[}+11.3, +23.7{]} for Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite (Figure 5; 98.75\% Bonferroni per-contrast intervals; Holm-adjusted p = 4/\allowbreak{}10,001 each). All but the Gemini 3.8 Flash dense-5 contrast reach ``at least +5''. The binding judge passed LLM-adjudicated validation (87 items from 59 scenarios; agreement .943, κ .846; Appendix B.2).

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio,alt={Figure 4: three-reader ladder}]{figures/fig4-three-reader-ladder.pdf}
\caption*{\textbf{Figure 4. Three-reader ladder on STALE.} For each of the three readers, the artifact pointer scores highest of the non-oracle arms. The panels show LLM-judged accuracy for three readers and nine arms on the reused test split, with the binding GLM-5.3-Flash judge; every arm supplies the full history. Each arm has 300 planned probes from 100 scenarios; GLM-5.3-Flash's OLD + wrong later control has 297 observed probes (asterisk), and every other arm has all 300. Bars: 95\% scenario-cluster bootstrap intervals; open markers: the privileged oracle notes. The pointer controls NEW only, OLD + wrong later, and All wrong (defined in §3.4) are secondary. The GLM-5.3-Flash panel is a later descriptive redraw of the original ladder's split, not the original ladder's own draw (§5.1). These LLM-judged outcomes support no reader-equivalence or native memory-system inference.}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio,alt={Figure 5: three-reader ladder, primary contrasts}]{figures/fig5-three-reader-contrasts.pdf}
\caption*{\textbf{Figure 5. Primary contrasts of the three-reader ladder.} All four registered contrasts favor the exact two-session artifact pointer over the five-session retrieval pointers. Each contrast is the artifact pointer minus the hybrid-5 or dense-5 pointer over 100 paired scenarios, with the binding GLM-5.3-Flash judge; it changes the session count, the note, and the sessions named at once. Thick lines: 95\% intervals; thin lines: Bonferroni per-contrast intervals at 98.75\% (= 1 − .05/\allowbreak{}4), which target nominal 95\% coverage over the four-contrast test family; dashed line: +5-point threshold. One lower bound, Gemini 3.8 Flash against dense-5, is positive but below +5. On the reused test split, this is a robustness check, not fresh confirmation.}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Instructions that reveal the rubric.} In the original ladder, the three-sentence generic instruction alone closes about 53\% of the plain-to-oracle gap; 51 of its 58 net extra passes are false-premise rejections, the behavior the rubric rewards. In a post hoc analysis that drops the 100 false-premise probes, generic minus plain is +3.5 {[}−1.0, +8.5{]}, while artifact minus hybrid-5 remains +10.5 {[}+6.5, +14.5{]}. In the three-reader ladder, the generic instruction's gain over plain varies by reader, +27.0 points for Gemini 3.8 Flash and +16.0 for Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite, and all eight secondary Holm tests pass .05. For Gemini 3.8 Flash, the generic instruction (93.0\%) exceeds both five-session pointers, so they are weak comparators for that reader. Reader comparisons on STALE therefore need instruction controls (§8).

\textbf{Judge sensitivity.} In the pointer-contrast part of the second-judge study, GPT-6.1 Sol \citep{OpenAIGPT61SolDocs}, at requested medium reasoning, rejudged the three-reader ladder's 900 primary pointer-arm judge calls. All four contrasts keep their tiers, at +12.7, +7.7, +17.3, and +16.3 points. The GPT-6.1 Sol judge passes fewer answers than the GLM-5.3-Flash judge in every cell, with judge model family and temperature (1.0 for GPT-6.1 Sol, 0 for GLM-5.3-Flash) confounded (Appendix C.4). Its gate, Part B of that study, passed on the same 87-item packet against the same blind Claude Opus 5.5 labels (agreement .966, κ .910), but that packet holds only 27 of the 900 judge calls and has LLM, not human, labels. Registered with earlier results known, the second-judge study is a post hoc sensitivity analysis on exposed answers.

The premium also stays positive under the registered GLM-5.3-Flash secondary judge of the four-vendor ladder. This judge agrees with the binding judge on 95.29--98.14\% of paired outcomes across the four-vendor ladder's readers and on 97.41\% in the successor run, and with the adjudicator labels of the two 72-item packets on 71 and 72 of 72 items. Under it, all 16 completed-reader primary contrasts give +12.3 to +22.0 points against two-session pointers and +6.3 to +12.7 against five-session pointers. These point estimates, like the comparisons with the three-reader ladder, are descriptive and assign no tiers (Appendix B.4.11, B.5.11).

The secondary test families support generic instructions over plain prompts and five-session over two-session pointers, but trimming a five-session pointer to two sessions changes the session count, the note, and the sessions named, all at once. The descriptive gold-pair-plus-three arm, which names the gold pair plus three non-gold sessions, identifies no count-versus-identity effect. With a different judge, reader settings, and terminal-unit rules, these results do not redecide the three-reader ladder (Appendix B.4--B.5).

In the one-grader human check (§7.3), the author, a single design-aware grader, graded 50 disagreement-enriched answers of the original ladder after the LLM regrade, and the binding judge agreed with the grader on 46 of them.

Across four readers, binding judges from two vendors, and budget-matched comparators, naming STALE's gold pair beats question-driven retrieval pointers; on a reused split, this measures the value of exact retrieval, not a cue-specific effect.

\subsection{5.2. A constructed style ranker exploits the cue in retrieval on STALE only}\label{a-constructed-style-ranker-exploits-the-cue-in-retrieval-on-stale-only}

A deliberately constructed ranker adds twice the out-of-fold score of a question-blind style classifier to the percentile of the hybrid retrieval score. It raises STALE's all-gold@5 on the original haystack from 65.75\% to 99.75\% (+34.00 points), adds nothing (0.00 points) under source-swap distractors (§6.1), and retains +33.50 points under the failed style manipulation. On LongMemEval-S, the same construction lowers all-gold@5 on the original haystack from 90.85\% to 86.17\% (−4.68 points). This is deliberate ranking exploitability on STALE only, not native reliance or leaderboard inflation (Appendix D.1--D.3).

\section{6. Native reliance is not shown; a RAG pipeline scores equivalently with and without the cues}\label{native-reliance-is-not-shown-a-rag-pipeline-scores-equivalently-with-and-without-the-cues}

We then ask whether retrievers, readers, and memory systems rely on the cues natively, that is, in ordinary use with no pointer or constructed ranker. Replacing STALE's distractors lowers native retrieval recall but also adds harder negatives (§6.1), removing two cue channels leaves one plain reader's accuracy practically equal (§6.2), and the end-to-end tests could not be completed (§6.3). In the official layout, removing the same two channels leaves a RAG pipeline's accuracy equivalent within ±3 points, while a mem0 pipeline's test is inconclusive (§6.4). None of these results shows native reliance.

\subsection{6.1. Replacing STALE's distractors lowers native retrieval recall but adds harder negatives}\label{replacing-stales-distractors-lowers-native-retrieval-recall-but-adds-harder-negatives}

Source-swap distractors, evidence-type sessions from other scenarios (§3.3), lower STALE's hybrid all-gold@5 from 65.75\% to 53.17\%. Differences here are original minus counterfactual, so positive values mean lower recall under the counterfactual. The STALE source-swap retrieval test is POSITIVE: +12.58 points, Holm-adjusted interval {[}+9.64, +15.53{]}. It measures native retrieval sensitivity, not deliberate exploitability, and it is confounded with harder negatives: registered hard negatives rise from 3.36 to 5.98 per probe. Among the 132 scenarios without a mean increase in hard negatives, the drop is +3.54 {[}+0.04, +7.07{]}, but this selected subset cannot separate construction cues from harder interference. The single-unit manipulation check passes for STALE's source-swap distractors by construction, not because every cue has disappeared.

Same-type wrong-question retrieval on STALE is also POSITIVE in the secondary test family, +21.23 points, Holm-adjusted interval {[}+19.95, +22.54{]}: under source-swap distractors, wrong-question any-gold@5 falls from 42.08\% to 20.85\%, near uniform chance (19.2\%). Here the ten wrong questions come from the same dimension or type, whereas the atlas draws them from a different group, so 42.08\% differs from the atlas's 41.40\% (Table 4). STALE is cue-dominated (§4.2), so this cannot be read as a cue-specific causal effect.

The other two primary tests are exploratory because their manipulation check is PARTIAL, and neither establishes equivalence. The STALE style-rewrite test gives −0.25 points, Holm-adjusted interval {[}−1.67, +1.17{]}, and the LongMemEval-S source-swap test gives +1.06, Holm-adjusted interval {[}−0.37, +2.55{]}. Same-type wrong-question retrieval on LongMemEval-S, +7.55 with Holm-adjusted interval {[}+6.60, +8.53{]}, is exploratory for the same reason. For style rewrites, 88.61\% of the unique rewritten sessions pass the automatic (mechanical) checks, below the registered 98\%, and the planned blind 60-pair content check remains UNPERFORMED.

Replacing STALE's distractors therefore changes native retrieval, but the change cannot be attributed to the cue.

\subsection{6.2. Removing typography and clock cues leaves one plain reader's accuracy within ±5 points}\label{removing-typography-and-clock-cues-leaves-one-plain-readers-accuracy-within-5-points}

The de-cue test asks whether a reader's ordinary computation benefits from two cue channels. Typography was shown above to be detectable and, through the artifact pointer, exploitable; clock time is a candidate channel, not tested for detectability in §4.1. In an exploratory count, the evidence pair shares a clock time in 97 of 100 scenarios (Appendix B.3). The test was registered with earlier ladder and judge results known, and two substitute reviews replaced its planned pre-freeze review (Appendix I.2).

The RAW arm renders the ladders' user-only history (§3.4) with STALE's own typography and HH:MM clock times in the session headers. The DECUE arm, which has neither, is the three-reader ladder's plain prompt byte for byte, so its outcome under the GLM-5.3-Flash judge was already known. Nothing else is edited, although RAW prompts are about 1.5\% longer.

A plain Gemini 3.8 Flash reader answered the 300 probes of the reused test split twice per arm. The counterbalanced ABBA order balances mean planned position, but not drift or retry effects. Every answer was judged by GPT-6.1 Sol. Its validity was inherited from Part B of the second-judge study, whose plain-arm subset has nine items (agreement .889, κ .780), and was not retested. The estimand is \(D = \mathrm{acc}(\mathrm{RAW}) - \mathrm{acc}(\mathrm{DECUE})\), with a 95\% scenario-bootstrap interval.

All 1,200 reads and 400 judge calls were valid. The estimated difference is \(D\) = +1.33 points, 95\% interval {[}−1.17, +3.83{]} (sign-flip p = .36), inside {[}−5, +5{]}; LLM-judged accuracy is 64.5\% for RAW and 63.2\% for DECUE. For one plain reader, Gemini 3.8 Flash, full-history accuracy under the two prompts is therefore practically equal within the registered ±5-point margin. This holds on the reused test split, in the ladders' user-only rendering rather than the official STALE evaluation layout, and with the typographic and clock-time cues removed together; clock time is also legitimate template content. The result need not transfer to the official STALE evaluation layout, in which §6.4 tests two pipelines instead of a plain reader.

The interval still allows a benefit from the cues of up to about 4 points. The two channels cannot be separated, and DECUE also removes real time-of-day information. Length, turn-count, and content cues remain in both arms: on the wrong-question atlas's full 400-scenario STALE population, the fewest-turns prior alone reaches an any-gold@5 of 96.1\%. Reliance on these remaining cues is untested, as is reliance by other plain readers and in other layouts; §6.4 tests two pipelines in the official layout.

\subsection{6.3. The end-to-end tests could not be completed (final INVALID)}\label{the-end-to-end-tests-could-not-be-completed-final-invalid}

No end-to-end comparison yields an admissible primary estimate. The counterfactual-haystack study's planned end-to-end stage stopped at its registered budget-forecast rule before any end-to-end execution step ran (registered outcome: branch not computed). The separately registered end-to-end successor study was designed on the same populations after the positive STALE source-swap retrieval test and the hard-negative results were known (§6.1), so it neither erases that stop nor supplies an independent replication. It ran hybrid top-five retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and a mem0 2.2.1 pipeline \citep{Mem0Library} on the original haystack and with source-swap distractors, with GLM-5.3-Flash as reader and judge. The mem0 pipeline kept mem0's memory decisions unmodified; its adapter was the registered one plus the successor's protocol amendment M (registered as annex M), an extraction-budget escalation. Amendment M, a declared measurement deviation, raised the token budget for extraction and judge attempts that had come back empty because their budget ran out (Appendix D.5).

The RAG and mem0 end-to-end tests are both final INVALID, because judge failures left two of the four primary cells, one per test, below the completeness rule's unchanged 98\% bar. All 8,250 test reads succeeded, but 19 of the 2,750 binding judge calls, across all test cells, failed terminally. Each call covers three probes, and a primary probe needs verdicts for all three of its reads, so the four primary cells lose 42 probe-cell entries in total, a different denominator from the 19 calls. RAG with source-swap distractors keeps 552/\allowbreak{}567 (97.35\%) and mem0 on the original haystack 288/\allowbreak{}300 (96.00\%). RAG on the original haystack keeps 558/\allowbreak{}567 (98.41\%), and mem0 with source-swap distractors keeps 294/\allowbreak{}300 (98.00\%), exactly at the bar.

After an outage, one repair of the memory-store allocation was needed; with the two repair cycles inherited from the counterfactual-haystack study, it exceeded the registered maximum of two, and we report it as a protocol deviation. Terminal judge losses could not be retried, so the incomplete cells are final (Appendix D.4--D.6). We report no primary effect, interval, or McNemar \citep{McNemar1947} result, and we draw no inflation, null, equivalence, or upper-bound conclusion. The positive source-swap retrieval test does not make these invalid tests a negative end-to-end result.

The GLM-5.3-Flash judge passed both validation gates of the successor study against adjudicator labels (development 57/\allowbreak{}60, κ .900; test 56/\allowbreak{}60, κ .866; Appendix D.5.2). The adjudicator labels came from Claude Sonnet 5.5 in two fresh API contexts that saw only the packet; they are not human labels. Passing these gates cannot restore the missing GLM-5.3-Flash verdicts on GLM-5.3-Flash answers. Missing judgments need not be random, copy diagnostics do not certify independent generations, and cell means with different valid populations cannot replace the suppressed comparisons (Appendix D.5--D.6).

Even complete tests could not have separated inflation from harder interference, because source-swap distractors also add hard negatives (§6.1). The two eligible nonprimary contrasts, both original minus counterfactual, have ordinary 95\% intervals spanning zero. The gold-pair-plus-three context (called Oracle-5 in this study), which holds the gold pair plus three fixed distractors and bounds reading interference only, gives −0.71 points {[}−3.92, +2.48{]}, a point estimate slightly in favor of the source-swap haystack. The exploratory style-rewrite RAG contrast on read 1 gives −2.33 {[}−5.56, +0.90{]}, likewise slightly in favor of the style rewrites. No further successor on these populations is allowed (Appendix D.4--D.6).

No end-to-end test therefore bears on inflation in either direction.

\subsection{6.4. Two pipelines on STALE with and without its cues}\label{two-pipelines-on-stale-with-and-without-its-cues}

The pipeline de-cue study (S20; Appendix L) asks whether two pipelines score differently on STALE as distributed (RAW) than with its typography and session clock times removed (DECUE), in the official STALE evaluation layout. DECUE applies the typographic normalizer of §3.4 to every turn and probe and keeps only the date in every session stamp. The study is not a successor of the invalid end-to-end tests of §6.3: it estimates a different contrast on a reused population, so it is conditional robustness evidence, not fresh confirmation.

\textbf{Design.} S20-B1 is a hybrid top-five RAG pipeline (the retriever of §3.4 over user turns) whose reader sees the top five sessions with both roles (398 scenarios). S20-B2 runs the mem0 2.2.1 library \citep{Mem0Library}, its memory decisions unmodified, through our registered adapter, with one store per scenario and condition (150 scenarios). GLM-5.3-Flash extracts, reads three times per probe and condition, and judges, at temperature 0; the judge sees the same gold fields and probes in both conditions, so only the answers differ. The estimand \(\Delta = \mathrm{acc}(\mathrm{RAW}) - \mathrm{acc}(\mathrm{DECUE})\), clustered by scenario, is tested one-sided for a benefit of the cues, with Holm adjustment over the two tests and a smallest effect size of interest of 5 points. The equivalence margins are ±3 points for S20-B1 and ±5 for S20-B2, whose separately built RAW and DECUE stores add store (extraction) variance (§7.1), so that ±3 would need more scenarios than STALE has. Each test takes the first registered tier that holds: POSITIVE, SMALL, EQUIVALENT, REVERSED, BELOW-SESOI, or INCONCLUSIVE (Appendix L.1).

\textbf{Pre-freeze changes and power.} By the author's decision, the throughput preflight ran one step instead of five. At its 1,660.9 output tokens per second, the smallest design would have needed an expected 32.0 hours with both-role mem0 ingestion, above the registered 24-hour threshold, so user-turn ingestion was registered before the freeze. It keeps both cues: U+2019 occurs in the user turns of 799 of the 800 evidence sessions and in no distractor user turn, and the stamp carries the clock time. At a true \(\Delta\) of 0, TOST power was .90--.95 for S20-B1 and, at 150 scenarios, .58--.74 for S20-B2 (Holm worst to best case).

\textbf{Results.} The judge passed validation against a fresh Claude Opus 5.5 instance that saw only the 60-item packet (agreement 53/\allowbreak{}60 = .883, κ .769). Complete read pairs reach 99.50\% for S20-B1 and 99.11\% for S20-B2, above the 98\% bar, and on the no-history control's 100 scenarios, pooled accuracy (60.4\% and 48.2\%) far exceeds the control's 6.3\%. S20-B1 is EQUIVALENT: \(\Delta\) = +0.15 points, Holm-adjusted interval {[}−1.29, +1.60{]}, Holm-adjusted TOST \(p \approx 1.2 \times 10^{-5}\) at ±3. S20-B2 is INCONCLUSIVE: \(\Delta\) = +4.30 {[}−0.21, +8.89{]}. Its Holm-adjusted one-sided superiority p is .033, but the registered rule decides by the interval, whose lower bound is below 0.

In the official layout, removing typography and clock times together leaves the RAG pipeline's LLM-judged accuracy equivalent within ±3 points. S20 gives the paper's first admissible primary end-to-end estimates. This joint contrast is not evidence that the pipeline ignores either cue: the cues were removed together, opposite effects could cancel, and using a cue need not change accuracy. For mem0, the estimate favors the cue-bearing text by 4.3 points, but the test neither shows nor excludes reliance of up to about 9 points. Clock times are also legitimate temporal content that mem0's extraction and memory ordering may use, so a RAW advantage would not by itself show artifact exploitation. GLM-5.3-Flash judges its own answers, the results hold only for these pipelines and this layout, and turn-count, length, and content cues remain. None of this shows that a published score is inflated.

\section{7. Labels are unstable item by item, and scoring rules can reward forgetting}\label{labels-are-unstable-item-by-item-and-scoring-rules-can-reward-forgetting}

A cue can be shown to change a score only if the score itself is stable. We therefore ask how much item-level labels and run-level accuracy move when an unchanged pipeline is rebuilt (§§7.1--7.2), how far the LLM labels behind §§5--7 can be trusted (§7.3), and what a public scoring rule rewards (§7.4).

\subsection{7.1. Replicas of one memory pipeline disagree on 16\% of judged labels, but run-level accuracy is stable}\label{replicas-of-one-memory-pipeline-disagree-on-16-of-judged-labels-but-run-level-accuracy-is-stable}

The replica study rebuilt one bespoke GLM-5.3-Flash memory pipeline, modeled on Mem0 \citep{Chhikara2025Mem0} but not its code, ten times on four LoCoMo conversations. On the 671 complete-case questions (Figure 6), the mean pairwise label disagreement in LLM-judged correctness is 16.25\%. These are the questions with labels from all ten replicas and from the reruns and raw-turn controls aligned with them; 680 of the 692 questions have all ten replica labels (Appendix C.1). The replica study's judge was validated against LLM labels on development answers only. In post hoc checks, byte-identical answers with identical judge prompts make up 1.2\% of disagreeing pair cells. Latency and token counts indicate that the shared API proxy answered some repeated requests with a cached copy; dropping the cells these copies touch moves the disagreement to 16.32\% (Appendix H.1).

The pair-mean oracle gain is \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\) = 8.12 points {[}7.18, 9.07{]}, with a conversation-level t interval over the four conversations of about {[}5.77, 10.48{]}; \(G_{\mathrm{better}}\) is 7.41. Run-level accuracy, by contrast, varies little: a nested model gives an accuracy SD of 1.04 points {[}0.65, 1.39{]} across the ten replicas. This is what averaging predicts. If item labels flipped independently, a run's accuracy over \(n = 671\) questions would have an SD of \(\sqrt{d/(2n)}\), with \(d\) = 16.25\% taken as a proportion: \(\sqrt{0.1625/1{,}342} \approx 1.10\) points. Stable run-level accuracy is therefore the average of unstable labels, not a separate finding. Under designated-store assumptions, the model attributes 57\%, 29\%, and 14\% of the variance to store (extraction), reader, and judge (Appendix C.1).

In the one-grader human check (§7.3), the judge agreed with the author on 45 of 50 sampled replica answers, and 10 of 15 sampled pairs whose judge labels disagree were real correctness differences for this grader.

Our 8.9-point reference gain, recomputed from the released labels of \citet{SharmaLall2026Selection} as the gain of a per-question oracle union of two of their systems over the better one, serves only as a reference scale (§3.5); it is a quantity we derived, not a figure taken from their paper. Dividing our gain by theirs would not estimate what share of their improvement is noise, and we do not claim that their gap is noise.

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=0.72\textheight,keepaspectratio,alt={Figure 6: replica oracle}]{figures/fig6-replica-oracle.pdf}
\caption*{\textbf{Figure 6. Replica label disagreement and its per-question oracle union (not deployable).} Two replicas of one pipeline disagree on 16.25\% of LLM-judged labels, so a per-question oracle gains 8.12 points over their mean, and the gain grows with more replicas. (A) The bespoke GLM-5.3-Flash pipeline of the replica study, not upstream Mem0: 671 complete-case questions of 692 planned (Appendix C.1) in four LoCoMo conversations, ten replicas, and 45 replica pairs. Label disagreement is not established error. \(G_{\mathrm{mean}} = d/2\), where \(d\) is the label disagreement, is the gain over the pair mean, 8.12 points, with a 95\% interval {[}7.18, 9.07{]} conditional on these four conversations; \(G_{\mathrm{better}}\), 7.41, uses the better member of each pair by pooled-question accuracy and has no interval. (B) Oracle union over \(k\) replicas, averaged over every size-\(k\) subset, minus mean single-replica accuracy, for \(k\) = 2 to 10, without a band. Panel B is exploratory: the oracle selects with correctness labels, so it is not a deployable selector, and its endpoint is not a leaderboard gain. Questions and pairs have equal weights; pairs share replicas and are not independent observations.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{7.2. Replica follow-ups failed judge validation}\label{replica-follow-ups-failed-judge-validation}

One follow-up stopped at its gate, and two are UNRESOLVED. The crossed-family follow-up stopped at its development gate, where the Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite judge's design-weighted κ of .635 on a 70-item, outcome-enriched packet missed .70 (GLM-5.3-Flash: .717); no test ran.

The extractor follow-up tested whether \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\) exceeded 4.45 points, half of that 8.9-point reference gain, in two cells with Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite and GLM-5.3-Flash extractors. Its analysis code was amended after the lock while its test ran (Appendix I.2). Its GLM-5.3-Flash judge failed the test validation on design-weighted κ, .697 against .70, although agreement passed at .876, so both cells are UNRESOLVED (judge validation). This is a near-threshold miss that a fresh packet could reverse (§7.3). Descriptively, on 1,112 questions from seven reused conversations, \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\) is 8.53 {[}7.48, 9.58{]} with Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite extraction and 7.90 {[}6.36, 9.44{]} with fresh GLM-5.3-Flash extraction. Nine of the judge's ten packet disagreements involve GLM-5.3-Flash-extractor answers, so a near-zero extractor contrast does not show that the extractors behave alike.

Part A of the second-judge study, a post hoc sensitivity analysis, had GPT-6.1 Sol rejudge 3,150 of these answers. This judge failed the same gate (design-weighted agreement .851, κ .649), so both cells remain UNRESOLVED. Under it, the descriptive gains are lower, with judge model family and temperature (1.0 for GPT-6.1 Sol, 0 for GLM-5.3-Flash) confounded (Appendix C.3). Both judges disagree with the adjudicator mainly on GLM-5.3-Flash-extractor items, and the validation packet cannot determine which labels are correct (Appendix C.2--C.3).

\subsection{7.3. How far the LLM labels can be trusted}\label{how-far-the-llm-labels-can-be-trusted}

Every result in §§5--7 rests on LLM-judged labels. This section gathers the evidence on how far those labels can be trusted: the registered judge-validation gates and their noise, a regrade by three other LLMs, and a one-grader human check (Table 6).

\textbf{Gates.} A registered gate requires agreement of at least .80 and Cohen's κ of at least .70 between the binding judge and an LLM adjudicator on a packet of at least 60 labeled or determinate items (§3.5). Every gate is LLM adjudication, not human validation, and each covers only the answers its packet was drawn from. The reader-ladder gates passed by wide margins (agreement .943--.986, κ .846--.910), and so did the end-to-end successor study's two gates (§6.3). The replica gates failed, or passed narrowly, near the κ threshold. The replica study's own judge was validated on development answers only, and the de-cue test inherited its judge's validation from the second-judge study without retesting it on its own answers (§6.2).

\textbf{Gate noise.} Near the thresholds, a gate is a noisy classifier of judge quality. In a post hoc simulation that treats the observed agreement tables as true, a judge like the extractor follow-up's passes a gate-sized packet in about 43--49\% of draws, and the three-reader ladder's judge in about 87--97\% (Appendix I.3). These are conditional pass fractions, not probabilities that a fresh packet passes or that the true κ exceeds .70. A failed gate therefore withholds a verdict; it does not show that the judge is unreliable, and a passed gate does not certify it.

\textbf{LLM regrade.} Three LLM adjudicators, Claude Sonnet 5.5 \citep{AnthropicSonnet55Announcement}, Gemini 3.8 Flash, and GPT-6.1 Sol, graded 100 items, 50 answers of the original ladder and 50 replica answers, with the judges' own rubrics and before the human grade. Some comparison code postdates the regrade's lock (Appendix F). Their design-weighted agreement with the GLM-5.3-Flash judge is .939--.996 on the ladder answers and .965--.981 on the replica answers. Their regraded replica gains, 6.09--7.23 points, are not corrected estimates of \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\): they apply to a wider 692-question frame and fix at zero the disagreement hidden in pairs whose judge labels agree (Appendix F.3). Precision is unresolved, because the design-weighted points lean on strata with two to seven decided items (Appendix F.4). As a regrader, GPT-6.1 Sol passes fewer of the original ladder's pointer answers than the GLM-5.3-Flash judge (design-weighted 74.1\% against 86.6\%), but this gap rests on two of 17 sampled items, and on both the human grader sided with the judge (post hoc). Neither regrade supplies ground truth, and neither satisfies nor resets the gates of the extractor follow-up or the second-judge study.

\textbf{One-grader human check.} The author, a single design-aware grader rather than a panel, graded the same 100 items with arm, reader, judge, and verdict concealed, although residual cues remained (Appendix J.1). The human route was reinstated after the LLM regrade and was not registered (Appendix J.6). According to the author's record, the grading used an LLM-translated Turkish/\allowbreak{}English rendering with the English rubrics binding; the exported grades cannot confirm which rendering was used. The translation was prepared and checked without access to grades or outcomes, but 76 of the 100 items had no full semantic review of it, so these labels assume that the rendering preserved each answer's content (Appendix J.6). The grader's labels are not ground truth, and with one grader there is no human--human agreement. With 50 items per study, below the 60-item minimum, the check is not a gate, and no registered decision depends on it (Appendix I.3).

On the 50 original-ladder answers, which oversample judge disagreements, the binding judge agreed with the grader on 46 (agreement .920, κ .83; design-weighted .991, κ .97). Its design-weighted pass rate is within 1 point of the grader's in every arm group (78.3\% against 77.9\% overall). These point estimates show no leniency signal, but they cannot bound leniency, or a judge-error difference between the two arms of the +12.0-point contrast, to a few points (Appendix J.3).

On the 50 replica answers, the judge agreed with the grader on 45 (agreement .900, κ .80; design-weighted .969, κ .93). Of 15 sampled pairs whose judge labels disagree, 10 were real correctness differences for the grader under the generous Mem0-paper rubric, two were judge-lenient, and three were judge-strict. The estimated real share is 68.6\% (pair-weighted; bootstrap {[}42.6, 91.2{]}), which does not exclude a minority. On the check's 692-question frame, the human-graded label disagreement \(d_{\mathrm{h}}\) is an estimated 11.3\%, and the human-graded gain \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) is 5.65 points (registered bootstrap interval {[}3.51, 7.52{]}), against the judge's 16.49\% and 8.25 points on the same frame. The 5.65 is the locked code's calibrated (Hájek-type) estimate, which rescales the weights of the sampled pairs to the known number of pairs whose judge labels disagree, 5,110. The plan's literal Horvitz--Thompson total \citep{HorvitzThompson1952}, computed post hoc, is 6.14, because those weights sum to 5,546.8 (6.14 ≈ 5.65 × 5,546.8/\allowbreak{}5,110). The interval fixes at zero the disagreement hidden in pairs whose judge labels agree, as none appeared in ten random pairs. \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) is therefore not a corrected \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\), and the direction of judge error stays open (Appendix J.4).

For this grader, the replica disagreement of our bespoke pipeline on four conversations is not explained away as judge noise. Across the gates, the regrade, and the human check, the LLM labels agree closely with other graders on these packets. The packets are small, outcome-enriched, and drawn from few answer sets, however, so they cannot bound judge error, or its differences between arms, to a few points.

\subsection{7.4. ForgetEval's public score can reward wiping memory}\label{forgetevals-public-score-can-reward-wiping-memory}

The null-memory policies of the null-policy study, trivial baselines such as storing nothing or wiping memory, show what public metrics reward; none is a comparison with a published system. On ForgetEval, introduced by \citet{Yang2026ForgetEval}, a wipe-plus-replacement policy passes 231 of 385 adversarial and 750 of 1,000 template cases by storing the supplied replacement, against 96/\allowbreak{}385 and 350/\allowbreak{}1,000 for storing nothing. A retrospective (post hoc) template-preservation check, a diagnostic use of construction labels rather than a registered evaluation (Appendix G.1), turns the wipe policy's 750 public passes into zero. A separate, registered coverage check asks whether a strict keep set, the material that must be preserved, can be derived for each item. It succeeds on 286/\allowbreak{}385 = 74.3\% of the adversarial part, below the registered 80\% bar, and that failure stands. Appendix G also gives an analytic LoCoMo abstention floor, a containment surrogate on FactConsolidation from MemoryAgentBench \citep{Hu2026MemoryAgentBench}, tables, and the failed closure study.

The public score therefore rewards discarding memory that a preservation check would require.

\section{8. Recommendations}\label{recommendations}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Benchmark construction.} Scan for single-feature separation and query-blind priors. Disclose evidence and filler sources, evidence labels, unit groups, and duplicate exclusions. Prespecify enough grouped permutation refits that family-adjusted significance is attainable. Compare wrong-question retrieval with fair-null baselines (uniform, turn-weighted, and matched-placebo chance), also on adapted haystacks. Control negative hardness, and prefer minimal-edit de-cues.
\item
  \textbf{Readers and systems.} State models, settings, and prompt rendering. Include cheap instruction controls and pointer controls matched in session count and note length. Report how many evidence tokens are returned. Report named or retrieved evidence, complete support \citep{Wang2026ExecutionProvenance}, and correctness separately.
\item
  \textbf{Replicas.} Report the number of reruns, the masks and weights, and how the better replica is selected. Give both conditional and conversation-level uncertainty. Audit repeated answers.
\item
  \textbf{Judges.} Validate judges on the compared answers, with registered packets of sufficient size. Report failed gates. Do not treat LLM agreement as ground truth.
\item
  \textbf{Forgetting.} Report closure of the required set and collateral loss separately. Do not treat retrieval depth as completeness.
\item
  \textbf{Review.} Tie each strong claim to its estimand and to a comparison that could falsify it. Inspect exposure history as well as protocol locks.
\end{enumerate}

\section{9. Limitations}\label{limitations}

\textbf{Selection and scope.} We chose settings with tractable adapters, not a sample that could estimate how common cues are. Weak discrimination does not rule out a cue that other features, evidence labels, or detectors could reveal. Each result in §§4--7 states its main confounds and scope limits; this section adds the general and secondary ones. Pending follow-ups, stopped stages, the completeness-invalid end-to-end tests, and the inconclusive mem0 de-cue test supply no negative finding, and none of them establishes the absence of inflation; nor does the equivalent RAG de-cue test, which estimates a different contrast (§6.4).

In the end-to-end successor study, a third repair cycle exceeded the registered maximum of two; we report it as a protocol deviation (§6.3). A required auxiliary check, in which Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite rejudges the reference packets, stopped after 18 development calls at its conservative cost cap, and its test run was not performed; the optional Gemini 3.8 Flash reader was not used (Appendix D.5). At the design stage, the study had a nominal probability of about .5 of a POSITIVE verdict at a true five-point effect, a ceiling set by the rule's requirement that the estimate be at least +5. Its mem0 equivalence test (two one-sided tests, TOST), with a ±3-point margin at the two-test Holm level α = .025, had at most .13 power under the planning scenario-SD range of 14--17 percentage points over 100 scenarios. These are design estimates, not observed-power calculations (Appendix D.6).

In the pipeline de-cue study, the throughput preflight ran one step instead of the registered five, the registered review of the preflight's live-call code was not repeated after that code changed, and one post-lock software repair changed the validation-packet checker (Appendix L.2--L.3). The run was interrupted by a machine restart and resumed from saved state under the registered resume rules. Before the registered gate ran, the author authorized a nonregistered provisional analysis with a different LLM rater; no number from it enters this paper. The mem0 test ingested user turns only, not mem0's both-role input, and at its 150 scenarios had a design power of only .58--.74 for equivalence within ±5 points at a true difference of 0.

\textbf{Judging.} Correctness labels come from LLM judges, often GLM-5.3-Flash, which also served as reader and extractor, and every judge-validation gate is LLM adjudication (§7.3). The judges of the original ladder and the replica study also have the one-grader human check, in which the design-aware author graded 50 answers each. The packets are small and outcome-enriched, most of them were labeled by one adjudicator model (Claude Opus 5.5), and the replica judges' gate decisions fell near the thresholds.

In the four-vendor ladder, GPT-6.1 Sol also judged its own model family's reader. That ladder's exploratory self-preference diagnostic \citep[cf.][]{Panickssery2024SelfPreference} takes the binding-minus-secondary pass-rate gap (GPT-6.1 Sol minus GLM-5.3-Flash) for the GPT-6.1 Sol reader and subtracts the mean gap for the Gemini 3.8 Flash and Muse Spark 1.3 readers, so positive values would indicate self-preference. It is −1.27 points {[}−2.44, −0.20{]}: if anything, the binding judge was slightly stricter with its own family's reader. Its baseline includes partial Gemini 3.8 Flash data (17 of 100 scenarios), its interval is not multiplicity-adjusted, and it does not establish a general absence of bias.

\textbf{Dependence and exposure.} Questions share conversations and templates, replica pairs share members, and conditional intervals are not population guarantees. The three-reader ladder rests on one draw per cell and ran its arms in a fixed order (Appendix B.2). The four-vendor ladder and the Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run rest on one read per distinct request.

Eight follow-ups used populations whose outcomes had already been seen: the three-reader ladder, the extractor follow-up, the end-to-end successor study, the second-judge study, the de-cue test, the four-vendor ladder, the Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run, and the pipeline de-cue study. Excluding exposed items afterward cannot create a fresh test. The Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run was registered after the author had seen the four-vendor ladder's uninterpreted partial estimates for that reader. The pointer-contrast part of the second-judge study was reinstated after the sparse pointer signal of the LLM regrade (§7.3) was known. The forecast rule of the end-to-end successor study was chosen when it was already known that the counterfactual-haystack study's planned end-to-end stage would stop (Appendix D.5).

\textbf{Limits of the recomputations.} Separate computational checks recomputed the registered numbers from the saved outputs (Appendix I.4). They can share loaders, labels, and conventions with the analyses they check, and some replay saved predictions instead of refitting models, so they establish numerical consistency, not label validity or independent-laboratory replication. The four-vendor ladder, the Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run, and the one-grader human check each had two separate recomputations of their results. The two recomputations of the counterfactual-haystack and end-to-end successor studies did not refit classifiers or rerun retrieval (Appendix D.6). The pipeline de-cue study's numbers were recomputed twice from the saved outputs, byte for byte, by its registered replay check, which uses the study's own code (Appendix L.4). The recomputations also found denominator errors and overstated interpretations (Appendix H.1). The executed checkpoint revision of the configured bge-small-en-v1.5 dense encoder was not recorded (Appendix B).

The refits of the second post hoc round were not repeated, and the 1,000-refit permutation nulls of §4.1 were checked only from saved values. In these nulls, margins over the largest null are thin in PersonaMem-v2 (+.0028) and BEAM 100K (+.0084), where more draws could lift p.~On STALE, the permuted statistic is scored on only 257--324 of the 400 pools, a substantial change of population (Appendix H.1, which also reports an earlier 50-refit run).

\textbf{Future work.} Official-layout de-cue evidence now exists for one RAG pipeline (§6.4). These checks remain proposed or unrun: a blind second human grader, more random replica pairs whose judge labels agree, a de-cue of STALE's remaining length and turn-count cues, separate de-cues of typography and clock time, a mem0 de-cue precise enough to exclude a 5-point benefit, de-cues of other readers and memory systems in the official layout, and an end-to-end test on fresh populations whose replacement distractors keep negative hardness fixed.

\section{10. Conclusion}\label{conclusion}

Evidence can be located without the question in selected settings, most strongly on STALE and LoCoMo-Plus, while on BEAM atlas wrong-question retrieval is equivalent to chance under fair nulls for our hybrid retriever and unit definitions. On STALE, the cue yields question-blind oracle retrieval. On a reused test split, this raises LLM-judged accuracy by 13--21 points over budget-matched retrieval pointers for four readers from four vendors; it is the value of exact retrieval, not a cue-specific effect, and those pointers trail a pointer-free instruction. That instruction, three sentences that reveal what STALE's rubric rewards, closes about 53\% of the plain-to-oracle gap for the original reader, mostly through false-premise rejections, so reader comparisons need instruction controls. Native reliance on the cues is not shown: removing typography and clock times together leaves one plain reader's accuracy practically equal in a non-official layout and, in the official layout, leaves a hybrid RAG pipeline's accuracy equivalent within ±3 points. For a mem0 pipeline the question remains open, because its de-cue test is inconclusive; neither end-to-end test of replaced distractors could be completed. Two rebuilds of an unchanged pipeline disagree on 16\% of LLM-judged item labels on average, while run-level accuracy has an SD of about 1 point, as averaging over 671 questions predicts; item-level comparisons between memory systems therefore need replica reruns. The LLM labels behind these results agree closely with other graders on small packets, but no check bounds judge error to a few points. Public scoring rules such as ForgetEval's can reward discarding memory. The checks in §8 are cheap, and memaudit implements the question-blind scan, the fair-null comparison, null-memory-policy scoring, and replica replay. None of these findings shows that a published score is inflated.

\section*{Release, licenses and toolkit}\label{release-licenses-and-toolkit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Release, licenses and toolkit}

\textbf{What is released.} The paper, its appendix, and the toolkit memaudit 0.1.0 are public. The release holds code, documentation, invented examples, identifiers, and aggregate statistics only: no benchmark text, converted data, raw model or judge output, per-item outcome, or prediction archive is redistributed. The preregistrations, amendments, lock hashes, per-item outputs, study analysis code, and audit memos behind the registered verdicts are not public; the appendix describes them. The readers, judges, and adjudicators were called by provider model identifiers without dated snapshots (Appendix K.7). Providers may update or retire these models, so the LLM-judged studies of §§5--7 cannot be rerun exactly.

\textbf{Licenses.} Recorded licenses range from MIT and CC BY to noncommercial and no-derivatives terms. We found no license for LoCoMo-Plus and use it only for local exploratory statistics. The compatibility of PersonaMem-v2 with its upstream license, that of PersonaHub \citep{Ge2024PersonaHub}, and the licenses of LongMemEval's filler data remain unresolved. We do not certify clearance for the exact revisions we used. These are source disclosures, not legal clearance (Appendix H.3).

\textbf{Toolkit.} The toolkit memaudit 0.1.0 (Apache-2.0; \url{https://github.com/ozgurugurlu/memaudit}) is an open-source, offline CPU implementation of four measurements of this paper: the question-blind unit-style audit (§4.1); wrong-question retrieval against the fair-null baselines, with the cue-dominance check and the query-blind priors (§4.2); the null-memory policies under ForgetEval's own scorer, run from a user-supplied, pinned copy of its source (§7.4); and replica replay from numeric correctness labels (§7.1). It runs no language model, so the reader ladders, judges, and end-to-end pipelines of §§5--7 are outside its scope. Its default retriever is BM25; the atlas's hybrid retriever is an option that needs a local copy of the dense encoder. Its documentation records a local verification run of a development candidate (4 October 2026) that reproduced, from the public files, the STALE and LoCoMo-Plus AUROCs of Figure 1 and the stored aggregates of the LoCoMo-Plus atlas row, with BM25 and with the hybrid option. It packages no converter for the confirmatory BEAM and HaluMem-Medium rows, so it is an implementation of the audit's measurements, not a reproduction package for the paper. The source and installed-wheel test suites each pass 145 tests, with one optional real-model test skipped (macOS arm64, CPython 3.12.12), and its GitHub CI passes on macOS and Ubuntu. The toolkit ships no corpora or raw outputs (Appendix H.3).

\textbf{The author.} The paper has a single author. In this appendix, ``the author'' always means that person, who was also the only human grader (Appendix J). All other answer grades come from LLM judges, adjudicators, and regraders, each named by its model.

\textbf{Study records.} None of the records below is public, and this appendix states in words whatever it uses from them. A study's preregistration (PREREG in the archive) fixes its question, populations, estimands, and decision rules before the test, and a hash lock fixes it together with the study's code. A protocol amendment (registered as an annex) adds operational definitions to the preregistration or changes its rules; an amendment locked together with the preregistration is part of the registration, and one made after the lock is disclosed with its timing and scope. A study's report gives its registered results, deviations, and exposure. An audit memo (AUDIT-n in the archive) records a separate computational check of a study: a recomputation of its registered numbers from the saved outputs, with the scope of the check.

\textbf{Models and labels.} Models carry the main text's display names; Appendix K.7 lists their API identifiers and settings. In tables, GLM is short for GLM-5.3-Flash; other short labels are defined in the table notes.

\textbf{Abbreviations.}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  AUROC: area under the receiver operating characteristic curve, reported as detectability AUROC (macro within-pool) or single-feature AUROC (within-haystack).
\item
  BM25: the Okapi BM25 lexical ranking function \citep{RobertsonZaragoza2009BM25}.
\item
  CE: chance-corrected excess, the item mean of (wrong-question hit rate − null rate)/\allowbreak{}(1 − null rate) (§3.3).
\item
  CP: Clopper--Pearson, the exact binomial interval or bound.
\item
  CR/\allowbreak{}KU: contradiction resolution and knowledge update, the two BEAM probing categories whose evidence labels the audit uses.
\item
  CR1: a cluster-robust standard error with the small-sample factor \(m/(m-1)\) for \(m\) clusters.
\item
  HGB: histogram gradient boosting, the classifier of the detectability audit and of the manipulation checks (Appendix K.2).
\item
  HT: Horvitz--Thompson, the inverse-inclusion-probability estimator.
\item
  LLM: large language model.
\item
  MDE: minimum detectable effect.
\item
  QI: query-independence rate, the mean any-gold@5 hit of one item over its ten wrong questions (Appendix E.1).
\item
  RAG: retrieval-augmented generation.
\item
  RRF: reciprocal rank fusion \citep{Cormack2009RRF}.
\item
  SESOI: smallest effect size of interest.
\item
  SR, PR, IPA: STALE's three probe types, state resolution, premise resistance (the false-premise probe), and implicit policy adaptation \citep{Chao2026STALE}.
\item
  T1, T2: STALE's two scenario types, co-referential (Type I) and propagated (Type II).
\item
  TOST: two one-sided tests, an equivalence test.
\end{itemize}

\section{A. Evidence detectability and the scope of the audit}\label{a.-evidence-detectability-and-the-scope-of-the-audit}

\subsection{A.1. Protocol and populations}\label{a.1.-protocol-and-populations}

Appendix A supports the main text's audit design (§3.2), detectability results (§4.1), and limitations (§9). The classifier asks whether an evidence unit can be distinguished from its distractors without the current question. It does not answer the question, run a memory system, or estimate how much a published system's score is inflated.

The classifier is a histogram gradient-boosting (HGB) classifier on 31 counts and rates that describe a unit's typography, formatting, length, and lexical surface form; it never sees the question, evidence ids, or timestamps. It was evaluated with five-fold cross-validation whose groups are the pools or the conversations, users, or hosts that contain them, so that no group appears on both sides of a split. Appendix K.2 specifies the learner (library, version, and settings), the groups and seed of each setting, and the definition of every feature; Appendix K.1 gives the benchmark revisions and populations. The score, detectability AUROC (macro within-pool), is the unweighted mean of the AUROCs of the scored pools. It is computed after the test-duplicate exclusion of §3.2, and a pool is scored only if it keeps both classes. The 95\% intervals come from 2,000 group-bootstrap resamples of the saved out-of-fold predictions, so they omit the uncertainty of refitting the model.

The open-source toolkit memaudit 0.1.0 implements this classifier (\nolinkurl{memaudit audit run}). Its documentation reports that the command regenerates the STALE and LoCoMo-Plus rows of Table A1, and so of Figure 1, to four decimals from the pinned public files. The other seven rows were not checked against the toolkit, so memaudit is not a reproduction package for Table A1 or Figure 1.

The exclusion matters to interpretation. In STALE, it removes 10,540 of the 19,200 distractor rows and keeps all 800 evidence rows, so the mean candidate pool shrinks from 50 to 23.65 units; the perfect detectability AUROC describes the retained rows. For LongMemEval-S, an alternative that keeps every test row and removes duplicates from the training side instead scores all 470 pools at .752 {[}.732, .769{]}; the .783 value describes the smaller scored population and does not show an improvement on an unchanged population. For BEAM 1M, the 1M split holds 35 conversations, but the adapter drops the four whose message ids are duplicated or not strictly increasing, because their evidence labels cannot be matched to messages unambiguously. These are the same four nonmonotonic conversations that the wrong-question atlas excludes from its primary BEAM row (Appendix E.2). The preregistration lists the remaining 31, which is also the count in Figure 1, so 35 is not the registered population.

\subsection{A.2. From construction cues to a measurement claim}\label{a.2.-from-construction-cues-to-a-measurement-claim}

The strongest evidence is the contrast across settings, together with the downstream reader and retrieval controls; we do not claim that every benchmark admits the same shortcut. The initial reader branch of S10, the evidence-audit study, failed its judge gate and supplies no confirmed reader effect, so Appendix B reports only the later reader studies. Appendix D separates a retrieval intervention from a native-memory outcome. Appendix E compares wrong-question hits with three nulls: uniform (combinatorial) chance, turn-weighted chance, and matched placebo gold.

Construction artifacts, hypothesis-only prediction, partial-input baselines, shortcut learning, adversarial filtering, and contrast sets have an established literature \citep{Gururangan2018AnnotationArtifacts, Poliak2018HypothesisOnly, KaushikLipton2018ReadingComprehension, Geirhos2020ShortcutLearning, Zellers2018SWAG, LeBras2020AFLite, Gardner2020ContrastSets}; §2 cites the work on evaluator agreement and evaluation variance. Our claim applies these measurement principles to memory benchmarks, with explicit retrieval, null-policy, and replica controls. It is not the invention of artifact auditing, nor evidence that every artifact changes an end-to-end system. \citet{Huang2026RetentionSelection} shows that retrieval with uninformative queries can exploit distractor markers and latent type structure, and separates selection gains from unequal history access. Huang's setting is a small streaming environment, unlike the long-history benchmarks audited here; we apply related diagnostic principles to the annotated evidence units of existing long-history benchmarks, with benchmark-specific nulls and downstream tests. Training-initialization variance and same-model decoding replicas are different sources of variability, so evidence on the former says nothing about the magnitude of the latter.

\section{B. Reader studies}\label{b.-reader-studies}

Every arm of a STALE reader ladder supplies the full history and the question. Retrieval pointers are built from the question; the artifact pointer is built from the character U+2019, which identifies STALE's gold pair. Every pointer note also carries a staleness instruction, so the pointers confound where to look with what to do. The arms are interventions, not cue discovery by an unmodified memory system. The bge-small-en-v1.5 citation \citep{Xiao2023CPack} identifies the encoder's model family; the checkpoint revision that ran was not recorded. Appendix K.3 gives the retrieval settings, and Appendix K.5 the reader-side texts.

B.1 reports the original ladder (S10b), B.2 the three-reader ladder (S10c), and B.3 the de-cue test (S18). B.4 reports the four-vendor ladder (S19), which adds two-session retrieval pointers with the artifact pointer's note and four readers from four vendors. B.5 reports S19's separately registered Gemini 3.8 Flash successor run (S19b), which completes S19's test families.

\subsection{B.1. S10b: the original ladder and its exposure}\label{b.1.-s10b-the-original-ladder-and-its-exposure}

\textbf{Protocol card.} S10b tested 100 STALE scenarios, three probes each, with GLM-5.3-Flash as reader and binding judge. The binding judge was changed after development validation: S10's binding judge, Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite, had failed its gate, and a rule fixed after S10's development agreement values were known, but before any S10b test output, chose GLM-5.3-Flash. Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite served as secondary judge and also read 26 scenarios (78 probes) in four arms (plain, generic instruction, hybrid-5, and artifact pointer). This nonrandom subset consists of the first 26 of the 30 planned scenarios, because a budget stop ended the second-reader run there. The LoCoMo-Plus part added 161 items from four host conversations. The 100 STALE test scenarios are positions 41 to 140 when STALE's 400 scenarios are sorted by a salted SHA-256 hash of their ids; Appendix K.1 gives the rule and the other samples. All STALE inputs had been exposed to the artifact analysis, and ten test scenarios also had reader outcomes from an earlier pilot, so excluding those ten does not create a pristine split.

\textbf{Outcome.} The artifact pointer's accuracy exceeds hybrid-5's by +12.0 points (95\% scenario-cluster interval {[}+8.0, +16.0{]}; Holm-adjusted \(p = 5.7 \times 10^{-10}\)). This meets the registered rule: an estimate of at least +5 points, a lower bound above 0, and a Holm-adjusted exact McNemar p below .05. The effect differs by scenario type: +4.7 {[}+1.3, +8.7{]} in co-referential and +19.3 {[}+12.7, +26.0{]} in propagated scenarios. Excluding the ten scenarios with pilot outcomes gives +13.3 {[}+9.3, +17.8{]} on the other 90. The study's report calls the retrieval pointers typography-free. A post hoc token check, which was not repeated, supports this only for the per-turn retrieval path, not for the session-joined path that built the pointers, and it does not isolate any effect of typography (Appendix H.1). Table B1.1 defines the arms and gives their accuracies.

The generic instruction's gain comes mainly from false-premise probes, whose accuracy rises from 28.0\% under plain to 79.0\%. The placebo instruction is not inert: it lowers false-premise accuracy to 22.0\%. The artifact pointer names two sessions and hybrid-5 names five, so their contrast also differs in count and note length (§3.4). S10b's dense retriever had no reader arm; on the test split, it named both gold sessions in 74\% of probes, against 61\% for hybrid-5.

On LoCoMo-Plus, the registered hint raised full-context accuracy by +6.2 points over the official prompt (four-host bootstrap interval {[}+1.2, +11.2{]}; t interval over the four hosts {[}−3.2, +15.7{]}). Its predicted gain of at least +15 points was unreachable, because the official prompt already scored 85.1\%. Under the binding judge, a context of only the cue turns (LoCoMo-Plus's evidence turns) scores +52.8 points above a context of the five turns that the hybrid retriever returns. The hybrid-context accuracy, however, depends heavily on the judge: 47.2\% under the binding GLM-5.3-Flash judge and 17.4\% under the secondary Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite judge. Neither LoCoMo-Plus comparison is independent of the judge, and neither compares published systems.

\textbf{One-grader human check.} After the LLM regrade (Appendix I.2), the author, who knows the design, graded a disagreement-enriched sample of 50 STALE answers from this ladder, with arm and verdict concealed. The binding judge agreed on 46 (raw agreement 92.0\%, κ .83; design-weighted agreement 99.1\%, κ .97; precision unresolved, Appendix J.2). Its design-weighted pass rate was within 1 point of the author's in every arm group (78.3\% against 77.9\% overall). These point estimates show no leniency signal, but they cannot bound arm-group leniency to a few points: the post hoc, model-based judge-minus-human sensitivity ranges are −14.5 to +18.4 points in the plain group (the plain and placebo-instruction arms) and −4.95 to +23.8 points in the pointer group (the wrong-partner pointer, hybrid-5, and artifact-pointer arms; Appendix J.3). Nor can they bound a judge-error difference between the two arms of the +12.0-point contrast, with five and nine sampled answers. The exposure and confounds above remain.

\subsection{B.2. S10c: conditional robustness on the reused test split}\label{b.2.-s10c-conditional-robustness-on-the-reused-test-split}

\textbf{Protocol card.} S10c reused S10b's 100 test scenarios (300 probes) after the direction of S10b's artifact-minus-hybrid effect was known. The readers were Gemini 3.8 Flash, Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite, and a fresh GLM-5.3-Flash anchor draw; the two Gemini readers share a vendor. Each of the nine arms had one draw per cell, in a fixed order within each scenario (the artifact pointer always followed hybrid-5), under implicit provider caching. Besides plain, the generic instruction, hybrid-5, dense-5 (the top five sessions of the dense encoder alone), the artifact pointer, and the oracle note, three pointer controls use the shared pointer note. NEW and OLD denote the newer and the older gold session. NEW only names NEW alone. OLD + wrong later names OLD and the non-gold session after OLD that lies closest to NEW. All wrong names that same later non-gold session and the earlier non-gold session closest to OLD, so it names no gold session. Pointer identity and note length remain confounded: notes naming one, two, and five sessions contain 35, 46, and 79 tokens.

\(D\) averages the paired probe differences within each scenario and then weights scenarios equally. Each of the four primary contrasts used 10,000 scenario-bootstrap resamples, a sign-flip permutation test with 10,000 draws over scenario means, and Holm adjustment across the four. The Bonferroni per-contrast intervals, at 98.75\% (= 1 − .05/\allowbreak{}4), target nominal 95\% coverage for the family, not exact finite-sample coverage. The tier ``supported'' requires a Holm-adjusted p below .05, a per-contrast lower bound above 0, and \(D \geq 5\) points; ``supported, at least +5'' also requires that lower bound to exceed +5. The registered rule then checks ``reversed'', ``practically equal'' (the per-contrast interval inside the open band (−5, +5)), and ``inconclusive''; no primary contrast reached them. Table B2.1 gives the primary contrasts and Table B2.2 every reader's accuracy by arm.

In an exposure control without history (30 test scenarios, one probe each), accuracy falls to 3.3--6.7\%. This is consistent with answers that depend on the supplied history, but it does not show that pretraining contamination is absent.

All eight secondary contrasts pass Holm adjustment at .05; they carry no effect-size tiers. For Gemini 3.8 Flash, the generic instruction recovers 79\% of the oracle-minus-plain gap (95\% interval {[}69, 90{]}; descriptive), more than either retrieval pointer recovers; both pointers are therefore weak comparators for that reader. The exposure sensitivities keep the sign of every primary effect on the 90 scenarios without pilot outcomes and on the 74 that S10b's Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite reader had not read; neither subset is pristine. One descriptive GLM-5.3-Flash contrast is incomplete: OLD + wrong later minus All wrong, because OLD + wrong later has 297 of 300 probes. Its complete-case estimate on 99 scenarios is +7.07 points. Its bounds for the three missing probes, on the planned denominator of 300 probes, are {[}+6.33, +7.33{]} points; they are not a confidence interval. The registered escalation rule was not triggered: it would have added a further reader only if Gemini 3.8 Flash's premium over hybrid-5 had fallen below +5 points while GLM-5.3-Flash's did not, or had taken the opposite sign.

\textbf{Validation and recomputation.} The binding judge agreed with the adjudicator labels on 82 of 87 items from 59 scenarios (agreement .943, κ .846), which passes the registered gate. The adjudicator was a fresh Claude Opus 5.5 instance (\nolinkurl{claude-opus-5-5}) that saw no arm, reader, item id, or verdict. All three items that the judge passed and the adjudicator failed are Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite answers, and none of the five disagreements involves the artifact pointer. Because the packet is clustered and LLM-labeled, it is not human validation. The secondary Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite judge agreed with the adjudicator on 78 of 87 items (κ .722); this is descriptive. According to the recorded chronology, a post-lock repair of the analysis code (a recorded amendment; Appendix I.2) was signed before the first test call. Separately, the test reads paused for about 13 hours while the host machine slept. The five reads interrupted by the pause (one Gemini 3.8 Flash and four GLM-5.3-Flash reads) timed out and were re-sent under the registered recovery rule; no read was lost, and none was repeated outside that rule. Two separate recomputations from the saved outputs reproduced the inference and validation arithmetic. They could not verify from the files the adjudicator's isolation, the semantic prompt audit, the access chronology, or the billing (Appendix I.4).

\subsection{B.3. S18: jointly removing STALE's typographic and clock-time cues}\label{b.3.-s18-jointly-removing-stales-typographic-and-clock-time-cues}

S18, the de-cue test of §6.2, is the one reader study in this appendix that removes cues instead of adding a pointer. It asks a narrow question about one reader on the reused S10c test split, so it measures conditional robustness, not fresh confirmation. Its registered tier is ``practically equal''. Card B3 gives the protocol.

\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Question & Is a plain full-history reader's accuracy different when STALE's typographic and clock-time cues are removed together, with everything else fixed? \\
Registration and freeze & A preregistration plus a binding protocol amendment (registered as an annex, which takes precedence where the two differ), hash-locked together on 2026-10-04 before any S18 model call. Known before registration: S10c's tables (Gemini 3.8 Flash plain 66.0\% under the GLM-5.3-Flash judge), S10b's GLM-5.3-Flash reader at 63.7\% (plain) against 63.3\% (unsanitized plain), and the results of S17 Parts B and B2 (Appendix C.4) \\
Population & The 100 scenarios of the S10c test split (identical to S10b's), with three probes each: state resolution (SR), premise resistance (PR, the false-premise probe), and implicit policy adaptation (IPA); 300 probes \\
Reader & Gemini 3.8 Flash with no thinking level sent, that is, at the model's default level, which S19's registration records as ``medium'' (Appendix B.4.7); temperature 1.0; up to 16,384 output tokens; concurrency 1. These are S10c's Gemini 3.8 Flash settings. Plain prompt: the official STALE reader system prompt, the history, then the probe; no note \\
Layout (both arms) & The user-only rendering of S10 and S10c: all 50 sessions in order, user turns only, one header per session. The 29,661 assistant turns of the 100 test scenarios are removed. This is not the official STALE evaluation layout \\
RAW & The user-only layout with STALE's own typography and the full \nolinkurl{YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM} stamp in every session header \\
DECUE & The registered de-cue of RAW: delete the clock time from each session header, then apply S10's typographic normalizer. DECUE equals S10c's plain prompt byte for byte (300 of 300 by SHA-256) \\
What DECUE keeps & The probe (S10c's normalized text, identical in both arms), the system prompt, session order and numbering, dates, roles, line structure, and every word. Length, turn-count, content, and markdown cues remain in both arms \\
Draws and order & Two independent draws per probe per arm: 1,200 reads. A probe's four calls are consecutive, in the order A1 B1 B2 A2, where A is the arm sent first in draw 1, B the other arm, and the digit the draw. A hash rank decides which arm goes first in draw 1 (RAW for 150 probes, DECUE for 150); draw 2 reverses the order. Both arms have the same mean planned position, 599.5 \\
Judge & GPT-6.1 Sol at medium reasoning, up to 16,384 output tokens, with S10c's STALE binding-judge prompt; one judge call per scenario, arm, and draw: 400 calls. Validity is inherited from S17 Part B (87 items: agreement .966, κ .910; plain-arm subset of nine items: agreement .889, κ .780) and was not retested on S18's answers \\
Estimand & \(D = \mathrm{acc}(\mathrm{RAW}) - \mathrm{acc}(\mathrm{DECUE})\), in points, with S10c's estimator: the mean over scenarios of each scenario's mean paired probe difference \\
Per-draw rules & A judged draw counts its binary verdict. A draw whose read exhausted its allowed attempts counts 0. A draw whose judge call is missing or unsent is missing. A probe's value is the mean over its nonmissing draws (0, 1/\allowbreak{}2, or 1). A complete-case sensitivity treats exhausted draws as missing \\
Inference & A 10,000-resample scenario-bootstrap 95\% interval (seed 2202); a two-sided seeded sign-flip test on scenario means with 10,000 draws, \(p = (1 + e)/10{,}001\), where \(e\) is the number of draws at least as extreme as the observed \(D\). Tier comparisons use exact rationals \\
Tiers (first that holds) & ``native reliance supported'': p \textless{} .05, lower bound \textgreater{} 0, and \(D \geq 5\). ``small positive'': p \textless{} .05, lower bound \textgreater{} 0, and \(0 < D < 5\). ``reversed'': p \textless{} .05, upper bound \textless{} 0, and \(D < 0\). ``practically equal'': the 95\% interval lies inside the closed band {[}−5, +5{]}. ``inconclusive'': otherwise. Health checks come first: an integrity failure yields the status UNRESOLVED, and a share of valid reads or of valid judge calls below 98\% yields the status INCOMPLETE \\
Judge anchor (registered as EP-19) & Descriptive and run first. GPT-6.1 Sol judges S10c's existing 300 Gemini 3.8 Flash plain answers in 100 judge calls, whose prompts equal S10c's GLM-5.3-Flash judge prompts by SHA-256. The anchor gives a same-judge drift check and judge--judge agreement. It is outside the primary health and completeness rules \\
\end{longtable}

\endgroup

RAW carries the cues because the literal design was degenerate: S10c's plain prompt is already de-cued, so applying the de-cue to it changed 0 of 300 prompts. The registered design therefore restores STALE's own typography and clock times in RAW and keeps S10c's plain prompt as DECUE. The probe itself is not de-cued. Because RAW restores STALE's typography and clock times, RAW prompts are slightly longer than DECUE prompts: 20,141 against 19,840 mean input tokens, about 1.5\%.

\subsubsection{B.3.2. Closure and populations}\label{b.3.2.-closure-and-populations}

The pipeline ran the verification, anchor, reading, judging, and analysis steps in the registered order, and every step completed without error. Reading and judging both ended before the analysis started. No stop was recorded, and no planned read or judge call was pending at analysis time. The post-freeze, reporting-only amendment makes a verdict final only under these conditions, so the registered verdict is final. Table B3.1 gives the acquisition counts and Table B3.2 the endpoint counts.

\endgroup

The binding p is the seeded Monte Carlo value. The stored output marks exact enumeration as not applicable, because the code enumerates sign vectors only when at most 20 scenario differences are nonzero; here 31 are. A separate recomputation enumerated all \(2^{31}\) sign vectors and obtained .361, and a second recomputation confirmed this value by integer convolution (193,838,655/\allowbreak{}536,870,912 ≈ .36105). The tier is the same under either value, since both are far above .05. In this run, \(D\) and both bounds happen to fall on a 1/\allowbreak{}12-point grid; that is not true in general, so the tier rests on the exact rational comparisons.

In the form that the reporting-only amendment requires, the verdict is as follows. On the reused S10c test split, in the user-only rendering of S10 and S10c (not the official STALE evaluation layout), and with STALE's typographic and clock-time cues removed jointly (clock time is also legitimate template content), accuracy under the two prompts was practically equal within the registered ±5-point margin for one plain reader, Gemini 3.8 Flash. The estimate is \(D\) = +1.33 points, with a 95\% interval of {[}−1.17, +3.83{]} inside the closed band {[}−5, +5{]}. The upper bound means that a benefit from the two cues of up to about 4 points remains compatible with the data.

\textbf{Realized precision.} The interval's half-width is about 2.5 points. The planning estimates for two draws were 3.1 points, if all per-scenario variance were draw noise, and 3.5 points, if about a quarter of it were stable. The planning probabilities of a ``practically equal'' verdict at a true \(D\) of 0 (.76 and .60) were illustrations under those assumptions, not bounds. Under a stable-variance fraction of one half, the same planning model gives about .46.

Two of the nine strata have a nominal p below .05: T2 and the RAW-first stratum. T2's interval excludes 0, while T1's point estimate has the opposite sign (propagated scenarios +4.00 {[}+1.3, +7.0{]}, co-referential −1.33 {[}−5.0, +2.7{]}). This is a pattern for a later registered test, not a finding of S18. The split into T1 and T2 rests on benchmark metadata that neither the study report nor the recomputations rederived; it is internally consistent (the T1 and T2 values average to the overall \(D\) and accuracies).

The two draw-1 first-arm strata are descriptive probe subsets, not an order contrast. They use the two-draw per-probe values, and draw 2 reverses the order, so every probe in both strata had each arm first once. A common additive first-versus-second effect cancels in the planned ABBA schedule, subject to the one realized-order exception below and to drift. Their sign difference is therefore a difference between two hash-assigned probe subsets. No interaction test was registered.

The two recorded answers differed byte for byte on every probe (0 of 300 identical pairs in each arm); this does not establish statistical independence of reader or judge errors. These are within-arm, draw-to-draw discordances. The planning noise anchor, S10b's 13.7\%, is a between-arm discordance (plain against unsanitized plain) with a different reader and judge, so the two are not directly comparable.

\textbf{Position descriptives (post hoc, not registered).} Pooled over both draws and grouped by planned position within a draw's adjacent pair, RAW scores 65.7\% when sent first and 63.3\% when sent second; DECUE scores 63.0\% and 63.3\% (300 draws per cell). By realized send time, RAW scores 65.8\% first (n = 301) and 63.2\% second (n = 299); DECUE scores 62.9\% first (n = 299) and 63.5\% second (n = 301). The gaps are at most about 2.6 points, and none is a test. The order design balances mean planned position, not arbitrary drift or retry effects. Each arm went first exactly once in 300 of 300 probes as planned and in 299 of 300 as realized: one retried read moved one probe's DECUE draw-1 call to the end of its block.

\textbf{Exploratory.} On the 97 scenarios whose evidence pair shares a clock time, \(D\) = +1.72 points, 95\% interval {[}−0.69, +4.12{]}. The other subset has three scenarios and is not evaluable, since it has fewer than 10. The per-scenario difference has a Pearson correlation of .166 with the typographic edit count and of .034 with RAW history length.
