Retriever & BM25 Okapi \citep{RobertsonZaragoza2009BM25} plus bge-small dense retrieval \citep{Xiao2023CPack}, fused by RRF \citep{Cormack2009RRF} with constant 60; no cross-encoder \\
Wrong questions & Ten eligible peers per target, taken in a fixed hash order from a different group; peers with evidence overlap, duplicate questions, or near-duplicate evidence are excluded \\
Item outcome & The item's wrong-question hit rate (QI) is its mean any-gold@5 hit over those ten questions \\
Chance controls & Exact uniform sampling; Plackett--Luce sampling weighted by nonempty scoring turns; independent random turn scores followed by the same maximum pooling (the random-score null) \\
Matched placebo & 20 non-gold sets matched within the haystack on user-character decile, turn-count decile, and position tercile; each set is evaluated on the same wrong-question rankings \\
Family estimands & P: mean item CE against turn-weighted chance; S: mean item CE against matched placebo on supported items \\
Scale and margins & \(\mathrm{CE} = (\mathrm{QI} - \mathrm{null})/(1 - \mathrm{null})\); practical threshold .05; equivalence margin ±.03 \\
Inference & The wider of a 10,000-draw two-way cluster bootstrap (target group × peer group) and a CR1 cluster-robust t interval; separate Holm families at .05 \\
\end{longtable}

\endgroup

As in the main text (§3.3), this appendix calls the effect the chance-corrected excess (CE). The atlas's internal report labels this quantity κ; it is not Cohen's κ.

The normalization occurs within each item before averaging, so a displayed difference between two benchmark-wide percentages is not itself the registered CE. BEAM's family estimands weight its three length splits equally, whereas the atlas percentages keep the stored item weights. This distinction applies to both the CE and the family point contrasts, so the atlas percentages cannot replace the family estimates. Family-S intervals condition on each item's observed placebo denominator and therefore do not represent the full uncertainty of a ratio estimate.

The admission rule (D1) first determines whether MemOps-round enters either family. The cue-dominance check (K3) then labels a row cue-dominated if any registered query-blind feature reaches a direction-free single-feature AUROC (within-haystack) of .90, averaged over items. A cue-dominated primary row still shows its tier, but only its placebo estimand may be interpreted. A family-S row needs common support for at least 80\% of its primary, non-starved items (items with ten eligible peers). A row that fails support keeps its family slot with p = 1 and no tier. The registration fixed these rules before any retrieval ran. The eight registered single-feature priors rank a haystack's units by characters, turns, mean turn length, or position, each in both directions (Appendix K.4). The best of the eight is a selected maximum and is descriptive only.

\subsection{E.2. Complete atlas at hybrid any-gold@5}\label{e.2.-complete-atlas-at-hybrid-any-gold5}

We fixed the primary target selection before any retrieval ran. For HaluMem we excluded 369 items whose prefix haystacks have at most ten nonempty units and applied a cap of 60 targets per user; for BEAM we excluded 72 targets from four nonmonotonic conversations; for MemOps we selected one item per generated file; and for LoCoMo we capped targets at 120 per conversation. Table E1 gives the resulting target counts. The MemOps sample spans 97 registered groups formed by shared gold hosts, while LoCoMo and LoCoMo-Plus each span ten host conversations. These sampling and unit choices are ours, so differences between atlas rows are not comparisons of benchmark validity.

The two sensitivity rows keep the target sample of a primary row. LoCoMo /\allowbreak{} citation sensitivity adds to the peer pool the 55 citation-flagged items that we excluded from LoCoMo's primary population. It differs from a larger sensitivity analysis that also adds these 55 items as targets (1,213 items; CE over turn-weighted chance .012 {[}−.002, .028{]}). HaluMem-Medium /\allowbreak{} full-history session keeps the prefix row's targets but uses each target's full history as the haystack. We also excluded BEAM's targets from the four nonmonotonic conversations from its primary population; they enter only a sensitivity analysis, which gives a CE over turn-weighted chance of −.005 {[}−.013, .004{]} on 1,609 items, against −.004 for the primary population.

Because MemOps-round was not admitted, both families have two members, and Holm rejects neither superiority test (Tables E3--E5). In family P, no item was excluded for a degenerate null, incompleteness, or lack of support; exclusion for lack of support applies only to family S, where it removes 19 BEAM items. HaluMem's supported items include none with an undefined CE. No bootstrap replicate was dropped. BEAM's equal split weights are those of the registered population and were not recomputed after exclusions. With these weights, its family point contrast is −0.34 points, whereas the difference of the item-weighted means is about −0.40 points.

\end{longtable}

\endgroup

\subsection{E.4. Interpretation across populations}\label{e.4.-interpretation-across-populations}

BEAM supports the narrow registered equivalence statement for this retriever stack and these units on both nulls. That is a bound within the specified margin, not a certificate that the benchmark lacks every cue. HaluMem-Medium yields no interpretable primary conclusion: position triggers K3, and insufficient placebo support prevents a full-population secondary conclusion. Gold occupies the last unit for 868 of its 1,200 prefix items. On the same items' full histories, the CE over turn-weighted chance is −.005 {[}−.015, .006{]} and placebo support is 453 of 1,200 items; the maximum single-feature AUROC there is .561, below the K3 threshold. That sensitivity analysis remains descriptive.

MemOps-round's supported placebo contrast is descriptively CE = .099 {[}.063, .134{]} on 390 of 403 items, outside both confirmatory families. Its groups connect shared gold hosts, while reused non-gold hosts still enter rankings across groups, so the interval may be too narrow. For MemOps-session, the only permitted statement is that the inserted gold conversations are longer; its longest-first prior reaches 100.00\% (Table E2). This favorable query-blind outcome was known before the study was frozen.

The remaining intervals in this subsection are descriptive 95\% intervals (the wider of the bootstrap and CR1 intervals), without confirmatory tiers. LoCoMo gives CE = .008 {[}−.014, .030{]} over turn-weighted chance, and LoCoMo-Plus gives .013 {[}.004, .023{]}. LoCoMo's placebo support is only 133 of 1,158 items, so its matched comparison cannot describe the full population. STALE is cue-dominated: its non-ASCII and typographic-marker features both reach a single-feature AUROC of 1.000. Its CE over turn-weighted chance, .311 {[}.283, .339{]}, is reported but not interpreted, and the matched comparison applies only to 756 of its 1,200 items. On that subset, QI is 41.18\% against a placebo rate of 16.62\%, a CE of .272 {[}.231, .314{]}. No conclusion extends from that comparison to the unsupported items. No placebo estimand reaches the registered POSITIVE tier that a construction-cue claim would require.

\textbf{Selection null and design power.} In a post hoc check, the selected best prior exceeds a conservative bound on its selection null for STALE, the HaluMem prefix haystacks, both MemOps rows, LoCoMo, and LoCoMo-Plus, but not for BEAM or LongMemEval-S. The bound assumes independent items and ignores shared haystacks, so it is a descriptive comparison, not calibrated inference. By design, BEAM could often resolve equivalence (an EQUIVALENT verdict in 75--99\% of simulated runs at a true CE of 0), while HaluMem-Medium, with 20 user clusters, almost never could (at most 10.6\%).

\textbf{Fair nulls on LongMemEval-S.} The legacy reference and the current atlas row differ in both peer rules and retrieval path. The legacy gap between QI and legacy uniform chance satisfies the following approximate identity, in points:

\(5.038027 \approx 1.872340 + 2.134398 + 0.981927 + 0.049361\)

The four terms are, in order: legacy QI minus current QI (a reduction under the combined change of peer rules and retrieval path); current turn-weighted minus current uniform chance; current QI minus current turn-weighted chance; and current minus legacy uniform chance. This is bookkeeping between stored aggregates, not a causal decomposition. A path-only control exists for STALE but not for LongMemEval-S, so the first term cannot be allocated to the peer rules alone. The current residual corresponds to CE = .011 {[}−.017, .041{]}, so positive effects up to the upper bound remain compatible with this exploratory interval. The placebo comparison uses only 176 of 470 items (supported QI 19.77\%, placebo 22.57\%) and supports no conclusion about the full row.

\subsection{E.5. Exposure, deviations, and recomputation}\label{e.5.-exposure-deviations-and-recomputation}

The registration disclosed query-blind summaries of MemOps and HaluMem seen before the freeze, including HaluMem's last-unit structure. All decision rules were fixed before any new row was retrieved. Registered departures from the design include the MemOps grouping rule, the CR1 interval of the item mean, power calculations run after the freeze as a design diagnostic, and the inclusion of sensitivity-only items in the peer pool. The placebo interval conditions on the observed placebo denominators, and interval containment replaced a target-only TOST as the equivalence rule. The atlas figure (Figure 2 of the main text) was drawn after the run from the stored aggregates; its two panels separate full-population and supported-subset comparisons. The waiting time for compute slots was not recorded per job. None of these reporting and recording qualifications changes the tables above.

A recomputation from the saved outputs reconstructed the family estimates, the analytical inference, the support masks, and the atlas values. Its bootstrap check used 750 draws instead of reproducing the registered 10,000-draw endpoints, and the verdicts were checked separately against the stored full-draw intervals. It did not rerun the query-blind feature extraction, the Monte Carlo null generation, the corpus conversion, or the retrieval. Agreement among the stored outputs shows that they are numerically consistent, not when or in what order the steps ran. The interpretation in E.4 is limited to this retriever stack, these unit definitions, and these registered populations. It neither measures a memory system's reliance on cues nor quantifies inflation of published scores.

\section{F. LLM regrade: estimators, ambiguity, and uncertainty}\label{f.-llm-regrade-estimators-ambiguity-and-uncertainty}

\subsection{F.1. Packet and adjudicators}\label{f.1.-packet-and-adjudicators}

\textbf{Protocol card.} The packet sampled 100 answers that the original LLM judges had graded, oversampling judge disagreements: 50 answers of the S10b STALE ladder (claim C2) and 50 S11 replica answers (claim C3). Adjudicator G1 (Claude Sonnet 5.5) graded the items in five batches of 20, one context per batch. Adjudicators G2 (Gemini 3.8 Flash, thinking level high) and G3 (GPT-6.1 Sol, reasoning effort medium) graded each item in a separate call. Appendix K gives the adjudicators' instructions (K.6) and settings (K.7). G1 is a different model of the same family as Claude Opus 5.5, the earlier adjudicator of the S10c, S11b, and S11c gates. G2 shares its vendor with Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite, the secondary STALE judge, which also read some C2 answers. G3 shares a vendor with no judge or reader of this packet. All three adjudicators received the same rubrics, so their agreement is not evidence of independent judgment, and their wrapper prompts and recorded reasoning also differed. There is no human ground truth; Appendix J gives the author's labels on the same packet, from one informed grader.

In the pointer arm group of claim C2, adjudicator G3's design-weighted pass rate is 74.1\%, against 86.6\% for the GLM-5.3-Flash judge. Two of the 17 sampled pointer items, both hybrid-5 answers, produce this 12.4-point gap; one of them, in a high-weight stratum, contributes about 12.2 points. In the other arm groups the net difference is zero, because G3's disagreements with the judge offset each other. This is evidence from a sparse stratum, not established leniency of the judge toward pointer arms. S17-B2 (Appendix C.4) rejudged pointer answers from a different population and has no non-pointer controls. On both items, the author, as human grader, sided with the GLM-5.3-Flash judge (post hoc; Appendix J.5).

\subsection{F.3. Conditional flip confirmation and the estimand E6 erratum}\label{f.3.-conditional-flip-confirmation-and-the-estimand-e6-erratum}

Estimand E5 sampled one judge-disagreeing replica pair (a flip) from each of 15 unstable questions (part P); estimand E6 added 10 random pairs (part R), all of which were judge-agreeing. Let \(d\) be the judge's label disagreement on the frame, in percent. Let \(q_1\) be the probability that an adjudicator also finds a disagreement when the judge's labels disagree, and \(q_0\) that probability when they agree. Full-frame adjudicator disagreement is then \(d q_1 + (100 - d) q_0\), in percent. Because no part-P pair is reversed, estimand E5 equals \(q_1\) here; it identifies neither full-frame disagreement nor the direction of the judge's bias.

The frozen plan specifies estimand E6 as Horvitz--Thompson (HT) totals over the 30,981-pair frame, whereas the locked code normalizes within the two domains of 5,110 judge-disagreeing and 25,871 judge-agreeing pairs. Because the sampled weight sums (5,546.8 and 31,140) differ from these domain sizes, the two estimators give different values. This is a conflict between two frozen specifications, not evidence of tuning after the grades. Unless the unresolved grades receive an assumption, the HT values under exclusion are observed contributions, not full-frame estimates.

The stored estimand E6 equals 8.25 points, the judge's own \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\) on this frame, times the decided share of confirmed disagreement in part P, because the sampled part-R term is zero. Its bootstrap keeps that zero: adjudicator G3's interval, {[}4.06, 7.73{]}, varies only the part-P share. The ceiling of 8.25 therefore depends on the observed labels and is not a population bound. The frame also differs from that of S11's \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\) of 8.12 points on 671 questions, so \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) is neither a corrected S11 gap nor evidence of the direction of the original judge's bias. Replacing the zero-event hidden-disagreement component by its one-sided 95\% bound would give \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) values of 16.9--18.5 points across adjudicators G1--G3, a conditional sensitivity rather than a joint confidence bound. Appendix J.4 gives the values of estimands E5 and E6 from the author's human grade, which has the same zero-event structure (0 of 10 random pairs disagreeing).

\subsection{F.4. Precision and computational provenance}\label{f.4.-precision-and-computational-provenance}

Most strata have only two to seven determinate items, often with no observed disagreement, so empirical bootstrap intervals can degenerate. A separate post hoc model treats the determinate items within each stratum as independent binomial draws of equal weight and the ambiguous labels as missing at random; it ignores the dependence within question pairs and the unequal S11 weights. Its grouped point equals the C2 primary statistic but is a different statistic for C3.

The consensus view keeps the 83 items on which adjudicators G1 and G2 give the same determinate grade and drops the other 17. Selecting on agreement can move agreement with the original judge in either direction, and it does not show that the dropped items are harder. In strata where the original judge's label is constant, κ is undefined under the plan, including the cells where the output prints zero.

Two separate recomputations from the saved outputs reproduced the arithmetic for adjudicator G3. The estimator, domain, and precision corrections described above are kept here. The runtime model identities, reasoning effort, costs, isolation, and access history rest on the run records alone and were not recomputed. Arithmetic agreement neither certifies semantic correctness nor reopens S11c's failed judge gate.

\section{G. Null-memory policies and the failed forgetting closure}\label{g.-null-memory-policies-and-the-failed-forgetting-closure}

\subsection{G.1. Null-memory policies and scoring boundaries}\label{g.1.-null-memory-policies-and-scoring-boundaries}

The null-policy study (S1) examined whether a public metric rewards an operation that discards the intended memory utility. Its protocol distinguishes the public forgetting score from a stricter requirement that the facts to be kept are preserved. The null-memory policies are diagnostic baselines and analytic scoring checks, not proposed memory systems. ForgetEval's public score \citep{Yang2026ForgetEval} rewards erasing targeted material without necessarily checking that unrelated material is preserved, so the study reports both objectives and keeps the failed strict criterion.

Strict keep-set derivability is a separate coverage check: 286 of 385 adversarial items (74.3\%) and all 1,000 template items have a derivable strict keep set. These are coverage rates, not policy successes, and the adversarial coverage misses the registered 80\% threshold. The retrospective template-preservation check turns the wipe policy's 750 public passes into zero when the construction-derived keep labels are enforced. This is a diagnostic use of those labels, not a newly registered external evaluation. An always-abstain policy scores 446 of 1,986 LoCoMo questions (22.5\%) under the released scorer, if it receives no credit on any other question; this is arithmetic on released numbers, not a new judge run. On the single-hop FactConsolidation task from MemoryAgentBench \citep{Hu2026MemoryAgentBench} at 262k tokens, BM25 retrieval with a keep-latest rule contains the answer in 75 of 100 cases. This is an oracle-reader substring surrogate, not the accuracy of a model.

The conversation-cluster sensitivity intervals are {[}+5.59, +8.73{]} and {[}+2.28, +3.88{]} for the Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite reader, and {[}−0.64, +2.10{]} and {[}+0.78, +1.51{]} for the GLM-5.3-Flash reader. These diagnostics use 692 (Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite reader) and 691 (GLM-5.3-Flash reader) test questions in four conversations. Because the category-conditioned gain is +7.21 points with the Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite reader but +0.50 with GLM-5.3-Flash, harness effects of category-conditioned prompting depend on the reader; this rests on two reader stacks only. The four test conversations and the gold and category information that the options use rule out interpreting the table as a general ranking of published memory systems. The failure of the strict preservation criterion remains the registered outcome, despite the large public scores of the null policies. In a post hoc check, the GLM-5.3-Flash best of three often drew on fewer than three distinct samples, because the model proxy returned some redraws as copies (Appendix H.1). Its +1.15 therefore does not come from three independent draws, and the direction of the resulting bias is not identified. The Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite redraws were regenerated, but in 13.8\% of that reader's test rows the second judge draw copied an earlier draw, which can raise the best of three slightly.

\subsection{G.2. Forgetting closure: a failed practical target}\label{g.2.-forgetting-closure-a-failed-practical-target}

The forgetting-closure study (S14) asked whether identifying linked information can reduce collateral loss while meeting a prescribed forgetting objective. Collateral is the number of non-revealing comments deleted divided by the store size, averaged over requests. Its exploratory starting point was already known during design. With the best zero-shot similarity ranker at top five, 65.0\% {[}59.3, 70.5{]} of 638 nonempty test requests from 150 authors kept at least one revealing comment outside the top five, and closing every request by ranking depth cost a collateral of 38.6\% {[}35.5, 41.6{]}. These values motivated the study but are not a fresh confirmation.

The registered practical target is not supported. At nominal \(\alpha = .10\), the GLM-5.3-Flash scan's collateral is 45.7\% {[}42.9, 48.8{]}, against 50.1\% for the logistic-regression comparator, a difference of −4.5 points {[}−7.6, −1.1{]}. When calibration uncertainty is also resampled, the difference is −4.4 points {[}−12.1, +2.3{]}. Coverage is 94.0\%, but the scan misses the required collateral ceiling of 30\% and the improvement margin of 11 points. The macro AUROC for ranking revealing comments is .887 against .859, a difference of +.029 {[}+.015, +.047{]}: its point estimate exceeds the .02 margin, but its lower confidence bound does not.

The implemented nonincreasing envelope selected a full wipe, whose collateral is 86.7\%, against 45.7\% for label closure, a ratio of 1.90. This result does not establish that every interior threshold or every alternative risk-control construction fails. The semantic probe also finds that, after the labeled revealing comments are deleted, six of eight tested attributes remain at least 10 points above their prior. Label-level closure therefore does not establish semantic forgetting in this experiment, and the negative result supplies no supported memory method.

\section{H. Post hoc checks, provenance, and release boundaries}\label{h.-post-hoc-checks-provenance-and-release-boundaries}

\subsection{H.1. Descriptive follow-ups and their corrections}\label{h.1.-descriptive-follow-ups-and-their-corrections}

The follow-ups in this section were designed after the registered results were visible, and they leave every registered gate and study verdict unchanged. The study archive records the recomputation of each follow-up from the saved outputs, together with explicit limits on what was recomputed; a recomputation is not a blinded confirmation.

For the replica studies, among answer pairs that were byte-identical and received the same recorded judge prompt, the judge's labels still disagreed in 59 of 5,429 S11 pairs, 13 of 1,850 S11c original-extractor pairs, and 17 of 1,792 S11c alternate-extractor pairs. These conditional disagreement rates are 1.087\%, 0.703\%, and 0.949\%, respectively, and the pairs account for 1.20\%, 0.73\%, and 0.90\% of all disagreeing pair cells in the corresponding analyses. Matches of normalized text answer a different question: normalization can erase distinctions, and token-set overlap does not establish semantic equality. Neither kind of match gives an identified lower bound on judge-caused disagreement.

Setting selected disagreeing pair cells to agreement is a sensitivity analysis at the level of pair cells. Because one replica can receive incompatible labels across different pairs, the operation need not correspond to a realizable relabeled experiment or to a corrected oracle gap. Its intervals condition on the questions of the selected categories, the observed conversations, and the memory stores. The gate simulations (Appendix I.3) give conditional pass fractions under the specified table models, not the probability that a fresh validation packet will pass or that an unknown κ exceeds the threshold. The zero-event hidden-disagreement calculations (F.3) condition on the sampling model of the decided pairs and plug in an estimated disagreement component, so their totals are not joint upper confidence bounds.

The second follow-up recomputed the observed detectability AUROCs (macro within-pool) of Table A1 from the saved outputs, together with the arithmetic of each setting's 50 saved label-permutation null values. Each observed score exceeds all its saved null values (null means .498--.505), which gives the exploratory add-one p-value 1/\allowbreak{}51 = .0196 in every setting. At that resolution no setting can clear a nine-comparison Holm family at .05, so Holm rejects 0 of 9; this is a resolution limit, not evidence for the null.

A later post hoc run refitted each detector 1,000 more times with the same code, arrays, folds, mask, and settings, but with new seeds; the observed AUROCs match the earlier ones exactly. With \(p = (1 + e)/1{,}001\), where \(e\) is the number of exceedances, Holm over the nine settings at .05 rejects all nine. Eight settings have no exceedance among the 1,000 sampled nulls, which gives the minimum reported plus-one p of 1/\allowbreak{}1,001 = .000999. These draws do not resolve a smaller p, and this minimum is not a confidence bound on the underlying exceedance probability. Margins over the largest null are thin in PersonaMem-v2 (8 pools, +.0028) and BEAM 100K (20 pools, +.0084), the two settings with the widest null SDs (.028 and .032); more draws could lift their p above the floor. In LoCoMo (observed AUROC .518), 10 of 1,000 nulls reach the observed value, so p = .011. LoCoMo passes only as the last Holm step, at the full .05 threshold, and would not pass a Bonferroni cut (.0056). Pooling all 1,050 draws gives the same 9 of 9. The nine settings and the classifier were chosen after the data were seen. Holm controls error only within this family, not for how the family was chosen, and a small p shows neither that the classifier measures benchmark quality nor why the labels are detectable.

A separate computational check recomputed every reported number from the saved null values, without refitting. In STALE, the permuted statistic is scored on 273--310 pools in the 50-refit run and on 257--324 in the 1,000-refit run, rather than on the 400 observed pools, because the labels are permuted before the fixed row mask is applied and pools with a single class are omitted. Smaller mismatches occur in LoCoMo-Plus (null 399--401 pools, observed 393) and LongMemEval-S (360--414, observed 379). We report this mismatch; by itself, it does not show that applying the same statistic to the permutations is arithmetically invalid. Null means near .5 under this null model do not show that the pipeline is unbiased.

The STALE feature-tier refits remain fitted-model diagnostics as reported. They remove the typography features, then the punctuation and markup counts, and still reach a detectability AUROC near .999, but the saved follow-up outputs keep only their scalar AUROCs, not new out-of-fold predictions, so these refits were not recomputed. Removing the named typography and punctuation columns also leaves length and lexical quantities that can depend on punctuation. Likewise, the token-survival check concerns literal U+2019 in the checked token paths. Literal U+2019 is absent from S16's normalized per-turn retrieval-token path, but the session-joined pointer path was not rebuilt, and no separate check repeated the tokenization. The check does not compare retrieval outcomes and cannot establish that typography has no causal effect on retrieval.

Selection among eight priors raises a different issue from a single prespecified prior. The checked combinatorial means, selected observed maxima, and analytic upper expressions assume independent item-level random-gold draws, so they do not preserve the dependence from shared haystacks or shared gold. The simulated two-position mean and quantile are Monte Carlo estimates, not guaranteed exact bounds, and no complete joint simulation of the eight priors is available. The power illustration is also conditional. With the rounded store-variance SD of 1.04 points, one independent run per system gives approximate detectable differences of 2.88268 points at 50\% power and 4.12052 points at 80\% power (about 2.9 and 4.1). At the SD's upper interval bound, 1.39, the difference at 80\% power is about 5.5 points. This illustration applies to single-run differences and assumes a known SD and independent, equal-variance normal errors on the fixed population. Pairing changes the variance through covariance, and no general rule about all small leaderboard gaps follows.

A separate cross-arm identity scan of S10c found 11 identical answer pairs across the core arms: nine for GLM-5.3-Flash and one each for Gemini 3.8 Flash and Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite. The hybrid-5 and dense-5 pointers give identical reader prompts on 17 probes per reader, but no whole judge input is identical across the compared arms, so the scan cannot estimate the judge's stability on identical inputs. Each reader has 10,800 available answer pairs, of which 10,783 have different reader prompts; the original GLM-5.3-Flash denominator of 10,775 mixed the masks for reader probes and judge calls. In S17, selecting the first or the last valid record picks the same physical record in all 900 judge calls. Equal input hashes do not certify semantic equivalence. The next paragraph explains the nine GLM-5.3-Flash pairs.

\textbf{Proxy copies of identical requests (post hoc, descriptive).} A later check of the call log found that the shared proxy serving GLM-5.3-Flash returned a copy when an identical request body was sent again shortly after the first call had finished. Of 4,650 such repeats sent within a minute, 4,530 (97.4\%) came back in under 500 ms with the earlier token counts. Repeats sent while the first call was still running were regenerated, apart from 4.4\%. These exceptions followed an earlier finished identical call or overlapped their source by a median of 0.09 s, within the noise of the estimated send time. Gemini and OpenAI calls went directly to their providers and showed no copy pattern. Copies are inferred from latency and token equality, because the log holds no response text; only the S10c cases were also checked against answer hashes. The findings by study follow.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \emph{S11 replicas.} Each replica sent its own distinct requests: an output-token cap that was never reached differed between replicas, so no two replicas shared a request. Copies touched only the second copies of 11 twin-question pairs (identical questions within one conversation) and a few design probes. Dropping every cell that touches a copy moves label disagreement from 16.25\% to 16.32\% (29,979 of 30,195 cells kept). It moves the flip rate on byte-identical answer pairs from 59 of 5,429 (1.087\%) to 59 of 5,353 (1.102\%). No flip pair was an identical request, and S11c had no copies.
\item
  \emph{S10c cross-arm duplicates.} All nine identical GLM-5.3-Flash answer pairs above were proxy copies: the dense-5 request repeated the hybrid-5 request and received its answer. On the eight other probes with identical requests, the request was regenerated and the answer differed every time. The nine identical pairs therefore reflect copying, not determinism at temperature 0. On those nine probes, the descriptive GLM-5.3-Flash dense-5 cell of the Appendix B.2 accuracy table repeats the hybrid-5 answer; the registered contrasts, which concern the Gemini readers, are not involved.
\item
  \emph{S1 redraws.} The GLM-5.3-Flash reader's redraws, which the S1 design and report describe as independent, were partly copies: among 1,676 complete best-of-three test cells, 29.8\% had one real generation and 23.4\% had two. The second judge draw copied an earlier draw in 13.8\% (Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite reader) and 6.6\% (GLM-5.3-Flash reader) of test rows. The Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite redraws were regenerated. S1's registered verdict is unchanged.
\item
  \emph{S15 recoveries.} In S15's paraphrase build, at the log snapshot used for this check (taken while S15 was still running), 1,230 planned calls had an empty first answer. Their 2,460 identical recovery calls were copies in 2,459 cases, so no call was recovered by an identical retry.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{H.2. Exposure, deviations, and the scope of recomputations}\label{h.2.-exposure-deviations-and-the-scope-of-recomputations}

Input and outcome exposure are distinct from inferential decisions. Later reader comparisons on the reused STALE test split are conditional robustness checks, and the replica follow-ups keep their failed judge gates. S15a-A1 is POSITIVE but confounded by harder negatives, S15a-A2 and S15a-A3 are exploratory, and S15a's content check was not performed. S15b stopped before any end-to-end call, and both S15c primary tests are final INVALID under its completeness gate (S15c-K1). The human regrade consists of the author's labels, from one informed grader, not a blind panel or ground truth.

Recomputations from the saved outputs checked the registered numerical reductions of the studies within documented limits on sources and masks (Appendix I.4). The S15 and S15c results were each recomputed twice, and the S19 and S19b secondary outputs had one further recomputation. None of these checks validates the meaning of raw answers or amounts to a replication by another laboratory. Detailed private manifests and administrative correspondence are omitted.

\subsection{H.3. Licenses and toolkit interfaces}\label{h.3.-licenses-and-toolkit-interfaces}

Public license declarations do not settle every upstream right. The recorded licenses are: MemOps, MIT; STALE, CC BY 4.0 (recorded in the study configuration, not verified); LoCoMo, CC BY-NC 4.0 (noncommercial research statistics only); HaluMem-Medium, CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 (statistics only); BEAM, CC BY-SA 4.0; LongMemEval, MIT for its repository; and LoCoMo-Plus, no license found (local exploratory statistics only). PersonaMem-v2's dataset card declares CC BY 4.0 and identifies descriptions from PersonaHub \citep{Ge2024PersonaHub} as a source. PersonaHub declares CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, with additional stated conditions on research use and on the generating models. The compatibility and precise scope of that upstream relationship remain unresolved here. The checked ForgetEval and MemoryAgentBench repositories declare MIT, but we do not certify the terms of the exact revisions used or the rights inherited from external material. LongMemEval's ShareGPT attribution points to FastChat code, whose repository states explicitly that the dataset is not released there, so FastChat's code license does not establish the license of the filler data. UltraChat \citep{Ding2023UltraChat} declares MIT for its data, alongside language on intended research and educational use. The archive redistributes no benchmark text or model responses. These are source disclosures, not legal clearance.

The released toolkit, \nolinkurl{memaudit} 0.1.0, runs offline on CPU and provides four groups of commands. The commands \nolinkurl{audit run}, \nolinkurl{audit marker}, and \nolinkurl{audit report} run the question-blind unit audit on pinned local benchmark files, count a specified construction marker, and replay saved prediction archives. The command \nolinkurl{qi run} measures wrong-question retrieval against the fair-null baselines (uniform chance, turn-weighted chance, and matched-placebo gold) and reports the CE with S16's interval rule, the query-blind feature scan, and the eight single-feature priors; it ranks with BM25 by default and offers the S16 hybrid retriever as an option. The command \nolinkurl{baselines nulls} scores the null-memory policies (store nothing, wipe plus replacement, keep all, and an oracle target) under ForgetEval's own scorer, from a pinned local checkout that the user supplies. The command \nolinkurl{replica replay} computes the replica estimators from pinned numeric outcomes, and \nolinkurl{replica rehearse} exercises the same path on invented data with a fake client. Users obtain the public datasets themselves. The package ships no benchmark text, prompts, or model outputs; it downloads nothing, runs no language model, and makes no provider calls, so it reproduces no reader, judge, or end-to-end result.

The toolkit's documentation records two reproductions from a local verification run of a development candidate on 4 October 2026. The audit command matched the paper's recomputed STALE and LoCoMo-Plus detectability AUROCs (Figure 1) to four decimals, and \nolinkurl{qi run} matched the stored S16 aggregates of the LoCoMo-Plus atlas row exactly, with BM25 and with the hybrid option. These historical checks are distinct from verification of the released artifact. The toolkit does not reproduce the confirmatory BEAM and HaluMem-Medium rows, because it packages no item converter for them. Nor does it reproduce the other atlas controls (cross-benchmark and different-type peers, constant queries, and the random-score null) or the full study pipelines. It is an open-source implementation of the audit's checks, not a reproduction package.

Before the release on 12 October 2026, the source and installed-wheel test suites each passed 145 tests, with one optional real-model smoke test skipped, and GitHub Actions on macOS and Ubuntu with Python 3.12 passed for the release code. Version 0.1.0 of \nolinkurl{memaudit} was released under Apache-2.0 for code and documentation on 12 October 2026 (\url{https://github.com/ozgurugurlu/memaudit}). Apache-2.0 does not relicense the input datasets or authorize their redistribution. These release boundaries complement the release statement that follows the main text's conclusion.

\subsection{I.2. Protocol, amendments, and exposure}\label{i.2.-protocol-amendments-and-exposure}

Each study had a reviewed preregistration, a hash lock over its registration and analysis code, development gates, and a test stage if the gates passed. The S10b judge was chosen after development validation was known. S10c and S11c changed analysis code after the lock under recorded amendments. S11c's amendment was installed while its test was running, and its development gate reused development data with known outcomes. Some regrade comparison code was written after its lock. S16 disclosed question-blind aggregates seen before its freeze, so its MemOps-session row is descriptive only. S17 was registered after S11c's failed gate, with S10c's results known; we treat it as a post hoc sensitivity analysis on reused answers. S18 was registered with the S10b, S10c, and S17 results known. Two substitute reviews replaced its planned pre-freeze review, and a reporting-only amendment fixed its claim wording before any outcome was opened.

S19 was registered with aggregate S10b, S10c, S17, and S18 results known, so its two-session direction was predictable and its five-session direction known. A fallback review replaced its planned pre-freeze review, and a further recheck came only after the freeze. Six amendments followed the freeze: five reporting-only amendments, and one that admitted the successor study's new runtime module without changing the runtime that S19 used. As registered, S19's gate was scored after all reads, when the pre-gate analysis already existed. S19b was registered after S19's partial Gemini 3.8 Flash estimates had been seen, so its direction was predictable. Two reviews preceded its freeze, but its planned review of the build came after the freeze instead of before it (a recorded order deviation). Three reporting-only amendments followed the freeze, and S19b's gate was likewise scored after all reads, when the pre-gate analysis already existed.

The human regrade ran its frozen plan v1 and locked analysis unchanged. No amendment registers the reinstated human route, and the author, who knows the design, graded after the LLM regrade aggregates existed (J.6, J.8). Exposure logs record which files were opened and when. A lock fixes the content of the locked files, not when inputs or outcomes were first seen. Appendix H.2 summarizes exposure, deviations, and the scope of the computational checks.

\subsection{I.3. Judge-validation gates}\label{i.3.-judge-validation-gates}

The S10c, S11b, S11c, S17, S19, and S19b follow-up gates summarized here require agreement of at least .80 and Cohen's κ of at least .70 against an LLM adjudicator on a registered packet. S11b, S11c, and S17 Part A (which reuses S11c's rule) use design-weighted agreement and κ, weighted by the packet's sampling weights. S10c's gate, which S17 Part B and the S17-B2 validity condition reuse, is unweighted, as are the gates of S19 and S19b (Appendices B.4.3 and B.5.3). Each gate also needs at least 60 labeled or determinate items. A failed gate makes the dependent cells UNRESOLVED (in S19 and S19b, ``judge-limited''), whatever their statistics say.

The binding adjudicator of the S10c, S11b, S11c, and S17 gates was Claude Opus 5.5. These are four study gates, not four independent label sets, because S17 reuses S11c's packet and S11c's development gate reuses S11b's validation. The 72-item gates of S19 and S19b were each labeled by a fresh Claude Opus 5.5 instance (\nolinkurl{claude-opus-5-5}) that saw no arm, reader, or outcome; Appendix K.6 gives the adjudicator instructions. S15c validated its judge separately, against two fresh, packet-only Claude Sonnet 5.5 reference raters approved by the author, one for development and one for test (Appendix D.5.2). Both reference gates passed, but S15c failed its primary completeness rule, and that failure is final. S11's own judge validation covered development answers only (Appendix C.1). S18 inherited S17 Part B's validation and did not retest its new answers (Appendix B.3). The one-grader human check (Appendix J) is not a gate: it has one design-aware grader and 50 items per study, fewer than the 60-item minimum, and no registered verdict of any study depends on it.

In a post hoc simulation that treats the observed agreement tables as true, a judge like S11c's passes a gate-sized packet in about 43--49\% of draws, and a judge like S10c's in about 87--97\%. These are conditional pass fractions, not pass rates on fresh packets or probabilities that a true κ exceeds .70 (see also Appendix H.1). Near the thresholds, a gate outcome therefore carries limited information about judge quality: a failed gate withholds a verdict, but it does not show that the judge is unreliable.

\subsection{I.4. Recomputations from the saved outputs}\label{i.4.-recomputations-from-the-saved-outputs}

Two separate recomputations from the saved outputs checked the registered numerical results of S10c, S11c, S16, S17, S18, S19, and S19b, and S18 had a third. Two recomputations checked the arithmetic of the one-grader human regrade (J.8); they add no human grader. S11b's stop rests on one recomputation of its gate.

S15 and S15c each had two recomputations from the saved outputs before their final reports (Appendix D). The S19 and S19b secondary results had one more. It reproduced every registered target, including the self-preference interval, and kept the partial-reader coverage and the binding decisions unchanged. It did not reopen the protected original files, and it does not establish that the sources or the model identities are authentic.

The post hoc checks were recomputed within their stated scopes. The saved scores of the second round were checked without repeating the model fits, and the 1,000-refit nulls were checked from saved values rather than refitted. These are computational checks, not replication by another laboratory or label validation; failed gates and missing data keep their status.

\section{J. One-grader human regrade of the regrade packet}\label{j.-one-grader-human-regrade-of-the-regrade-packet}

One human grader, the author, who knows the study design, graded all 100 items of the frozen packet that the LLM adjudicators of Appendix F graded: 50 answers of the original STALE ladder (S10b; claim C2 in the plan) and 50 replica answers (S11; claim C3). These are the labels of one design-aware grader. They are not an independent blind panel, not ground truth, and not human validation in a general sense; the main text calls them a one-grader human check. Every number below is an estimate on this packet, with about 50 items per study. Raw figures are unweighted over the sample. Design-weighted figures are reweighted to each study's frame, the full answer set from which that part of the packet was drawn. Values marked post hoc were computed after the labels were seen; they change no registered output and no study verdict. No item text appears here. Card J gives the protocol.

\subsection{J.2. Weights, leverage, and precision}\label{j.2.-weights-leverage-and-precision}

S10b items carry stratum weights \(N_s/n_s\), where \(s\) indexes the sampling strata, from 2.0 (generic-DIS) to 128.4 (pointer-AP). S11 answers carry \(1/\pi\), with \(\pi\) the union inclusion probability, from 18.6 (part P) to 346.0 (stable part-R answers); the 20 part-R answers carry 69.0\% of the S11 weight. The Kish effective sample size is 27.7 of 50 for S10b and 29.6 of 50 for S11. One pointer-AP item carries 5.3 points of C2's design-weighted agreement, and one stable part-R item carries 4.9 points of C3's, so the design-weighted points move in steps of a few points per item (post hoc values, computed from the design weights; none depends on the labels).

\textbf{Why both C2 design-weighted figures are high.} The author's grades matched both judges on all 30 sampled agreement-stratum items, which stand for 2,331 of the 2,412 frame units (96.6\%). On a DIS item the judges disagree and no label is ambiguous, so the author agrees with exactly one of them, and the two judges' design-weighted disagreements sum to the DIS frame share: 0.90 + 2.46 = 3.36 points. Once the agreement strata show no disagreement, each design-weighted agreement is at least 96.6\% by construction. The design-weighted figures therefore mainly encode 30 zero-disagreement items, while the raw 92.0\% and 68.0\% reflect how the 20 oversampled DIS items split. In C3, part R (20 of 20 agree) carries 69.0\% of the weight.

\textbf{Registered intervals.} Wilson intervals for raw agreement are binomial reference intervals for this disagreement-enriched sample. They ignore the stratification and, in C3, the pairing, and they do not describe the frame. The design-weighted quantities have a stratified bootstrap (2,000 resamples; S10b answers within strata, S11 pairs within parts). Every resample keeps zero disagreements wherever none was observed. In C2, whose weights are equal within strata, the brackets therefore move only with the DIS strata. In C3, part R stays 20 of 20 in agreement in every resample, but resampling its unequal weights still moves the agreement denominator, so that bracket does not reflect part P alone. The brackets omit the uncertainty from errors that the zero-event cells did not show, so they understate the uncertainty and are not confidence intervals. The tables below give them only in their captions, and Table J1 gives post hoc sensitivity values instead. The plan had expected brackets of about ±8--12 points. Appendix F.4 notes the same degeneracy in the LLM regrade and does not quote its brackets.

\KeepTable{25}{0}

Each sampling stratum is a group, with a Jeffreys \(\mathrm{Beta}(x + 0.5, n - x + 0.5)\) model of its disagreement rate, where \(x\) is the number of disagreements among the group's \(n\) items (seed 20261004; 20,000 draws). The Clopper--Pearson (CP) lower value is the design-weighted agreement obtained when each group's disagreement rate is set to its one-sided CP upper bound, Bonferroni-adjusted over the groups. These are model-specific sensitivity values, not intervals for the registered estimates. Binomial independence within groups and the omitted question dependence are model assumptions, not properties of the sampling design, and so is the grouped weighting in C3; in C2 the groups are the design's own strata, within which the weights are equal (Table J3). The C3 grouping (part × stability) gives a grouped point that differs from the registered 96.9\%. The C3 values equal adjudicator G3's in Appendix F.4, because both graders have five disagreements in part P and none in part R. The Jeffreys prior gives each zero-disagreement stratum (3--7 items in C2) a posterior mean disagreement of \(0.5/(n + 1)\), from 6.25\% to 12.5\%. The quantiles can therefore lie below the observed grouped point, and in both C2 rows they do (for example, 99.10 against 80.33--96.97). They show sensitivity to that prior, not uncertainty around the point.}\tabularnewline

\textbf{Where the judges disagree.} All 30 sampled agreement-stratum items matched the author's grades, so every disagreement lies in the DIS strata (Table J3). On the 20 sampled disagreements, the author sided with GLM-5.3-Flash 16 times: 5 of 8 in plain-DIS, 5 of 5 in generic-DIS, and 6 of 7 in pointer-DIS. The author backed GLM-5.3-Flash both where it was the lenient judge (it passed and Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite failed 13 answers, of which the author passed 10) and where it was the strict one (7 answers, of which the author failed 6; post hoc counts). This is consistent with the author's decision of 4 October 2026 not to rely on Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite at judge-disagreement points. Because the author both took that decision and graded the packet, the agreement is not an independent confirmation, even though the packet concealed every recorded verdict. Concealment also does not show that the author's prior preference, or identities inferred from the residual cues, could not affect individual grades. In design-weighted terms, GLM-5.3-Flash passes an answer that the author fails on an estimated 0.64\% of the frame and fails one that the author passes on 0.26\%; for Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite, the shares are 0.76\% and 1.70\%.

\textbf{Leniency.} A recomputation from S10b's saved outputs had found the GLM-5.3-Flash judge 4.0 and 3.3 points more lenient than Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite on plain and placebo-instruction answers. On this packet, the plain-group gap is +3.7 points (57.9 − 54.2; Table J4). Against the human grade, it splits into +0.9 points for GLM-5.3-Flash and −2.8 for Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite. For this grader, GLM-5.3-Flash's extra passes on plain and placebo-instruction answers are therefore mostly correct, and the gap mainly reflects the strictness of Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite. This interpretation rests on the six sampled plain-DIS answers that GLM-5.3-Flash passed and Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite failed, of which the author passed four (Wilson 30--90\%; post hoc). The post hoc four-cell model gives judge-minus-human 2.5--97.5\% ranges of −14.5 to +18.4 points for GLM-5.3-Flash in the plain group (Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite: −17.7 to +15.2) and of −4.95 to +23.8 for GLM-5.3-Flash in the pointer group. The packet shows no leniency signal, but it cannot bound arm-group leniency to a few points.

The binding judge's four disagreements with the author are all on answers of the GLM-5.3-Flash reader (Table J5; 34 of 38 agree, Wilson {[}75.9, 95.8{]}, κ .745), and in three of them the judge passed an answer that the author failed. The sample holds 15 DIS items from the GLM-5.3-Flash reader and 5 from the Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite reader (post hoc count), so the pattern fits a mild leniency of the GLM-5.3-Flash judge toward answers from its own model but cannot show it. By arm (raw), the binding judge agrees with the author on 5 of 5 artifact-pointer and 9 of 9 hybrid-5 answers, the two arms of S10b's headline contrast. Its four disagreements are on one plain, two placebo-instruction (one in each direction), and one wrong-partner-pointer answer. With five and nine items and no disagreement, the one-sided 95\% Clopper--Pearson upper bounds on the raw per-arm disagreement rates are about 45\% and 28\% (post hoc, treating each arm's sampled answers as a simple random sample). A judge-error difference as large as the +12.0-point contrast is therefore not excluded, and the packet does not test that component.

\textbf{What this means for the S10b ladder.} On this packet, the author, applying the official rubric, agrees with the binding judge on 46 of 50 answers, an estimated 99.1\% of the frame, and the judge's design-weighted pass rate is within a point of the human pass rate in every arm group. This is the paper's first non-LLM evidence on how the binding judge applies the STALE rubric. It does not bound a judge-error difference between the arms of the +12.0-point contrast, says nothing about the judges of S10c, S17, S19, or S19b, and leaves S10b's exposure and confounds unchanged (Appendix B.1).

All five disagreements lie in part P (Table J7). In two, the judge passed an answer that the author failed, and in three it failed one that the author passed; in design-weighted terms, the two directions cover an estimated 1.40\% and 1.66\% of the frame. The packet's design-weighted judge pass rate (65.4\%) is 5.5 points below the frame's known judge pass rate (70.9\%). This is realized sampling error, probably mostly from the 10 random pairs that carry 69\% of the weight (not decomposed). The human pass rate of 65.6\% should therefore be compared with 65.4\%, not taken as the frame's share of correct answers.

\endgroup

\textbf{What the interval covers.} The bootstrap resamples part-P and part-R pairs. All 10 part-R pairs show zero human discordance, so the interval covers only the variability of the part-P share, and the hidden term, the discordance hidden in judge-concordant pairs, cannot move. Unlike the C3 agreement bracket of J.2, this interval does not move with the resampled part-R weights, because the estimator of estimand E6 calibrates each domain to its fixed size. The interval therefore lies below 8.25 only because the hidden term is fixed at zero; it does not show that \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) is below the judge's \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\) (Table J9). The stored value is the registered number. It is the locked code's calibrated (Hájek-type) estimate, which rescales the sampled part-P pair weights to the known 5,110 judge-discordant pairs. The plan's prose specifies a literal Horvitz--Thompson (HT) total instead, which uses the raw weights. Those weights sum to 5,546.8, so the HT total on the same labels is 6.14 points (post hoc; 6.14 ≈ 5.65 × 5,546.8/\allowbreak{}5,110). Reproducing the stored value therefore does not establish adherence to the plan's estimator. Adjudicators G1--G3 face the same estimator conflict (Appendix F.3), and both values are kept.

\textbf{Hidden-term sensitivity (post hoc).} Each part-R pair graded as dis\-cor\-dant would add 4.18 points to \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\). A hidden share of 6.2\% would make \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) equal to the judge's 8.25, and at that share, no dis\-cor\-dant pair among 10 has probability .53. Replacing the zero by its one-sided 95\% Clopper--Pearson bound (.259) gives a \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) of 16.46 points, the human counterpart of the 16.9--18.5 points of adjudicators G1--G3 (Appendix F.3). This figure adds an upper sensitivity value for the hidden term to the estimated observed component, so it is a conditional sensitivity, not a joint confidence bound. The packet is therefore compatible with a human-graded gain from about the real-flip component to well above 8.25 points, and it cannot show whether the judge overstates or understates the replica gap.

\textbf{What this means for the replica result.} For this grader, the judge's replica flips are mostly real differences in correctness, as a point estimate (10 of 15; 68.6\% pair-weighted, bootstrap {[}42.6, 91.2{]}). The real flips alone give a human-graded discordance of about 11\% and a \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) of 5.65 points {[}3.51, 7.52{]} on this frame. On this packet, therefore, the label disagreement of our bespoke GLM-5.3-Flash pipeline (modeled on Mem0, not its code) on four LoCoMo conversations is not explained away as judge noise. Grader inconsistency cannot be measured with one grader: a single grading pass cannot separate real answer differences from within-grader error, and, depending on the error process, such error could add or remove observed discordance. \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) is not a corrected \(G_{\mathrm{mean}}\): it does not re-estimate the main analysis's 8.12 points {[}7.18, 9.07{]} or that estimate's intervals (Appendix C.1). It touches neither S11c's UNRESOLVED gate nor S17 Part A (Appendices C.2 and C.3). Rubric B is generous by design, and a stricter interpretation of the rubric, which the plan left out, could move the flip classification.

\textbf{Sharma and Lall.} The packet contains none of the systems, answers, or judges of \citet{SharmaLall2026Selection}. It estimates no share of their improvement and has no bearing on whether their gap is real; nothing here calls their gap noise. Our recomputed 8.9-point gain from their released labels remains a reference scale only (§3.5), and no ratio of \(G_{\mathrm{h}}\) to it is formed.

\end{longtable}

\endgroup

\textbf{Item-level overlap (post hoc counts).} On the 78 items where all three adjudicators gave the same determinate grade, the author agreed on 76. Both exceptions are STALE items. In one, the author departs from the judge, whose grade all three adjudicators share. In the other, a pointer-DIS hybrid-5 answer, the author sides with the judge against all three adjudicators. On the nine items where the author departs from the GLM-5.3-Flash judge (four in C2, five in C3), the adjudicators sided with the author on four, unanimously or by a majority of decided votes, and with the judge on three; two were split. Neither the author nor the adjudicators are ground truth, so these are departures, not corrections. Adjudicator G3's sparse pointer signal (Appendix F.2) came from two hybrid-5 items, and on both the author sided with the judge. Of G3's 11 disagreements with the judge, the author sided with G3 on five. The author and the adjudicators gave the same flip category on 10 of 10 jointly decided pairs (G1), 12 of 14 (G2), and 12 of 15 (G3). The author's two judge-lenient pairs are the main reason that the human share for estimand E5 is the lowest of the four graders.

\textbf{What the human labels add, and what they cannot.} They are the first non-LLM reference on this packet. They agree with adjudicators G1 and G2 that judge agreement is high on both claims, do not support G3's pointer signal, and move estimand E5 down: as a point estimate, ``most judge flips are real'' survives at about two-thirds (68.6\%) rather than 74--88\%, and its interval does not exclude a minority. They cannot add independence, because every grader shares the rubric text. They cannot add precision, because the strata are as sparse, and the random pairs as few, as in the LLM regrade. Nor can they cover answers outside the packet or settle the direction of the S11 judge's error in the population.

\subsection{J.6. Deviations from the plan}\label{j.6.-deviations-from-the-plan}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Deviation D1. Turkish/\allowbreak{}English packet.} According to the author's record, the author graded an LLM-translated Turkish/\allowbreak{}English rendering of the same 100 items, with the same codes and order. The question, the reference or context, the rubric for the item's probe type, and the system answer appear in Turkish beside the English original; the verbatim English rubrics are kept and marked binding; and the exported file format and the browser storage key are unchanged. The translation was prepared and checked without access to grades or outcomes. The author approved the rendering on 5 October 2026; the approval covered model use for preparing the packet only and did not permit model assistance while grading. Numeral checks passed on all 300 translated fields, and literal checks found zero mismatches. A full semantic review of 24 items, chosen from the English before any translation was seen, found no material mismatch; the other 76 items had no full semantic review, and two source-language phrases remain flagged. Post hoc, 2 of the 9 human-judge disagreements lie in the 24 reviewed items and 7 outside them (agreement 22 of 24 inside and 69 of 76 outside), and the one named flagged item is not a disagreement. These descriptive counts neither estimate nor rule out a translation effect on the grades: every item was shown bilingually, so there is no English-only counterfactual, and full semantic review supports fidelity only for the 24 reviewed items. Both renderings write the same file name, columns, and storage key, so the export cannot confirm which one was used, and which language the author relied on while grading is not recorded. The plan describes an English packet; the translation is a layer that the plan did not foresee, and its meaning risk is a limit of this grade.
\item
  \textbf{Deviation D2. The human route was reinstated after it had been replaced.} On 4 October 2026, the author decided not to grade by hand, and a registered amendment for LLM adjudication replaced the human route. The author then approved the bilingual rendering (5 October 2026) and graded the packet. No amendment registers the reinstatement; the analysis is plan v1's, run unchanged, and the human grade came after the LLM regrade aggregates existed (J.8).
\item
  \textbf{Deviation D3. Open decisions not taken.} Before grading, four options were left open: a ``strict'' box on the C3 items, more agreement-stratum items (a plan v2), S10b's LoCoMo-Plus items, and a second grader. None was taken. These are not breaches, but they bound what the grade shows: no split between strict and lenient grading, sparse strata, no LoCoMo-Plus items, and no human-human baseline.
\item
  \textbf{Deviation D4. Interval behavior.} The registered intervals ran as planned, but every resample keeps zero disagreements in the zero-event cells (in C3, the resampled part-R weights still move the denominator), so the intervals understate the uncertainty (J.2), which the plan's precision paragraph did not foresee. The post hoc sensitivity values are not registered and are not confidence intervals.
\item
  \textbf{Deviation D5. The estimator of estimand E6.} The stored calibrated estimate (5.65 points) differs from the Horvitz--Thompson total that the plan's prose specifies (6.14 points; J.4). Adjudicators G1--G3 face the same conflict (Appendix F.3). The stored value is kept as the registered number.
\item
  \textbf{Deviation D6. Label use.} The grading instructions asked for a note on every ambiguous item and on any item of hesitation. The author used no ambiguous label and wrote one note, so the plan's ambiguity bounds are moot. Whether zero ambiguity reflects confidence or forced choices is not known.
\item
  \textbf{Deviations D7 and D8. File location and self-test.} The author saved the export in the packet folder, and a byte-identical copy at the documented location was analyzed. The analysis code's self-test passed, and a later rerun of the self-test in a copy of the study folder also passed and reproduced the real analysis byte for byte (J.8).
\item
  \textbf{Unchanged.} The sample, the item codes and their order, the frozen list, the plan, the builder, the analysis code, and the key; no item was dropped, and none was left ungraded.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{J.7. What the human regrade cannot show}\label{j.7.-what-the-human-regrade-cannot-show}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Human-human reliability, and any reliability of this grader beyond the 100 items.
\item
  The design-based precision of any design-weighted figure (J.2).
\item
  Judge validity on other answer sets: S10c, S11c, S17, S19, S19b, and S10b's LoCoMo-Plus items are not sampled, and every registered gate in this paper remains LLM adjudication (Appendix I.3).
\item
  The judge-error component of any contrast between arms (J.3).
\item
  The size of the discordance that the S11 judge hides inside its concordant pairs, beyond ``none in 10'' (J.4).
\item
  A corrected S11 gap, or anything about another pipeline, including Sharma and Lall's.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{J.8. Exposure and computational checks}\label{j.8.-exposure-and-computational-checks}

The author saw the bilingual text and knew the study design. Grading took place between the delivery of the bilingual packet on 5 October 2026 and the export on 6 October 2026, with no detailed grading log. Within the packet, the author was blind to arm, reader, judge, verdict, stratum, and study labels, apart from the residual cues in J.1. The LLM regrade aggregates already appeared in manuscript drafts from 4 October 2026, before grading. Whether the author had seen them is not recorded, so prior exposure must be assumed; no item-level LLM label was ever in the packet. Compliance with the instruction to avoid models and private study reports while grading was not recorded. The translation was prepared and checked without access to grades or outcomes. No registered study decision depends on these human labels.

Two separate recomputations from the saved outputs checked the human regrade. The first reproduced the registered outputs byte for byte and every post hoc value. The second reproduced 1,211 floating-point and 224 exact fields of the registered estimands (largest difference \(1.33 \times 10^{-15}\)), all 100 answer and 25 pair inclusion probabilities, and all 27 groups of post hoc outputs. Its calculations were sealed before the target outputs were opened, but they used the analysis source code, so they are source-informed reproductions. A later check supplied the exact stored lower end of the pointer-group leniency range, −4.95 (J.3). These checks establish neither which language the author relied on nor that the author had no assistance; they add no human grader and reopen no failed validation gate.

\section{K. Implementation details: data, retrieval, prompts and models}\label{k.-implementation-details-data-retrieval-prompts-and-models}

This appendix gives the implementation details that the study sections only summarize. K.1 identifies the benchmark files and the samples drawn from them, K.2 specifies the question-blind detectability classifier, K.3 gives the retrieval settings, and K.4 defines the registered single-feature priors and the hard-negative count. K.5 reproduces the reader-side texts and their rendering, K.6 gives the instructions for the judges, the adjudicators, and the human grader, K.7 lists the models and their settings, and K.8 specifies the replica pipeline and the mem0 adapter. Every value below is taken from the studies' locked configurations, input manifests, and code, or from the toolkit's data cards.

\subsection{K.2. The question-blind detectability classifier}\label{k.2.-the-question-blind-detectability-classifier}

\textbf{Learner.} The classifier of Appendix A.1 is scikit-learn's \nolinkurl{HistGradientBoostingClassifier}, a histogram gradient-boosting (HGB) model, run with scikit-learn 1.9.1, NumPy 2.5.3, and SciPy 1.18.1 under Python 3.12, the versions pinned in the study environment. It departs from the library defaults in six settings: 200 boosting iterations (default 100), learning rate 0.05 (default 0.1), maximum tree depth 4 (default unlimited), L2 regularization 1.0 (default 0), early stopping off (by default it switches on above 10,000 training rows), and random state 0. Every other setting keeps its default: log loss, at most 31 leaves per tree, at least 20 rows per leaf, 255 feature bins, and no class weights. Fits ran with four OpenMP threads. The same learner, trained once on 198 STALE reserve scenarios (F1 text, both roles), gives S10b's style pointer, the two sessions with the highest predicted probability.

\textbf{Units, pools, and folds.} Each setting is fitted separately (Table K2.1). A row is a pair of a pool and a unit, labeled evidence or not; a unit that several pools share, such as a LoCoMo session, appears once per pool. Cross-validation is scikit-learn's \nolinkurl{GroupKFold} with five folds on the setting's CV groups. The folds have no seed: without shuffling, \nolinkurl{GroupKFold} sorts the groups by size and gives each to the fold with the fewest rows so far. A test row whose unit text (a hash of its role-tagged turns) also occurs among the training rows of its fold is dropped and counted; this is the test-duplicate exclusion. A fold whose training rows hold a single class gives its test rows that class's share as their score.

\textbf{Score and interval.} The out-of-fold probabilities give one AUROC per pool, with tied scores sharing their average rank. A pool is scored if it keeps at least one evidence row and one non-evidence row, and detectability AUROC (macro within-pool) is the unweighted mean over the scored pools. Its 95\% interval is a percentile bootstrap that resamples CV groups (2,000 draws, seed 0) and recomputes the ratio of the summed pool AUROCs to the number of pools; it conditions on the saved predictions. The registered reading classes are negligible below .55, weak from .55 to .65, strong from .65 to .90, and saturated at .90 or above. The F1 level applies the typographic normalizer of Appendix K.5, and the F2 level also strips Markdown markup; Table A1 reports the raw level.

\textbf{Permutation refits.} The post hoc label-permutation nulls of Appendix H.1 refit this pipeline after permuting the labels within each pool, which keeps each pool's number of evidence units. They reuse the folds, the saved test-duplicate mask, the learner settings (random state 0), and four threads, and they recompute the same statistic. The first run drew \(B = 50\) permutations per setting with seed \(20261004 + i\), and the second drew \(B = 1{,}000\) with seed \(20261005 + i\), where \(i = 0, \dots, 8\) indexes STALE, LoCoMo-Plus, LongMemEval-S, BEAM 100K, BEAM 500K, BEAM 1M, HaluMem-Medium, PersonaMem-v2, and LoCoMo; each seed starts NumPy's default generator for all permutations of its setting. The p-value is \(p = (1 + n_{\geq})/(B + 1)\), where \(n_{\geq}\) counts the null values at or above the observed value. Before the first run, a refit reproduced the saved out-of-fold scores exactly in all nine settings.

\textbf{Manipulation check (S15-K3).} The text-only check of S15 (Appendix D.3) applies the same 31 features and the same learner to the sessions of each counterfactual haystack, computed on their rendered text (the F1-normalized user turns, all counted as user turns). It differs in four ways. Its CV groups are S15's five construction folds: a scenario's or question's fold is the SHA-256 of \nolinkurl{S15-fold|} or \nolinkurl{S15-lme-fold|} followed by its id, modulo 5. Duplicates are purged from the training side: training rows whose unit text occurs in the test fold are dropped. The random state is 1515. The 95\% interval comes from 10,000 cluster-bootstrap draws over pools (seed 1515). The check passes if the detectability AUROC is at most .60 and its upper bound at most .65; a nonfinite score fails. Two companion diagnostics do not gate. The first is a character 3- to 5-gram TF-IDF model (n-grams within word boundaries, minimum document frequency 2, at most 200,000 features, sublinear term frequency) with class-balanced L2 logistic regression (\(C = 1\), liblinear solver), under the same folds and purge. The second is the pair cue, the share of pools whose two most TF-IDF-similar sessions are both evidence, against its chance rate \(k(k-1)/(n(n-1))\) for \(k\) evidence sessions among \(n\). S15's constructed style ranker (Appendix D.3) scores each session by the percentile of its hybrid score within the pool plus twice its out-of-fold S15-K3 probability.

\textbf{Toolkit.} The toolkit memaudit 0.1.0 implements this audit as \nolinkurl{memaudit audit run}, with the same 31 features, learner settings, five grouped folds, test-duplicate exclusion, and 2,000-draw bootstrap with seed 0 (it fits with one thread), and with adapters for all nine settings. It does not regenerate Table A1 or Figure 1 as a whole. Its documentation verifies two rows, each with one command on the pinned files of Table K1.2. For STALE, \nolinkurl{memaudit audit run --benchmark stale --levels raw --variants all}, given the file and its digest, yields 1.000 {[}1.000, 1.000{]} on 400 of 400 pools, exact at three decimals. For LoCoMo-Plus, the same command with \nolinkurl{--benchmark locomo_plus}, which also takes the host file and its digest, yields .995 {[}.991, .997{]} on 393 of 401 pools within a stated tolerance of ±.002; the recorded verification run matched the saved values to four decimals. For the other seven settings the same command runs, and a population-parity check of the toolkit's release candidate found that its adapters select the same units, labels, pools, and groups as the study's adapters, but no toolkit refit of those rows was compared with Table A1. The toolkit has no plotting code, so Figure 1 is drawn from the saved values, and it implements neither the label-permutation refits nor the original LoCoMo-Plus position-only probe, for which it uses each turn's position in its current pool.

\subsection{K.3. Retrieval settings}\label{k.3.-retrieval-settings}

All retrieval ran on CPU with fixed models and no tuning. Table K3.1 lists what each use indexes.

\textbf{BM25.} BM25 \citep{RobertsonZaragoza2009BM25} is the \nolinkurl{BM25Okapi} class of the rank-bm25 package, version 0.2.2, with \(k_1 = 1.5\), \(b = .75\), and \(\varepsilon = 0.25\): a term with a negative inverse document frequency receives 0.25 times the mean inverse document frequency. The index is built over the turns of one pool, so document frequencies are computed per scenario, haystack, or item. The tokenizer lowercases the text, splits it into maximal runs of Unicode letters and digits, removes a fixed list of 153 English stopwords, and applies the Snowball English stemmer of py-rust-stemmers 0.1.8; a text without tokens is scored as one placeholder token.

\textbf{Dense encoder.} The dense encoder is BAAI/\allowbreak{}bge-small-en-v1.5 \citep{Xiao2023CPack} in the int8 ONNX export that fastembed 0.8.1 loads (\nolinkurl{Qdrant/bge-small-en-v1.5-onnx-Q}), run with onnxruntime 1.30.0. Its vectors have 384 dimensions and are L2-normalized, so the score is the cosine similarity. Queries carry the instruction prefix ``Represent this sentence for searching relevant passages:'', and documents carry none. S15, S15c, and S16 pinned the local snapshot, revision \nolinkurl{aa8f8b060edb00e03bfdd08813a2949946c8ba55} (ONNX weights SHA-256 \nolinkurl{51f1bd0addd6e859e42c2c8021a5e5461385bb676a649f4b269aa445449f2431}), and checked it before every stage. S10, S10b, and S10c loaded the encoder from the same shared model cache but did not record its revision. S19 rebuilt its dense-2 rankings from the cached embeddings of the S10 pointer computation, without encoding any text, and required the rebuilt rankings to reproduce the stored five-session and two-session lists on every probe.

\textbf{Fusion.} The hybrid score of a turn is reciprocal rank fusion \citep{Cormack2009RRF}, \(1/(60 + r_{\mathrm{BM25}}) + 1/(60 + r_{\mathrm{dense}})\), where \(r\) is the turn's rank among the pool's turns under each retriever and tied scores share their average rank. The dense-only pointers (dense-5 and dense-2) rank by the cosine alone.

\textbf{What is indexed.} Neither the ladder pointers nor the atlas index session-level text. Both score every user-side turn and give a session, or another unit, the score of its best turn. No path adds dates or session headers to the indexed text, and only S15a's secondary cross-encoder truncates by characters. The encoder library cuts inputs longer than the model's 512-token window without a warning, so a dense score sees at most the first 512 tokens of a turn, while BM25 sees the whole turn.

\textbf{Other encoders.} S15a also reports a cross-encoder, jinaai/\allowbreak{}jina-reranker-v1-turbo-en (local snapshot \nolinkurl{b8c14f4e723d9e0aab4732a7b7b93741eeeb77c2}), as a secondary retriever: it reranks a fixed hybrid shortlist of the top 30 sessions and reads the first 6,000 characters of each session's joined user turns. S15 used a third encoder, thenlper/\allowbreak{}gte-base (local snapshot \nolinkurl{c078288308d8dee004ab72c6191778064285ec0c}, with no query or document prefix), which is not one of the tested retrievers. It screens the donor scenarios and sessions of the counterfactual haystacks for topical closeness to the target (Appendix D.1) and defines the hard-negative count (K.4). Appendix K.8 gives the fact retrieval of the replica pipeline and of the mem0 adapter.

\subsection{K.4. Registered single-feature priors and hard negatives}\label{k.4.-registered-single-feature-priors-and-hard-negatives}

\textbf{Atlas priors (S16).} Among its descriptive controls, S16 registered a ``length-prior set (chars, turns, mean turn length, position; both directions) and its maximum''. Each of the eight priors ranks a haystack's nonempty units by one question-blind feature (Table K4.1). Empty units rank last, and ties are broken by the hash rule of the atlas's retrieval ranks, with the prior's name in place of the query id. The prior's hit for an item is any-gold@\(k\) of its top \(k\) units, for \(k\) = 1, 5, and 10. Table E2 reports, for each row, the largest any-gold@5 among the eight priors, a selected maximum and not a separately confirmed effect. The priors are distinct from S16-K3, which scans the single-feature AUROC (within-haystack) of characters, turns, position, non-ASCII count, and typographic-marker count (Appendix E.1).
