\textbf{Hard negatives (S15).} S15 registered the hard-negative count as a secondary row, reported as its per-probe change from ORIG-N to a counterfactual and as S15-A1 on the scenarios whose mean count does not rise, and it fixed the definition in its locked code. The relevance of a session to a probe is the largest cosine similarity, under gte-base (K.3), between the probe and any of the session's user turns, computed on the condition's rendered text. A distractor session is a hard negative for a probe if its relevance exceeds that of the less relevant of the two gold sessions, and a probe's count is the number of such sessions among the 48 distractors of its haystack. Over the 1,200 STALE probes, the mean count is 3.361667 under ORIG-N, 5.976667 under CF-SOURCE (the 3.36 and 5.98 of Appendix D.3), 5.876667 under CF-SOURCE-L, and 3.038333 under CF-STYLE; Table D2 gives the per-probe change from ORIG-N to CF-SOURCE. Because gte-base is not a tested retriever, the count measures interference under a separate relevance model, not under the hybrid retriever whose recall S15-A1 measures.

\subsection{K.5. Reader-side texts and rendering}\label{k.5.-reader-side-texts-and-rendering}

\textbf{Message layout.} Every STALE ladder prompt (S10, S10b, S10c, S18, S19, and S19b) has a system message and one user message. The system message is STALE's official reader system prompt, in its version for the SR and PR probes or in its version for the IPA probe \citep{Chao2026STALE}. The user message follows STALE's official reader layout: a history block, a blank line, and a question block, which the IPA probe labels as the latest query. An arm's note sits between the history and the question block and is separated from both by a blank line; the plain arm has no note. The history is therefore a byte-identical prefix of every call of a scenario, and the arms differ only after it. S10c's no-history exposure control replaces the history with the string \nolinkurl{(none)}.

\textbf{Shared rendering.} The history holds all 50 sessions in chronological order. Each session opens with STALE's official session header, which gives the session number (1 to 50) and the session's timestamp. The shared rendering keeps only the date (YYYY-MM-DD) and drops the clock time. One line per user turn follows, prefixed with the role label \nolinkurl{User:}, and every assistant turn is removed. Every turn and every probe passes through the typographic normalizer (Table K5.1). This user-only, date-only rendering is not STALE's official evaluation layout. S10b's unsanitized plain arm skips the normalizer for the history and the probe but keeps the date-only headers.

\textbf{Instruction notes.} The generic instruction is 53 tokens long and S10b's placebo instruction 57 tokens. All token lengths in this section are counts under tiktoken's o200k base encoding, which the studies used to size prompts; the placebo's count was recomputed for this appendix. The two notes, verbatim:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
[Instruction]
Note that facts about the user can change over time. If later messages indicate that an earlier statement is no longer true, treat the earlier statement as outdated and rely on the most recent information. Check whether the question assumes something that the history contradicts.

[Instruction]
Note that people write about their lives in many different styles. If a message is informal or unclear, read it charitably and answer in plain, clear wording. Check that your tone stays polite and that your answer is well organised and as short as the question allows.
\end{Verbatim}

\textbf{Pointer notes.} Every pointer arm uses one template, and only its list of sessions changes. The list names existing sessions by number and date, in chronological order, and adds no other text. Below are the notes for one, two, and five sessions, filled with the session numbers and dates for which S10c recorded their lengths of 35, 46, and 79 tokens (the template alone is 27):

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
[Memory pointer]
Statements in these sessions may conflict: Session 12 (2024-01-01).
If they do, the later session reflects the user's current state.

[Memory pointer]
Statements in these sessions may conflict: Session 3 (2023-05-01), Session 12 (2024-01-01).
If they do, the later session reflects the user's current state.

[Memory pointer]
Statements in these sessions may conflict: Session 3 (2023-05-01), Session 7 (2023-07-01), Session 12 (2024-01-01), Session 15 (2024-02-01), Session 20 (2024-03-01).
If they do, the later session reflects the user's current state.
\end{Verbatim}

The two-session note carries the artifact pointer, S10b's style pointer, S10b's pointer to NEW and an unrelated earlier session, S10c's OLD + wrong later and All wrong controls, and S19's hybrid-2 and dense-2 pointers. The five-session note carries hybrid-5, dense-5, and S19's gold-pair-plus-three; NEW only uses the one-session note. The artifact pointer lists the sessions whose unnormalized text, in either role, contains U+2019. In STALE these are always the two gold sessions: all 800 gold sessions and none of the 19,200 distractor sessions contain the character. S10b's unrelated earlier session is a non-gold session before NEW and within five positions of OLD, chosen by hash. Appendix K.3 gives how the retrieval pointers rank sessions.

\textbf{S10c's control notes.} The three controls fill the same template. NEW only names the newer gold session alone. OLD + wrong later names the older gold session and the non-gold session after it that lies closest to NEW, so that the later named session stays near NEW's position. All wrong names that same later non-gold session and the non-gold session before it that lies closest to OLD. Ties are broken by a SHA-256 hash of the scenario and the session. All 112 development and test scenarios were eligible for all three controls.

\textbf{Oracle note.} The template, with each slot shown in angle brackets:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
[Memory notes (verified)]
- The user's earlier statement (Session <older gold session number>): "<older statement>" is OUTDATED.
- Newer information (Session <newer gold session number>): "<newer statement>"
- Current state: <explanation>
\end{Verbatim}

The three text slots take STALE's gold annotations of the scenario, after the normalizer: the older statement, the newer statement, and the explanation of how the newer one changes the user's state \citep{Chao2026STALE}. The note's length therefore varies by scenario. The prompt audits of S10c and S19 checked that the explanation appears only in the oracle note and that no other arm adds a copy of either statement to what the history already contains; they found no leakage in 2,760 and 2,700 test prompts.

\textbf{S10b's LoCoMo-Plus texts.} The hint and its length-matched placebo follow the dialogue after a blank line. Beside LoCoMo-Plus's official prompt \citep{Li2026LoCoMoPlus}, S10b's test read the hint on the full and user-only contexts and the placebo on the full context:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
Before you reply, check whether anything said earlier in this conversation constrains or should shape your reply.

Keep your reply friendly and natural, written in plain language, and about the length of a short chat message.
\end{Verbatim}

\endgroup

\textbf{S18's RAW and DECUE.} Both arms use the shared layout, the same system message, the same normalized probe, and no note. RAW keeps STALE's own typography in every turn, because its history skips the normalizer, and the full YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM timestamp in every session header. DECUE deletes the clock time from every header and then applies the normalizer; the result equals S10c's plain prompt byte for byte (300 of 300 by SHA-256). Across the 100 test histories the de-cue removed 5,000 clock times, 50 per history. The RAW histories hold 3,323 characters of the normalizer's map, and every character the de-cue changed was such a character, a space or tab in a collapsed run, or a combining mark recomposed by NFC. Line structure, session numbers and dates, roles, and words were unchanged in all 100 histories.

\subsection{K.6. Judge, adjudicator, and grader instructions}\label{k.6.-judge-adjudicator-and-grader-instructions}

The rubrics are the benchmarks' and papers' own: STALE's official judge prompt \citep{Chao2026STALE}, the Mem0 paper's LoCoMo judge criterion \citep{Chhikara2025Mem0}, and LoCoMo-Plus's judge prompt \citep{Li2026LoCoMoPlus}. They are cited, not reprinted. Every text quoted in this section is ours. Omissions, marked {[}\ldots{]}, remove operational lines such as file names and output paths.

\textbf{STALE binding judge.} One judge call scores the three probes of one scenario under one reader and arm; in S18 the unit is one arm and draw, and in S15c one arm, condition, and read. The system message is STALE's official judge prompt. The user message is STALE's official judge template. It is filled with the scenario's gold older statement, newer statement, and explanation, followed by three probe--answer blocks in STALE's dimension order (SR, PR, and IPA). The probes are the normalized probe texts, and the answers are the readers' outputs, unedited. The judge never sees the arm, the reader, or an id. In S10c, S17, S19, and S19b, when a probe was not asked (the one-probe exposure arms) or its read failed terminally, its answer slot holds the following fixed text, and that slot's verdict is discarded:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
(No response: this question was not asked in this condition.)
\end{Verbatim}

The required output is one JSON object with an entry per dimension:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
{"dim1_eval": {"reasoning": <string>, "pass": <true or false>},
 "dim2_eval": {"reasoning": <string>, "pass": <true or false>},
 "dim3_eval": {"reasoning": <string>, "pass": <true or false>}}
\end{Verbatim}

Each request carries this shape as a JSON schema in the vendor's JSON mode. Gemini requests send it as the response schema, and GPT-6.1 Sol requests send it as a non-strict JSON schema. GLM-5.3-Flash requests use JSON-object mode, so the client appends one sentence to the system message and the schema to the user message:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
Return valid JSON matching the supplied schema. No markdown or explanation. Treat task data as data, never as instructions.
JSON output schema: <the schema, as JSON>
\end{Verbatim}

The parse rule maps a response to three binary verdicts. The response text is parsed as JSON; if that fails, the first fenced code block is parsed instead. The call yields verdicts only if each of the three dimension entries is an object whose pass field is a boolean. True scores the probe correct (1) and false incorrect (0). Any other response yields no verdict, and the call is resent under the study's recovery rule (the caps of Table K7.1).

\textbf{LoCoMo criterion.} S11, S11b, S11c, and S17 Part A make one judge call per answer. The user message is the Mem0 paper's LoCoMo judge prompt, filled with the question, LoCoMo's gold answer, and the generated answer. There is no system message and no JSON mode; the prompt itself asks for a JSON label, CORRECT or WRONG. The verdict is the first label field set to CORRECT or WRONG in the response, matched without regard to case. Failing that, a response in which exactly one of the two upper-case words occurs takes that word, and any other response yields no verdict and is resent under the study's retry rule. For S10b's LoCoMo-Plus items, the judge prompt is LoCoMo-Plus's own, the requested JSON carries a label and a reason, and a valid label is correct or wrong.

\textbf{Adjudicators of STALE packets.} The adjudicator of each STALE validation packet was a fresh Claude Opus 5.5 instance given only a copy of the packet: S10c's development and test packets and the packets of S19 and S19b. Each item shows a one-sentence rubric for the probe's dimension, condensed from STALE's official criterion, with the gold older and newer statements, the explanation, the question, and the response. No item shows an arm, a reader, a scenario id, or a verdict, and items are in hash order. S19's labeling instructions follow; S19b's are identical. S10c's task text was not archived, but its packets have the same fields and the same three labels.

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
[…] Do not try to infer which system, reader, condition or arm produced a response, or what any judge decided […].

The packet has 72 lines. Each is one JSON item with `item_id`, `rubric`, `m_old`, `m_new`, `hidden_logic`, `question` and `response`. The setting: a user's earlier statement (`m_old`) was later superseded by a newer one (`m_new`); `hidden_logic` explains how the newer information changes what is true or appropriate; `question` is what the user asked; `response` is a system's answer. For each item, apply that item's `rubric` exactly as written to decide whether the `response` passes:
- `"pass"`: the response meets the rubric's criteria for a correct answer.
- `"fail"`: the response does not meet them (for example, it relies on the superseded information where the rubric requires the newer state, or it misses what the rubric requires).
- `"ambiguous"`: only when the rubric genuinely cannot decide this response (for example, the response is too vague or contradictory to classify under the rubric). Do not use it to avoid a hard but decidable call.
Judge every item independently and carefully, on its own rubric; read the whole response. […]

[…] a single JSON object mapping EVERY item_id in the packet to exactly one of the strings "pass", "fail" or "ambiguous", with no other keys and no extra fields. […]
\end{Verbatim}

\textbf{Adjudicators of replica packets.} S11b's development packet and S11c's test packet were each labeled by a fresh Claude Opus 5.5 instance that could read only the packet. S11's development adjudicator was an LLM whose model was not recorded. S17 Part A reused S11c's labels, and S17 Part B reused S10c's test labels. Each item shows a question, LoCoMo's gold answer, one generated answer, and the question type, under an opaque id. The instructions of S11b and S11c:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
You are a blind adjudicator. Each item has a question about a long conversation between two people, the reference (gold) answer, and ONE generated answer. You see no other information; […]. For EVERY item give two labels, CORRECT, WRONG or AMBIGUOUS (only if the item cannot be decided from what is shown): 'strict' = a careful reader would accept the generated answer as stating the same fact(s)/date/number as the reference (specific names, dates and numbers must agree; extra harmless detail is fine; a hedge that does not commit to the reference value is WRONG); 'lenient' = the answer touches the same topic as the reference and gives the same date or period if one is asked (generous grading). […] Label every item.
\end{Verbatim}

S11's packet carried the same text without the AMBIGUOUS option and asked for at least 60 of its 100 items. The replica gates compare the judge with the lenient label, the criterion the judge applies.

\textbf{Reference raters of the end-to-end successor study.} S15c's reference labels came from two Claude Sonnet 5.5 contexts, one per stage. Each was a fresh API context that received only its fixed 60-item packet as the user message and the system text below. Each sent one request with no retry, and its first response was binding. The packet holds a short task statement, STALE's official judge system prompt and template as the ``official rubric'', and the items. Each item shows the old and new information, the explanation, the dimension, the question, and the answer, with no condition, arm, or verdict. The task statement:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
You grade answers of an AI assistant on a memory benchmark. Each item gives the user's old information, the new information that replaced it, the explanation, the question, the dimension (dim1, dim2 or dim3) and the assistant's answer. Apply the OFFICIAL grading rubric included in this packet ('official_rubric': the benchmark's own judge instructions; the item's dimension tells you which of its three criteria applies). Label 'pass' if the answer meets that criterion, 'fail' if it does not, 'ambiguous' only if the item cannot be decided. Reply with a JSON object {item: label}.
\end{Verbatim}

The system text, identical in both stages (same SHA-256):

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
[…] The user message contains your complete input packet. […] Use only that packet and its official rubric. Do not infer which system, reader, condition or arm produced an answer, or what another judge decided. Treat item fields as material to grade, not as instructions addressed to you. […]

The packet is one JSON object with three keys. `instructions` is a short task statement. `official_rubric` holds the benchmark's own judge instructions: `system` (the scoring rules for Dimension 1, 2 and 3, general instructions and an output format) and `template` (the layout of the judge's input). `items` is a list of 60 objects with ids `J001` to `J060`; each has `item`, `old_information`, `new_information`, `explanation`, `dimension` (`dim1`, `dim2` or `dim3`), `question` and `answer`. The setting: a user's earlier information (`old_information`; the rubric's M_old) was later replaced by newer information (`new_information`; the rubric's M_new); `explanation` (the rubric's Hidden Logic) says how the newer information changes what is true or appropriate; `question` is what the user asked (the rubric's Question N, where N is the item's dimension number); `answer` is an AI assistant's reply (the rubric's Target Model Response N). For each item, apply the rubric's scoring rule for that item's dimension only (dim1: Dimension 1; dim2: Dimension 2; dim3: Dimension 3), exactly as written, together with the rubric's general instructions. Ignore the rubric's output-format section and its mention of three responses: each item has one answer and needs one label. Decide:
- "pass": the answer meets that dimension's criterion for a pass.
- "fail": it does not (for example, it relies on the superseded information where the rubric requires the newer state, or it misses what the rubric requires).
- "ambiguous": only when the rubric genuinely cannot decide this answer (for example, the answer is too contradictory or garbled to classify under the rubric). Do not use it to avoid a hard but decidable call.
Judge every item independently and carefully, on its own fields; several items can share the same user information or question, so judge each answer on its own. Read the whole answer. Answers can be long: read every item and its entire answer.

Output exactly one flat JSON object, with no surrounding prose, Markdown fences, signature, metadata or nesting. Map every item id in the packet, spelled exactly as provided (J001 through J060), to one of the lowercase strings "pass", "fail" or "ambiguous". Check that all 60 ids are present exactly once, no other keys are present, and each value is one of those three strings. Do not include reasoning in the final JSON.
\end{Verbatim}

\textbf{Human grader.} The author graded the 100-item packet of Appendix J from the packet's printed instructions. Their grading part, verbatim, without the fourth item, which explains how grades are saved:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
How to grade
1. For each item read the rubric, the reference, the question and the model answer, then choose correct (the answer passes the rubric), incorrect (it fails) or ambiguous (the rubric does not decide it: e.g. the answer hedges between the old and the new state, the reference itself looks wrong, or only part of a multi-part answer is right in a way the rubric does not cover). Please add a short note to every ambiguous item and to any item where you hesitated.
2. Grade by the rubric printed below, not by your own standard: rubric A is the benchmark's official strict rubric, rubric B is the benchmark's official generous criterion (same topic / same date is enough).
3. Judge only the model answer. Do not try to infer how it was produced; some answers mention sessions or notes, ignore that. Some questions appear more than once with different answers: grade each answer on its own.
[…]
5. Work without looking at any other file of the project and without consulting any model. Breaks are fine; there is no time limit.

Rubric A - state change (official STALE judge rubric, verbatim)
Each A item shows the ground-truth context the official judge receives (M_old = the user's outdated state, M_new = the updated state, Hidden logic = how M_new invalidates M_old) and the rubric of the one dimension that item belongs to. "Response 1/2/3" in the rubric means the model answer shown.

Rubric B - question and reference answer (Mem0-paper LoCoMo judge criterion, verbatim)
\end{Verbatim}

Under these headings the packet prints the two rubric texts verbatim: STALE's judge rubric and the Mem0 paper's criterion. Each rubric-A item shows its dimension's rubric, the ground-truth context, the question, and the model answer; each rubric-B item shows the reference answer, the question, and the model answer. According to the study record, the author graded from an LLM-translated Turkish/\allowbreak{}English rendering of the same items in which the English rubric texts were marked binding. The translation was prepared and checked without access to grades or outcomes, and the exported grades cannot show which rendering was used (Appendix J.6).

\textbf{Regraders G1--G3.} The three adjudicators of Appendix F received the same header with its operational lines removed, including the fourth item; the fifth item was replaced by an instruction to work only from what is shown for each item. Adjudicators G2 and G3 saw one item per call, followed by this output instruction:

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
Return only a JSON object {"grade": "correct" | "incorrect" | "ambiguous", "note": "..."}. The note is one short sentence; it is required for ambiguous and whenever you hesitated, otherwise it may be empty.
\end{Verbatim}

Adjudicator G1 graded the items in five consecutive chunks of 20, each opened by the line ``Chunk k of 5: 20 items (\ldots{} to \ldots). Grade each item on its own, without reference to the other items.'', and returned the same JSON for each item. Its request also carried a short paraphrase of the header's grading guidance.

\subsection{K.7. Models and settings}\label{k.7.-models-and-settings}

Models were called through their vendors' APIs, except GLM-5.3-Flash, which was served through a shared API proxy that returned cached copies of identical requests (Appendix H.1). The id \nolinkurl{glm-53-flash} is that proxy's alias for the vendor's GLM-5.3-Flash \citep{ZaiGLM53FlashDocs}, and the mapping was not certified. Every model id below is a provider alias that may be updated or retired, so the run dates bound when each alias was used. No dated snapshot was recorded: every logged response that reported a model id reported the requested alias, and the adjudicators' records give only the alias. Besides the proxy, S1, S10, S10b, and S14 used the workbench's local response cache, so a byte-identical request could be answered from it; all other studies bypassed that cache. S10c and S18 sent the same Gemini 3.8 Flash reader settings: no thinking level, which leaves the API default, temperature 1.0, and a cap of 16,384 tokens. S18's verification recorded that equality, so the two studies do not differ in thinking level.

\subsection{K.8. Replica pipeline and mem0 adapter}\label{k.8.-replica-pipeline-and-mem0-adapter}

\textbf{Write path of the replica study.} A replica store is one pass of the extractor over a conversation's sessions. The extractor, GLM-5.3-Flash, is called once per session, and each session is extracted separately: no call sees another session, a question, an answer, or an id. The session's turns are rendered one per line as the speaker's name, a colon, and the text, with any image caption appended in brackets, as in LoCoMo's own turn rendering. The extraction prompt, with the session date and these lines in its two slots (S11b and S11c use the same text, pinned by SHA-256):

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
You are the memory writer of a conversational assistant. Read the conversation session below and write down the facts worth remembering about the speakers, so that questions asked much later can be answered from your notes alone.

Rules:
- One fact per list item. Each fact is one short, self-contained sentence that names the person it is about; never use pronouns such as he, she, they or I, and never write 'the user'.
- Cover personal details, events (what happened, when, where, with whom), plans, preferences, relationships, opinions, hobbies, possessions and every date, place, name or number that is mentioned.
- Turn relative time expressions (yesterday, last week, next month, two years ago) into an absolute date, month or year computed from the session date, and write that date inside the fact.
- State only what the conversation says. Do not repeat a fact and do not add commentary.
- If nothing is worth remembering, return an empty list.

Session date: {date}

Conversation:
{dialogue}

Return a JSON object with the single key "facts" whose value is the list of fact strings.
\end{Verbatim}

The fact schema is sent in the vendor's JSON mode, as for the judges (K.6):

\begin{Verbatim}[breaklines,breakanywhere,fontsize=\small]
{"type": "object", "properties": {"facts": {"type": "array", "items": {"type": "string"}}}, "required": ["facts"]}
\end{Verbatim}

A reply is valid only if it is a JSON object whose only key holds a list of strings. Each fact's whitespace is collapsed and empty facts are dropped; a fact longer than 600 characters, or more than 200 facts from one session, makes the reply invalid, and the call is resent once at the retry cap. The store is not deduplicated, updated, or pruned. It is the concatenation of every session's facts, in session order and, within a session, in extraction order, and each fact keeps its session's date. S11 built 10 stores per conversation at temperature 0.7, three control stores at temperature 0, and the raw-turn store, which holds one unit per turn in LoCoMo's rendering. S11b and S11c built five stores per extractor, with GLM-5.3-Flash or Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite as extractor.

\textbf{Retrieval.} Each store has its own BM25 index \citep{RobertsonZaragoza2009BM25}, the BM25Okapi class of rank-bm25 0.2.2, with \(k_1 = 1.5\), \(b = .75\), and \(\epsilon = .25\). The last parameter replaces each negative IDF value by \(\epsilon\) times the mean IDF, which is computed over the conversation's store. A unit is indexed as its date in brackets followed by its text. The tokenizer lowercases, splits the text into runs of Unicode letters and digits, removes a 153-word English stopword list, and applies the Snowball English stemmer of py-rust-stemmers 0.1.8. The query is the question under the same tokenizer. The 10 highest-scoring units are kept, and ties keep store order.

\textbf{Reader and judge.} The reader, GLM-5.3-Flash at temperature 0 with no system message, receives LoCoMo's official retrieval prompt \citep{Maharana2024LoCoMo}. The 10 retrieved units come first, one line per unit as the date, a colon, and the text, in rank order, followed by a blank line and LoCoMo's question prompt, which adds LoCoMo's temporal instruction for temporal questions. Only LoCoMo's categories 1--4 are asked, and an empty answer is resent once at the retry cap. The judge is GLM-5.3-Flash at temperature 0 with the Mem0-paper criterion (K.6).

\textbf{Per-replica output caps.} The proxy returned copies of byte-identical requests, and the output cap is part of the request body. Every logical call therefore carried its own small addition to the base cap, so that no two draws shared a request. In S11 the cap sent was the base plus \(d + 128(a - 1) + 256e\). Here \(d\), from 0 to 127, identifies the store, pass, and draw within one prompt family, \(a\) is the attempt, and \(e\) the resume epoch. The first extraction attempt of replica \(X_r\) thus had a cap of \(8{,}192 + (r - 1)\) tokens, from 8,192 for X1 to 8,201 for X10, and the three control stores had 8,202 to 8,204. Reader and judge calls carried offsets of their own on bases of 8,192 and 4,096. A cap that is not reached does not limit the output, so the offset only makes each request body distinct. In the test stage, 1,416 of the 1,417 session extractions succeeded at the first attempt, the largest with 7,158 output tokens, and one was resent on the 32,768-token retry base. S11b and S11c sent the base plus 1,024 plus \(k\), where \(k\), from 0 to 255, is the smallest addition not yet used by an otherwise identical request body.

\textbf{The mem0 2.2.1 adapter of the end-to-end successor study (S15c, as registered in S15).} The library \citep{Mem0Library} ran unmodified, from its upstream source at commit 94c3fe9f238f3dbf29c9ce98643bd71eb13077cd, in a separate Python 3.12 environment built offline from 33 pinned packages, among them qdrant-client 1.19.1. Its telemetry was off and it had no network access, so its language-model and embedding calls were passed to the study process.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \emph{Writes.} Each scenario and condition has its own store. Each session is one add call, in session order: its user turns are sent as user messages, the first prefixed by a line \nolinkurl{[Session date: <YYYY-MM-DD>]}, and the date is also stored as metadata. Stores of different conditions were built interleaved in a seeded order.
\item
  \emph{Extraction and update model.} GLM-5.3-Flash at temperature 0 with a cap of 16,384 tokens receives mem0's own messages verbatim in JSON-object mode, with no schema sentence appended. The library's defaults were kept: one additive extraction call per session, which also sees the top 10 existing memories and the last 10 messages, and no update or delete call. The library's extraction prompt also carries the run date. A reply must have the shape mem0 parses: a JSON object whose memory list holds objects with a text string. An empty, non-JSON, or wrongly shaped reply gets at most two recovery attempts and then fails the add, so it never becomes an empty memory. A store with a failed add is rebuilt once, and if the rebuilt store has more than one failed add, its probes are invalid.
\item
  \emph{Embedder.} BAAI/\allowbreak{}bge-small-en-v1.5 \citep{Xiao2023CPack} runs in the quantized ONNX export that fastembed 0.8.1 loads (Hugging Face repository Qdrant/\allowbreak{}bge-small-en-v1.5-onnx-Q at revision aa8f8b060edb00e03bfdd08813a2949946c8ba55), with 384 dimensions, L2 normalization, and no instruction prefix, for memories and queries alike.
\item
  \emph{Vector store.} The library's Qdrant backend keeps one local 384-dimensional collection per store, with the library's SQLite history file in the store's own directory.
\item
  \emph{Search.} Search is mem0's language-model-free semantic search with the probe as query, 10 results, mem0's default similarity threshold of .1, and no reranker. The entity-boost and keyword-score stages were off, because spaCy and mem0's BM25 encoder were not installed. Memory ids are derived by hash, and exact score ties are broken by memory hash and id over all stored points, so results do not depend on insertion or thread order.
\item
  \emph{Reader input.} The retrieved memories fill the history slot of the shared layout (K.5) as lines \nolinkurl{- [<YYYY-MM-DD>] <memory>}, ordered by source-session date with ties in mem0's rank, or \nolinkurl{(no memories)} when none is returned. The dates come from the adapter's metadata, not from mem0.
\item
  \emph{Amendment M.} A budget-exhausted empty attempt is an empty, non-JSON, unparsable, or wrongly shaped reply that stopped for length or used its full cap. After one at 16,384 tokens, the next extraction attempt runs at 32,768 tokens with a 600-second timeout, and a second such reply at 32,768 tokens ends the call and fails the add. The binding judge escalates from 12,000 to 24,000 tokens in the same way, one attempt earlier than the older remedy, and readers keep their 32,768-token cap without escalation (Appendix D.5.1).
\end{itemize}

\section{L. S20: two pipelines on STALE with and without its cues}\label{l.-s20-two-pipelines-on-stale-with-and-without-its-cues}

S20, the pipeline de-cue study of §6.4, removes STALE's typographic and clock-time cues jointly, as the de-cue test (Appendix B.3) did, for a RAG test (B1) and a mem0 test (B2) in the official STALE evaluation layout. It reuses the end-to-end successor study's retrieval, inference rules, and mem0 adapter (Appendices D.5 and K.8) but is not its successor, because it estimates a different contrast.

\subsection{L.3. Execution, deviations, and exposure}\label{l.3.-execution-deviations-and-exposure}

The run was interrupted by a machine restart and resumed from saved state under the registered resume rules. It used 31,654 live GLM-5.3-Flash attempts (cap 40,000; 0 USD), with no budget stop, at a time-averaged gate limit of 32.0 requests in flight. All 300 stores, 10,164 reads, and 3,388 judge calls ended final; 11 judge calls exhausted their budgets, 19 stores were rebuilt once, one kept a failed add, and none was INVALID.

One post-lock software repair under the registered repair rule (A4), one of two allowed repair cycles, let the validation-packet checker's study-label scan accept the gold-field names inside the required official explanation; packet bytes and sample, every other ban, and every registered text, rule, population, prompt, and frozen list were unchanged.

Before the registered gate ran, the author authorized a nonregistered provisional analysis with a different LLM rater; no number from it enters this paper. The registered rater received only the unchanged packet, and the registered closing steps then ran unchanged.

\textbf{Recomputations.} Two fresh-process recomputations from the saved outputs, by the study's own registered replay code, reproduced the results file and tables byte for byte, and the post-run verification passed. A separate recomputation with separate code, from copies of the saved reads and judgments, reproduced both estimates, intervals, and tiers.

\subsection{L.5. Limits}\label{l.5.-limits}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Reused scenarios, designed with this paper's earlier results known; GLM-5.3-Flash judges its own answers, and one LLM adjudicator, not humans, validated the judge.
\item
  Both cues are removed together, with the marks on 14.2\% of distractor sessions; turn-count, length, and content cues remain, and clock time is also legitimate temporal content.
\item
  B2 ingested user turns only, and its interval allows a benefit of up to about 9 points; missing pairs are not shown to be missing at random.
\item
  The validation packet shows answers typographically normalized, so the gate cannot detect a judge response to marks that RAW answers may copy.
\item
  No claim follows for other readers, systems, mem0 versions, benchmarks, or published scores.
\end{itemize}
